Syntactic presentations for glued toposes
and for crystalline toposes

Abstract




Gutachter:

Marc Nieper-Wißkirchen, Universität Augsburg

Thierry Coquand, University of Gothenburg

Datum der mündlichen Prüfung:

03.12.2021

Im Gedenken an meinen Papa,

der mich auf seine Schultern hob.

Abstract

We regard a geometric theory classified by a topos
as a syntactic presentation for the topos
and develop tools for finding such presentations.
Extensions (or expansions) of geometric theories,
which can not only add axioms
but also symbols and sorts,
are treated as objects in their own right,
to be able to build up complex theories from parts.
The role of equivalence extensions,
which leave the theory the same up to Morita equivalence,
is investigated.

Motivated by the question
what the big Zariski topos of a non-affine scheme classifies,
we show how to construct a syntactic presentation
for a topos if syntactic presentations for
a covering family of open subtoposes are given.
For this,
we introduce a transformation
of theory extensions
such that
when the result,
dubbed a conditional extension,
is added to a theory,
it requires part of the data a model is made of
only under some condition
given in the form of a closed geometric formula.
We also give a general definition for
systems of interdependent theory extensions,
to be able to talk about compatible syntactic presentations
not only for the open subtoposes in a given cover
but also for their finite intersections.

An important concept
for finding classified theories of toposes
in concrete situations
is that of theories of presheaf type.
We develop several techniques for extending a theory
while preserving the presheaf type property,
and give a list of examples of simple extensions
which can destroy it.

Finally,
we determine a syntactic presentation of
the big crystalline topos of a scheme.
In the case of an affine scheme,
this is accomplished by showing that
the biggest part of the classified theory
is of presheaf type
and transforming the site defining the crystalline topos
into the canonical presheaf site for this theory,
while the remaining axioms induce the Zariski topology.
Then we can apply our results on gluing classifying toposes
to obtain a classified theory even in the non-affine case.

1
Introduction

This thesis elaborates in various ways
on the theme in topos theory
that a Grothendieck topos can be viewed as

the essence of a geometric theory
.
Formally,
one says that a Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}

classifies
the geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

if the models of
\mathbb{T}
in any Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

correspond to the geometric morphisms from
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{E}
.
Since the topos
\mathcal{E}
is then uniquely determined
by the theory
\mathbb{T}
,
we can take the dual standpoint
that the theory
\mathbb{T}
is a
presentation

for the topos
\mathcal{E}
.
(All theories that we will meet
will be geometric theories,
and the term topos will always mean Grothendieck topos.)

One reason why we might seek such a presentation for a given topos
is that it can be much more concise
and, we would argue, even more intuitive
than a definition of the same topos by a site.
The prime example for this,
originating in
[
6
]
,
is the big Zariski topos
\mathcal{E}=(\Spec\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{Zar}}

of the affine scheme
\Spec\mathbb{Z}
.
A site of definition for
\mathcal{E}

is given by the opposite category of
the category of all finitely presentable rings,
equipped with a certain Grothendieck topology
called the Zariski topology,
which involves localizations
A_{a}

of a ring
A
at an element
a\in A

and the condition that some elements
generate the unit ideal
(1)=A
.
On the other hand,
one can also define
\mathcal{E}
as
the classifying topos of the theory of local rings.
Here,
a full definition consists simply in
writing down the usual algebraic operations and axioms
defining a (commutative, unitary) ring
and the extra axiom that the ring be local,
stated in the elementary form that if a sum of elements is invertible,
then one of these elements is invertible too.
Of course, to get an actual Grothendieck topos out of this,
one needs the whole machinery of classifying toposes,
but the presentation itself is quite short and very approachable.
To a certain degree,
it is even possible to judge
manipulations of the syntactic presentation correctly,
based on nothing but intuition from elementary algebra.
For example,
the classifying topos stays the same if we add to the list of axioms
a redundant one like
(xy)z=(zy)x
,
but not if we add an axiom like
x=-x
.

Another reason is simply that
a classified geometric theory for a topos
\mathcal{E}

is a description of the representable functor
\Hom(-,\mathcal{E})
,
that is,
it is a definition for
\mathcal{E}
by a universal property.
We would like to stress that
since Grothendieck toposes form a 2-category,
the representable functor
\Hom(-,\mathcal{E})

is in fact a pseudofunctor,
from the 2-category of Grothendieck toposes
to the 2-category of categories.
Such a pseudofunctor comprises
a huge amount of data,
and it is notoriously difficult to keep track of
the coherence conditions that this data must satisfy.
In contrast,
it is simple to check whether a geometric theory is well-defined,
and while it can in general contain arbitrarily big sets
(of relation and function symbols, say) as well,
the examples showing up in practice
are often more or less finitary.

It should be mentioned here that
syntactic presentations of toposes do always exist,
and there is a clear procedure
for constructing a classified geometric theory
out of a given site presentation of a topos.
But a presentation constructed in this way
will of course generally not tell us anything more about the topos
then the site itself does.
Whenever we speak of searching for syntactic presentations,
we intend to find a
concise
presentation,
or one that is interesting for some other reason.

The first of our two main goals,
which will occupy us in Sections

2

and

3
,
will be to give
a construction on the level of geometric theories
for an operation which is very natural
when viewing toposes as generalized topological spaces,
namely the operation of gluing toposes
along open subtoposes.
More precisely,
our setup will be that
\mathcal{E}
is a topos
covered by open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{E}_{o(U_{i})}
,
and syntactic presentations for the
\mathcal{E}_{i}
are given.
Then we ask
how to construct a syntactic presentation for
\mathcal{E}
,
and what additional data might be needed for this.
The appropriate gluing data will consist, unsurprisingly,
of syntactic presentations of the intersections of the
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
but not given independently of those for the
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
but rather compatible with them, or, really,
extending
them.
Here, the notion of extensions of geometric theories
will be crucial,
which will therefore be investigated first.
The formula we give for the theory classified by
\mathcal{E}

(see Theorem
3.5.4
)
will then be quite elegant,
it simply adds up all the given theory extensions,
after transforming them into theory extensions
for
“partial models”
over the respective open subtoposes.
This gluing technique
is then applied to deduce a syntactic presentation
for the big Zariski topos of a non-affine scheme
(see Theorem
3.7.6
)
from the well-known result in the affine case.

Our second objective,
in Sections

4

and

5
,
is to give syntactic presentations
for another family of toposes from algebraic geometry,
namely the crystalline toposes of schemes.
These toposes were introduced around 1970
to study crystalline cohomology,
a tool for extracting geometric information from schemes,
similar to de Rham cohomology,
but specifically adapted to schemes
over ground fields of positive characteristic.
While more and more classified theories
for other toposes from algebraic geometry
were found over the years,
a syntactic presentation of the crystalline topos
was up to now missing.
The construction of a crystalline topos depends in fact
not on a single scheme,
as for the Zariski topos,
but on two schemes with some additional structure.
This is reflected in the more involved classified theory we give
(see Theorem
5.7.4
),
but it is still very much related to the theory of local rings,
and the universal model living in the crystalline topos
consists precisely of its structure sheaf
and some additional data associated to it.
The case of affine base schemes is treated first,
and relies heavily on techniques
for recognizing theories of presheaf type
which we develop for this purpose.
It is then simply another application of the gluing theorem
to generalize to the non-affine case
(see Theorem
5.8.3
),
although some extra care is needed
in constructing an open cover
and a system of syntactic presentations for it.

2
Extensions of geometric theories

2.1
Background on geometric theories

For a full definition of geometric theories,
we refer to
[
7
, Chapter D1.1]
.
But we want to mention that
a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
can be thought of
as consisting of three
“layers”
,
first come the
sorts
,
then the
relation
and
function symbols
,
and finally the
axioms
.
The first two layers are often called the
signature

of the theory.
What is allowed in each layer
depends on the data in the previous layers:
The set of sorts of
\mathbb{T}

is just a set without any additional structure.
The relation and function symbols
have their own signatures,
which in this case just means a list of sorts,
and which we denote

R\subseteq A_{1}\times\dots\times A_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad f:A_{1}\times\dots\times A_{n}\to B\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and
B
are sorts of
\mathbb{T}
.
And the axioms are sequents of the form

\phi\;\vdash_{x_{1}\,{:}\,A_{1},\dots,x_{n}\,{:}\,A_{n}}\;\psi\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

to be read as

“
\phi
implies
\psi

in the context
x_{1}\,{:}\,A_{1},\dots,x_{n}\,{:}\,A_{n}
”
,
where the geometric formulas
\phi
and
\psi

can use the relation and function symbols of
\mathbb{T}
.
The context will sometimes be abbreviated

\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}
.

A relation symbol with the empty signature,
n=0
,
which we might denote
R\subseteq 1
,
is called a
proposition symbol
,
and we will rather use the letter
p
for it.
Similarly,
a function symbol with empty domain,
n=0
,
is called a
constant symbol
,
and instead of
f:1\to B
,
we simply denote it as
f:B
,
or rather
c:B
.
A geometric theory is
propositional

if it has no sorts,
and therefore also no function symbols
and no relation symbols except proposition symbols.

A
model

M
of a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

in a topos
\mathcal{E}

consists of objects

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M}\in\mathcal{E}

for all sorts
A
of
\mathbb{T}
,
subobjects

\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M}\subseteq\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}

for all relation symbols
R
of
\mathbb{T}

and morphisms

\llbracket f\rrbracket_{M}:\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket_{M}

for all function symbols
f
of
\mathbb{T}
,
such that the axioms of
\mathbb{T}
are fulfilled.
Given a model
M
,
we can not only
interpret

individual sorts and symbols in
M
,
but also any geometric formula
\phi

of
\mathbb{T}

in a context
x_{1}\,{:}\,A_{1},\dots,x_{n}\,{:}\,A_{n}
,
yielding a subobject

\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}\subseteq\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The requirement that an axiom

\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}}\;\psi

is fulfilled in
M

means that there is an inclusion of subobjects

\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}\leq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{M}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

There is also a notion of
morphism

between models of the same theory in the same topos,
and the resulting category of
\mathbb{T}
-models in
\mathcal{E}

will be denoted

\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Furthermore,
the requirement that the axioms of
\mathbb{T}
are geometric sequents
ensures that pulling back
the individual parts of a model
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})

along a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{E}

yields a model of
\mathbb{T}
in
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.
For a fixed model
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})

and varying
f
,
this constitutes a functor

\mathrm{Geom}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{E})\to\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and the model
M
is called a
universal model
of
\mathbb{T}

if this functor is an equivalence of categories
for all (Grothendieck) toposes
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.
The topos
\mathcal{E}
is then called a
classifying topos

for the geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
,
and we will call the pair
(\mathbb{T},M)

a
syntactic presentation
of the topos
\mathcal{E}
.

It is a theorem that every (Grothendieck) topos
classifies some geometric theory
and every geometric theory admits a classifying topos,
that is, a universal model in some topos.
The classifying topos of a theory
is also unique up to equivalence,
which justifies writing

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

for a classifying topos of a theory
\mathbb{T}
.
But it is not at all true that
the classified theory of a topos is unique.
Instead,
two theories admitting universal models in the same topos
are called
Morita equivalent
theories.

There is of course also a notion of provability
for geometric theories,
which we will not define here.
A geometric sequent which is provable in a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

is fulfilled in any model of
\mathbb{T}
in any topos,
so it could just as well be added as an axiom of
\mathbb{T}
.
Two theories
\mathbb{T}_{1}
and
\mathbb{T}_{2}

over the same signature
(same sorts and symbols)
are called
syntactically equivalent

if every axiom of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
is provable in
\mathbb{T}_{2}

and vice versa.
Note
that this is a much stronger condition on
\mathbb{T}_{1}
and
\mathbb{T}_{2}

than being Morita equivalent,
since Morita equivalent theories
can have different signatures.
We will simply write syntactic equivalence as equality,

\mathbb{T}_{1}=\mathbb{T}_{2}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The universal model of a theory
\mathbb{T}

is unique in the sense that
for any two universal models in toposes
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
,
there is an equivalence
\mathcal{E}_{1}\simeq\mathcal{E}_{2}

sending one to the other.
With respect to provability,
it has the following strong property.
A geometric sequent of a theory
\mathbb{T}

is fulfilled in the universal model of
\mathbb{T}

if and only if
it is provable in
\mathbb{T}
.

Finally,
we would like to make the point that
when manipulating geometric theories,
one has to think intuitionistically.
This is not the case when the matter is only about axioms;
it is a well-known theorem
that any geometric sequent
which is provable from the axioms of a geometric theory
using full classical first-order logic
is also provable from these axioms in geometric logic.
But if we are also interested in adding sorts and symbols,
the intuitionistic nature shows clearly.
For example,
the theory consisting of a single proposition symbol
p

has two models (up to isomorphism) in
\mathrm{Set}
,
as the interpretation of
p
can be either true or false.
The same is true for the theory with
two proposition symbols
p
and
q

and the axioms

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;p\lor q\quad\text{and}\quad p\land q\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

But these are two completely different theories,
as a model of the first theory in a topos
\mathcal{E}

is just an open subtopos of
\mathcal{E}
,
while a model of the second theory
is a decomposition of
\mathcal{E}
into two subtoposes
which are both open and closed.
And even the categories of models in
\mathrm{Set}

are not equivalent,
since proposition symbols are allowed to become true
but not to become false
under model homomorphisms.

2.2
Theory extensions as presentations of geometric morphisms

Definition 2.2.1
.

A (geometric)
extension

\mathbb{E}
of a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

consists of a set
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Sort}}
of sorts,
sets
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Rel}}
and
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Fun}}

of relation and function symbols
over the sorts
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-Sort}}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Sort}}

and a set of geometric axioms
over the sorts
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-Sort}}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Sort}}

and the symbols
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-Rel}}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Rel}}

and
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-Fun}}\sqcup{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Fun}}
.
We denote
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}
the theory obtained by
adding these sorts, symbols and axioms to
\mathbb{T}
.
The extension
\mathbb{E}
is
localic

if
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Sort}}=\varnothing
;
it is a
quotient extension

if additionally
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Rel}}=\varnothing

and
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Fun}}=\varnothing
.

If
\mathbb{E}
is an extension of
\mathbb{T}
,
we have a forgetful functor

U_{\mathbb{E}}:{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}\to{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

for every Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
.
Note that this functor is an isofibration.
Also,
after fixing classifying toposes
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]
and
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}]
,
the
\mathbb{T}
-model part of the universal
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})
-model
induces a canonical geometric morphism

\pi_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which in turn acts on generalized points
by the functors
U_{\mathbb{E}}

(up to natural isomorphism).
This is the geometric morphism

presented
by the extension
\mathbb{E}
.

The following theorem
says that every geometric morphism
can be presented in this way,
thus generalizing the result that
every Grothendieck topos classifies a geometric theory
to the relative situation
over some base topos
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

with an already chosen syntactic presentation.

Theorem 2.2.2

(
[
5
, Theorem 7.1.5]
)
.

Let a geometric morphism

p:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

to the classifying topos of a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

be given.
Then
p
is, up to isomorphism,
of the form
\pi_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

for some extension
\mathbb{E}
of
\mathbb{T}
.
If
p
is localic (respectively an embedding),
then we can take
\mathbb{E}
to be a localic extension
(respectively a quotient extension).

Proof.

See
[
5
, Theorem 7.1.5]
.
The case of an embedding,
which is not mentioned there,
is instead part of the duality
between subtoposes and quotient theories

[
5
, Theorem 3.2.5]
.
∎

Given two extensions
\mathbb{E}_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{2}
of a theory
\mathbb{T}
,
it is clear that we can also regard
\mathbb{E}_{2}

as an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}
and vice versa.
Then we have
a strict pullback diagram of categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{2})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

for any topos
\mathcal{E}
,
which is also a weak pullback,
since the the forgetful functors are isofibrations.
This means that
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}]

is the pullback topos
of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}]
and
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{2}]
over
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]
.
In particular,
the product
(as generalized spaces, not as categories)
of two classifying toposes
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}_{1}]
and
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}_{2}]

classifies the theory
\mathbb{T}_{1}+\mathbb{T}_{2}
.

In the same way in which
we prefer to have a symbol for an extension
\mathbb{E}

instead of only for the extended theory
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}=\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}
,
we will also want to regard the data
that is missing in a
\mathbb{T}
-model
compared to a
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})
-model
as an object in its own right.

Definition 2.2.3
.

A
model extension

E
of a model
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

along a theory extension
\mathbb{E}
of
\mathbb{T}

consists of interpretations for the sorts and symbols of
\mathbb{E}

that extend
M
to a model
M+E\in{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
.
A
homomorphism
of model extensions of
M

is a family of maps,
one for each sort of
\mathbb{E}
,
that constitutes a
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})
-model homomorphism
when combined with the identity homomorphism of
M
.
That is,
the category of model extensions of
M
along
\mathbb{E}

is isomorphic to the strict preimage of
M
under
U_{\mathbb{E}}
;
it will be denoted
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E},M)}

or simply
{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-mod}(M)}
.

Remark 2.2.4
.

Note that our terminology here
is somewhat in conflict with
the usage of for example
“elementary extension”

in set theory,
which means a bigger model of the same theory.

Using the notion of model extensions,
Theorem
2.2.2

can be formulated as follows.
Given any model
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})

of a geometric theory in some topos,
there is always an extension
\mathbb{E}
of
\mathbb{T}

and a model extension
E
of
M
along
\mathbb{E}

such that the model
(M+E)\in(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})

is universal.

2.3
Equivalence extensions

Definition 2.3.1
.

An
equivalence extension

is an extension
\mathbb{E}
of
\mathbb{T}

such that the forgetful functor

U_{\mathbb{E}}:{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}\to{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

is an equivalence of categories
for every Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}
.
Equivalently,
the geometric morphism
\pi_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

presented by
\mathbb{E}
is an equivalence.

If
\mathbb{E}
is an equivalence extension of a theory
\mathbb{T}
,
then it is also an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
,
for any other extension
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{T}
.
This is clear from the (weak) pullback property
of the category
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})
.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

An extension
\mathbb{E}
of a theory
\mathbb{T}

is an equivalence extension
if and only if
every model
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

in every Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}

admits exactly one model extension along
\mathbb{E}

up to isomorphism.

Proof.

The given condition means
that
U_{\mathbb{E}}:{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}\to{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

is bijective on isomorphism classes
for every topos
\mathcal{E}
,
which at first sight seems weaker than being an equivalence.
But it means that the functors

\mathrm{Geom}(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}])\to\mathrm{Geom}(\mathcal{E},\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}])

induced by the geometric morphism

\pi_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

are bijective on isomorphism classes,
in other words,

\pi_{\mathbb{E}}
is an isomorphism in the 1-category
of toposes and geometric morphisms up to isomorphism,
which is the same as an equivalence of toposes.
∎

Remark 2.3.3
.

Lemma
2.3.2

is a version of the slogan that
if we know the models of a geometric theory,
we also know the morphisms between them,
see
[
7
, below Lemma B4.2.3]
.
However, here it does not suffice to simply say that
a morphism of
\mathbb{T}
-models in
\mathcal{E}

is the same as a
\mathbb{T}
-model in the topos
\mathcal{E}^{\to}

(the arrow category of
\mathcal{E}
),
as
U_{\mathbb{E}}
can be bijective on isomorphism classes
for both
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\to}

without being an equivalence for
\mathcal{E}
.
For example,
let
\mathbb{T}=\varnothing
,
let
\mathbb{E}
be the theory of
G
-torsors for a group
G

and let
\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{Set}
.
Denote
\underline{G}

the one object groupoid associated to
G
.
Then both of the functors

\displaystyle\underline{G}\simeq{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}\to{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}\simeq\{*\}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

\displaystyle\underline{G}\simeq\underline{G}^{\to}\simeq(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set}^{\to})\to\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set}^{\to})\simeq\{*\}^{\to}\simeq\{*\}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

are bijective on isomorphism classes,
but the first is not an equivalence.

Lemma 2.3.4
.

Let
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
be a universal model,

\mathbb{E}
an extension of
\mathbb{T}

and
E
a model extension of
M
along
\mathbb{E}
.
Then
\mathbb{E}
is an equivalence extension
if and only if

M+E\in{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
is again universal.

Proof.

This is immediate from the two-out-of-three property
of equivalences of categories
in the diagram

\hbox to195.29pt{\vbox to70.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 97.64598pt\lower-35.00586pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-20.45143pt}{-21.19078pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qed

Apart from syntactic equivalences,
one way to produce an equivalence extension of a theory
\mathbb{T}

is to add new symbols
for relations or functions that were already definable in
\mathbb{T}
.
We now want to show that
all localic equivalence extensions are of this form
up to syntactic equivalence,
meaning that we have a simple syntactic characterization
of equivalence extensions among the localic extensions.

Definition 2.3.5
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a geometric theory,
let
R_{i}\subseteq\vec{A}^{i}

and
f_{j}:\vec{B}^{j}\to C^{j}

be families of new relation and function symbols
(with signatures consisting of sorts of
\mathbb{T}
)
and let
\{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}^{i}\,.\;\phi_{i}(\vec{x})\}

and
\{\vec{y}\,{:}\,\vec{B}^{j},z\,{:}\,C^{j}\,.\;\psi_{j}(\vec{y},z)\}

be formulas of
\mathbb{T}
in corresponding contexts,
where every
\psi_{j}
is provably functional.
Then the
extension by definitions
for this data
is the localic extension of
\mathbb{T}

consisting of the symbols
R_{i}
,
f_{j}

and the axioms

R_{i}(\vec{x})\;\dashv\vdash_{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}^{i}}\;\phi_{i}(\vec{x})\qquad\text{and}\qquad f_{j}(\vec{y})=z\;\dashv\vdash_{\vec{y}\,{:}\,\vec{B}^{j},z\,{:}\,C^{j}}\;\psi_{j}(\vec{y},z).

Remark 2.3.6
.

To show that a given localic extension
\mathbb{E}

of a theory
\mathbb{T}
is (syntactically equivalent to)
an extension by definitions,
one has to find formulas
\phi_{i}
,
\psi_{j}
of
\mathbb{T}
,
the latter provably functional,
for the relation and function symbols of
\mathbb{E}
,
such that the axioms in
Definition
2.3.5

are provable in
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}
,
and furthermore,
one has to check that all axioms of
\mathbb{E}

are already provable in
\mathbb{T}

if the symbols
R_{i}
,
f_{j}

are replaced by the formulas
\phi_{i}
,
\psi_{j}
.
However,
this last check can be omitted
if a model extension
E
along
\mathbb{E}

of a universal
\mathbb{T}
-model
M
is available,
since then any
\mathbb{T}
-sequent which is true in
M+E

is trivially also true in
M

and therefore provable in
\mathbb{T}
.
For the same reason,
it is then automatic that
the
\psi_{j}
are provably functional.
This will be relevant in applications of
Corollary
3.6.1
.

Proposition 2.3.7
.

A localic extension
is an equivalence extension
if and only if it is
(syntactically equivalent to)
an extension by definitions.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{E}
is an extension by definitions of
\mathbb{T}

then any model
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

admits exactly one model extension
E
along
\mathbb{E}
,
namely
\llbracket R_{i}\rrbracket_{M+E}\vcentcolon=\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket_{M}

and
\llbracket f_{i}\rrbracket_{M+E}
is the morphism with graph

\llbracket\psi_{j}\rrbracket_{M}
.
So
\mathbb{E}
is an equivalence extension by Lemma

2.3.2
.

On the other hand,
if
\mathbb{E}
is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}
,
then consider a universal model
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}])}

and the unique (up to isomorphism) extension
E
of
M
along
\mathbb{E}
.
By Lemma
2.3.4
,

M+E
is a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}
.
Now, for every relation symbol
R\subseteq\vec{A}

introduced in
\mathbb{E}
,

\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M+E}
is a subobject of

\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}

and since
M
is universal,

[
5
, Theorem 6.1.3]
tells us that
there is a formula
\phi_{R}
of
\mathbb{T}

with
\llbracket\phi_{R}\rrbracket_{M}=\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M+E}
.
Similarly, for every new function symbol
f

we find a provably functional formula
\psi_{f}
of
\mathbb{T}

such that
\llbracket\psi_{f}\rrbracket_{M}

is the graph of
\llbracket f\rrbracket_{M+E}
.
That is, the axioms for defining
R
by
\phi_{R}
and
f
by
\psi_{f}

are fulfilled for
M+E

and therefore provable in
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}
.
Thus,
we have
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}

where
\mathbb{E}_{1}
is an extension by definitions
and
\mathbb{E}_{2}
is a quotient.
But since both
\mathbb{E}_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}
are equivalence extensions,

\mathbb{E}_{2}
must be one too,
and we have (up to syntactic equivalence)
\mathbb{E}_{2}=\varnothing

and
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{1}
.
∎

The following lemma is about
“reverting”

an extension
\mathbb{E}
of
\mathbb{T}
by applying another extension
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
,
subject to extensibility of the universal
\mathbb{T}
-model along
\mathbb{E}
.

Lemma 2.3.8
.

Let
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
be a universal model
and
E
an extension of
M
along some theory extension
\mathbb{E}
.
Then there is a localic extension
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}

and a model extension
E^{\prime}
of
M+E
along
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}

such that
(M+E+E^{\prime})\in{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}+\mathbb{E}^{\prime})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

is again universal.
(In particular,
\mathbb{E}+\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
is an equivalence extension.)
If
\mathbb{E}
is localic,

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
can be chosen as a quotient;
if
\mathbb{E}
is a quotient,

\mathbb{E}^{\prime}=\varnothing
fits the bill.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}+\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof.

Fix a classifying topos
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}}
for
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}
.
We have the forgetful geometric morphism
u:\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}}\to\mathcal{E}
,
and
M+E\in{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
corresponds to
a section (up to isomorphism)
s:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}}
of
u
.
As such,
s
is localic (since
u\circ s
is).
By Theorem
2.2.2
,
it is therefore presented by some localic extension
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}

of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}
,
that is,
we have a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}+\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{E}

extending
M+E
, as required.

The two special cases follow since
a section of a localic geometric morphism is an embedding
and a section of an embedding is an equivalence,
but we can also show them more directly.
If
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{Q}
is a quotient,
then the existence of
E
just means that
the new axioms are fulfilled in
M
.
But this means that they were already provable in
\mathbb{T}

and
M=M+E
is indeed a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
.
For
\mathbb{E}
localic,
we have to deal with the new relation symbols
R

and function symbols
f
.
From
[
5
, Theorem 6.1.3]

we know that

\llbracket R(\vec{x})\rrbracket_{M+E}=\llbracket\phi_{R}(\vec{x})\rrbracket_{M}

and

\llbracket f(\vec{x})=y\rrbracket_{M+E}=\llbracket\phi_{f}(\vec{x},y)\rrbracket_{M}

for some formulas
\phi_{R}
,
\phi_{f}
,
where
\phi_{f}
is provably functional.
So taking for
\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
the axioms

R(\vec{x})\;\dashv\vdash_{\vec{x}}\;\phi_{R}(\vec{x})

and

f(\vec{x})=y\;\dashv\vdash_{\vec{x},y}\;\phi_{f}(\vec{x},y)
,
we obtain as
\mathbb{E}+\mathbb{E}^{\prime}
an extension by definitions.
∎

As a corollary,
we find that an equivalence between two theories,
and more generally an equivalence between
two extensions of some base theory
(meaning that they present the same geometric morphism),
can always be captured in a syntactic way.

Corollary 2.3.9
.

Let
\mathbb{E}_{1}
,
\mathbb{E}_{2}
be two equivalent extensions of a theory
\mathbb{T}
,
that is,
there exists a model
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

with extensions to universal models

M+E_{1}
and
M+E_{2}
of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}
respectively
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{2}

in the same topos
\mathcal{E}
.
Then there is a localic extension
\mathbb{E}_{1,2}

of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}

such that both
\mathbb{E}_{2}+\mathbb{E}_{1,2}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{1,2}

are equivalence extensions
(of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}
respectively
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{2}
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}+\mathbb{E}_{1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

and there is a model extension
E_{1,2}
along
\mathbb{E}_{1,2}

such that
M_{0}+E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{1,2}
is universal.
If
\mathbb{E}_{1}
or
\mathbb{E}_{2}
is localic,
then
\mathbb{E}_{1,2}=\mathbb{Q}_{1,2}
can be chosen as
a quotient extension.

Proof.

Regard
\mathbb{E}_{2}
as an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}
for the moment,
then we have an extension
E_{2}
of the universal model
M+E_{1}
,
so by Lemma
2.3.8

there exists a localic extension
\mathbb{E}_{1,2}

of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}

together with a model extension
E_{1,2}

of
M+E_{1}+E_{2}

such that
M+E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{1,2}
is universal.
Then, by Lemma
2.3.4
,
both
\mathbb{E}_{2}+\mathbb{E}_{1,2}
and
\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{1,2}

are equivalence extensions, as required.
If
\mathbb{E}_{1}
is localic,
we get a quotient extension
\mathbb{E}_{1,2}=\mathbb{Q}_{1,2}
from
Lemma
2.3.8
,
and if
\mathbb{E}_{2}
is localic, we swap the two.
∎

A completely different proof of
Corollary
2.3.9

in the absolute case
\mathbb{T}=\varnothing

can be found in
[
10
, Theorem 5.1]
.

Definition 2.3.10
.

We call an extension
\mathbb{E}_{1,2}
as in
Corollary
2.3.9

a
diagonal extension
for
\mathbb{E}_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{2}

over
\mathbb{T}
,
because it presents the diagonal geometric morphism
of the pullback topos

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}]=\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}]\times_{\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]}\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{2}]=\mathcal{E}\times_{\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]}\mathcal{E}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Diagonal extensions are not unique.
For example,
if
\mathbb{T}_{1}=\langle p_{1}\rangle+\langle p_{2}\rangle

and
\mathbb{T}_{2}=\langle p_{3}\rangle+\langle p_{4}\rangle

both consist of two proposition symbols,
there are clearly two different diagonal quotient extensions
for
\mathbb{T}_{1}
and
\mathbb{T}_{2}
over
\varnothing
.
Corollary
2.3.9

produces a diagonal extension
in accordance with the two universal models

M_{0}+E_{1}
and
M_{0}+E_{2}
living in the same topos.

Another easy consequence of
Lemma
2.3.8

is that any object of the classifying topos of a theory
can be introduced into the theory as a new sort
by means of an equivalence extension.

Corollary 2.3.11
.

Let
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
be a universal model
and let
X\in\mathcal{E}
be any object.
Then there is an equivalence extension
\mathbb{E}
of
\mathbb{T}

containing exactly one new sort
A
,
such that the unique (up to unique isomorphism)
model extension
E
of
M
along
\mathbb{E}

interprets
A
as
X
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
2.3.8

to the extension
\mathbb{E}_{1}
adding nothing but the sort
A

(and the model extension
E_{1}

given by
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{E_{1}}\vcentcolon=X
)
to obtain a localic extension
\mathbb{E}_{2}
,
and set
\mathbb{E}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}
.
∎

2.4
Some examples of equivalence extensions

Giving examples of localic equivalence extensions
does not seem necessary after
Proposition
2.3.7
,
but here are some non-localic equivalence extensions
that will be of use later.

Example 2.4.1
.

Let
A
be a sort of a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

and let
\phi(x)
be a geometric formula
in the context
x\,{:}\,A
.
Then there is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}

consisting of
a sort
S_{\phi}
,
a function symbol
\iota:S_{\phi}\to A

and the axioms

\iota(x)=\iota(y)\;\vdash_{x,y\,{:}\,S_{\phi}}\;x=y\qquad\text{and}\qquad\exists x{\,:\,}S_{\phi}.\;\iota(x)=y\;\dashv\vdash_{y\,{:}\,A}\;\phi(y)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

the first of which forces the interpretation of
\iota
in a model
to be a monomorphism,
while the second ensures that

\llbracket S_{\phi}\rrbracket\hookrightarrow\llbracket A\rrbracket

is the same subobject as

\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\hookrightarrow\llbracket A\rrbracket
.

Example 2.4.2
.

Let
A
be a sort of a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

and let
x\sim x^{\prime}
be a geometric formula
in the context
x,x^{\prime}\,{:}\,A

such that the usual axioms of an equivalence relation
(which are Horn sequents)
are provable for
\sim
.
Then there is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}

consisting of a sort
A/{\sim}
,
a function symbol
\pi:A\to A/{\sim}

and the axioms

\top\;\vdash_{y\,{:}\,A/{\sim}}\;\exists x{\,:\,}A.\;\pi(x)=y\qquad\text{and}\qquad\pi(x)=\pi(x^{\prime})\;\dashv\vdash_{x,x^{\prime}\,{:}\,A}\;x\sim x^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

These force the interpretation

\llbracket A\rrbracket\to\llbracket A/{\sim}\rrbracket

of
\pi

to be an epimorphism
with kernel pair

\llbracket\sim\rrbracket\rightrightarrows\llbracket A\rrbracket
.

Remark 2.4.3
.

An intermediate notion between
syntactic equivalence and Morita equivalence
is the notion of (geometric)
bi-interpretability
,
see
[
5
, Definition 2.1.13]
.
Two geometric theories are bi-interpretable
if and only if their syntactic sites are equivalent categories.
So while this notion is still syntactic in nature,
it does not assume any previously given relation
between the signatures of the two theories.
For example, a sort
A
of the first theory,
which is represented in the syntactic site
by the object
\{x\,{:}\,A.\;\top\}
,
can correspond to any formula in context

\{\vec{y}\,{:}\,\vec{B}.\;\phi(\vec{y})\}

of the second theory.

The equivalence extension in
Example
2.4.1

induces a bi-interpretation,
interpreting the new sort
S_{\phi}

by the formula in context

\{x\,{:}\,A.\;\phi(x)\}

of
\mathbb{T}
.
But already Example
2.4.2

shows that bi-interpretability is a stronger condition
than Morita equivalence,
since the formula in context
\{x\,{:}\,A/{\sim}.\;\top\}

can not be expressed as any formula in context of
\mathbb{T}
.

Example 2.4.4
.

Given a set
A
,
define a theory
\underline{A}

(named after its only sort)
consisting of
a sort
\underline{A}
,
constant symbols
c_{a}:\underline{A}

for every element
a\in A

and the axioms

c_{a}=c_{a^{\prime}}\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot\quad\text{for $a\neq a^{\prime}\in A$},\qquad\qquad\top\;\vdash_{x:\underline{A}}\;\bigvee_{a\in A}(x=c_{a})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

One can check that the unique model (up to unique isomorphism)
in any Grothendieck topos is the constant sheaf
associated to the set
A
,
which is also denoted
\underline{A}
.
The theory
\underline{A}

is therefore Morita-equivalent to the empty theory,
and adding
\underline{A}
to any given theory
is an equivalence extension.
In other words,
we can always
import
a set
A

into our theory
without changing it up to Morita-equivalence.

If we have a function like
f:A\to B

or a relation like
R\subseteq A
,
we can import it together with the respective sets.
Namely,
after adding a function symbol

\underline{f}:\underline{A}\to\underline{B}

or a relation symbol

\underline{R}\subseteq\underline{A}
,
the axioms

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\underline{f}(c_{a})=c_{f(a)}\quad\text{for $a\in A$}

respectively

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\underline{R}(c_{a})\quad\text{for $a\in R$},\qquad\qquad\underline{R}(c_{a})\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot\quad\text{for $a\in A\setminus R$}

produce an extension by definitions
in presence of the axioms of
\underline{A}
.

Another perspective on this is
that if we have any model of a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
in
\mathrm{Set}
,
then by Lemma
2.3.8

there is a localic extension to a universal model in
\mathrm{Set}
,
which then yields,
by pulling back along the unique geometric morphism to
\mathrm{Set}
,
the unique model of the extended theory in any topos.

3
Gluing classifying toposes

3.1
Introduction

In this section
we explicitly construct a geometric theory
classified by a given topos
from a cover by open subtoposes
with known classified theories.

We already saw in
Section
2

that taking the product of two toposes,
regarded as generalized spaces,
corresponds to the simplest possible operation
involving two unrelated theories,
namely forming the sum
\mathbb{T}_{1}+\mathbb{T}_{2}
.
The present topic is a generalization of
the dual question
what the coproduct of two toposes classifies.
This is much less obvious,
since a geometric morphism

\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}_{1}]\amalg\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}_{2}]

will neither define a
\mathbb{T}_{1}
-model
nor a
\mathbb{T}_{2}
-model in
\mathcal{E}
.
Instead,
it first of all defines a decomposition of
\mathcal{E}

into two clopen subtoposes,
and then a
\mathbb{T}_{1}
-model in one and a
\mathbb{T}_{2}
-model in the other
of these subtoposes.

Another good example is
the big Zariski topos of
the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}

over a ring
K
.
The big Zariski topos
X_{\mathrm{Zar}}
of an affine scheme
X=\Spec R

classifies the geometric theory of local
R
-algebras.
Now,

\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}
is not affine,
but it can be covered by two copies of the affine line,

\Spec K[X]
and
\Spec K[Y]
,
such that the intersection is
\Spec K[X,Y]/(XY-1)
.
We will see
that this induces an open cover
of the big Zariski topos
(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K})_{\mathrm{Zar}}

by open subtoposes
(\Spec K[X])_{\mathrm{Zar}}
and
(\Spec K[Y])_{\mathrm{Zar}}
,
which both classify local
K
-algebras with one distinguished element.
This suggests that

(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K})_{\mathrm{Zar}}
classifies
local
K
-algebras which are equipped with an element
X

or
with an element
Y
,
where the two possibilities are not mutually exclusive
but rather,
their intersection is described by the condition
XY=1
.
A formulation of this idea
as a geometric theory,
which indeed presents
(\mathbb{P}^{1})_{\mathrm{Zar}}
,
is given in Proposition
3.7.5
.

3.2
Conditional extensions

If a topos
\mathcal{E}
has an open subtopos
\mathcal{E}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{E}

which classifies some theory
\mathbb{T}_{1}
,
then a geometric morphism
f:\tilde{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathcal{E}

does not give us a model of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
,
so to find a classified theory for
\mathcal{E}
,
we should not look among extensions of
\mathbb{T}_{1}
.
However,
f
does give us a model of
\mathbb{T}_{1}

in some open subtopos of
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
,
namely in the preimage of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
.
We now show how to capture syntactically
the requirement of

“a model in some open subtopos”
.

To avoid technical complications,
we exclude function symbols from our discussion.
Recall
that a function symbol
f:\vec{A}\to B

can be considered an abbreviation for a relation symbol

R\subseteq\vec{A}\times B

together with axioms ensuring that
R
is provably functional,
as long as we don’t mind replacing axioms
with nested function applications
like
g(f(x))=z

by versions with auxiliary variables
like
\exists y.\,(f(x)=y)\land(g(y)=z)
.
That is,
function symbols can be considered
“syntactic sugar”
.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a geometric theory,

\phi
a closed formula of
\mathbb{T}

and
\mathbb{E}
an extension
(without function symbols)
of
\mathbb{T}+\phi
.
We define the
conditional extension

\mathbb{E}/\phi
of
\mathbb{T}

to consist of the following.

•

For every sort
S\in{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-Sort}}
of
\mathbb{E}
,
a sort
S

and the axiom
\top\;\vdash_{x:S}\;\phi
.

•

For every relation symbol

R\subseteq S_{1}\times\dots\times S_{n}
of
\mathbb{E}
,
a relation symbol
R\subseteq S_{1}\times\dots\times S_{n}

and the axiom
R(\vec{x})\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:\vec{S}}\;\phi
.

•

For every axiom
\psi_{1}\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi_{2}
of
\mathbb{E}
,
the axiom

\psi_{1}\land\phi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi_{2}
.

If the theory
\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}

asks us to specify a model of
\mathbb{T}
that satisfies
\phi

and furthermore a model extension along
\mathbb{E}
,
then the theory
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi

instead asks us to specify a model of
\mathbb{T}

and a model extension along
\mathbb{E}

in case
our
\mathbb{T}
-model happens to satisfy
\phi

— or, more geometrically speaking,

wherever
it satisfies
\phi
.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

The assignment
\mathbb{E}\mapsto\mathbb{E}/\phi

is well-defined with respect to syntactic equivalence.
Furthermore,
we have the following syntactic equivalences.

(i)

\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi+\phi=\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}

(ii)

\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}/\phi+\mathbb{E}_{2}/\phi=\mathbb{T}+(\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2})/\phi

Proof.

To show that the construction is well-defined,
let
\psi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\chi
be a geometric sequent
which is provable in
\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}
.
If it was added as an axiom to
\mathbb{E}
,
the axiom
\psi\land\phi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\chi

would be added to
\mathbb{E}/\phi
,
so we must show that this sequent
is already provable in
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi
.
This is equivalent to showing that

\psi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\chi

is provable in
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi+\phi
,
so we are done if (i) is true.
For (i),
we observe that the additional axioms of
\mathbb{E}/\phi

are indeed trivial
and
\psi_{1}\land\phi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi_{2}

is indeed equivalent to
\psi_{1}\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi_{2}

if our theory already contains
\phi
as an axiom.
For (ii),
Definition
3.2.1

produces the exact same axiomatization for both sides.
∎

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Let
\mathbb{E}
be an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\phi
,

M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

and
\mathcal{F}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{E}_{o(\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M})}

the open subtopos of
\mathcal{E}

corresponding to the subterminal object

\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}\subseteq 1_{\mathcal{E}}
.
Then there is an equivalence of categories

{\mathbb{E}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{F},M|_{\mathcal{F}})}\simeq{(\mathbb{E}/\phi)\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E},M)}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Proof.

The open inclusion geometric morphism
i:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{E}

admits a further (full and faithful) left adjoint
i_{!}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{E}

with essential image
\mathcal{E}/U
,
where
U\vcentcolon=\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}
.
The composition
i_{!}\circ i^{*}
is
X\mapsto X\times U
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}/U\simeq\mathcal{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{!}}

\vdash

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}

For a sort
S
of
\mathbb{E}
,
the axiom
\top\;\vdash_{x:S}\;\phi
of
\mathbb{E}/\phi

means precisely that
for every
E\in(\mathbb{E}/\phi)\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(M)
,
the object
\llbracket S\rrbracket_{M+E}
must lie in
the full subcategory
\mathcal{E}/U
,
so we have established the equivalence
for the case that
\mathbb{E}
contains only sorts.
The quasi-inverse is of course
the restriction functor
i^{*}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}
,
applied to the interpretation of every sort.
For a relation symbol
R\subseteq\vec{S}
of
\mathbb{E}

(where the list
\vec{S}
may contain sorts
from both
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{E}
),
the axiom
R(\vec{x})\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:\vec{S}}\;\phi

similarly means

\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M+E}\subseteq\llbracket\vec{S}\rrbracket_{M+E}\times U
,
so that
\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M+E}

corresponds to a subobject of

i^{*}(\llbracket\vec{S}\rrbracket_{M+E})=\llbracket\vec{S}\rrbracket_{M|_{\mathcal{F}}+E|_{\mathcal{F}}}
.
Finally, the modified axiom

\psi_{1}\land\phi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi_{2}

(or equivalently

\psi_{1}\land\phi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi_{2}\land\phi
)
is satisfied by
M+E

if and only if

\llbracket\psi_{1}\rrbracket\times U\subseteq\llbracket\psi_{2}\rrbracket\times U
,
that is, if and only if

\psi_{1}\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi_{2}

is satisfied by
M|_{\mathcal{F}}+E|_{\mathcal{F}}
.
∎

For a model
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})

and an extension

E\in\mathbb{E}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(M|_{\mathcal{E}_{o(\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M})}})
,
the corresponding extension of
M
along
\mathbb{E}/\phi

will be denoted
E/\phi
.
That means that we have

(M+E/\phi)|_{\mathcal{E}_{o(\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M})}}\cong M|_{\mathcal{E}_{o(\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M})}}+E

as models of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi+\phi=\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}
.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

An extension
\mathbb{E}
of
\mathbb{T}+\phi

is an equivalence extension if and only if

\mathbb{E}/\phi
is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{E}/\phi
is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}
,
then it is also an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}+\phi
.
But by
Lemma
3.2.2
(i),

\mathbb{E}/\phi
is syntactically equivalent to
\mathbb{E}

as an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\phi
.

Conversely,
if
\mathbb{E}
is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}+\phi
,
then by
Lemma
2.3.2
,
and
Proposition
3.2.3

every
\mathbb{T}
-model admits a unique (up to isomorphism)
model extension along
\mathbb{E}/\phi
,
so we are done by the other direction of
Lemma
2.3.2
.
∎

3.3
Systems of theory extensions

Given two equivalence extensions
\mathbb{E}_{1},\mathbb{E}_{2}
of a theory
\mathbb{T}
,
their sum
\mathbb{E}_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}
is again
an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}
.
The same is true if
\mathbb{E}_{2}
is not an extension of
\mathbb{T}

but of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}
,
that is, if
\mathbb{E}_{2}
depends on
\mathbb{E}_{1}
.
To formulate such statements in greater generality,
we first need to clarify
in which ways an extension can be built up
from smaller extensions,
possibly with dependencies among them.

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a geometric theory.
A
system of extensions
over
\mathbb{T}

is a family
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
,
indexed by some partially ordered set
I
,
where each
\mathbb{E}_{i}
consists of
a set of sorts,
sets of relation and function symbols
— whose signatures may contain sorts
from
\mathbb{T}
and all
\mathbb{E}_{j}
with
j\leq i
,
treated disjointly —
and a set of (geometric) axioms
— which may again use all sorts and symbols
from
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{E}_{j}
with
j\leq i
,
treated disjointly.
Taking the disjoint union of the sorts, symbols and axioms
of all
\mathbb{E}_{i}

thus yields an extension of
\mathbb{T}
,
which we denote
\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}
.

For a subset
J\subseteq I

of the partially ordered set
I
,
we use the notation

J{\downarrow}\vcentcolon=\{i\in I\mid\exists j\in J:i\leq j\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad J{\downarrow}{\downarrow}\vcentcolon=J{\downarrow}\setminus J\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Given an initial segment
J\subseteq I

(that is,
J{\downarrow}=J
),
we can of course restrict a system
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
to
J
,
obtaining a system
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in J}
over the same base theory
\mathbb{T}
.
In particular, we see that for every
i\in I
,

\mathbb{E}_{i}
is an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}
.
More generally,
the index set
I
can be restricted
to any
inward closed
subset
J\subseteq I

(if
i\leq j\leq k
and
i,k\in J
then
j\in J
),
resulting in a system
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in J}

over
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{i\in J{\downarrow}{\downarrow}}\mathbb{E}_{i}
instead of
\mathbb{T}
.

We can also push a system
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
over
\mathbb{T}
forward
along an order-preserving map
f:I\to J

to another partially ordered set
J

by setting
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{j}\vcentcolon=\sum_{i\in f^{-1}(j)}\mathbb{E}_{i}
.
Note that
f^{-1}(j)
is inward closed
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{j}
is an extension of

\mathbb{T}+\sum_{i\in f^{-1}(j){\downarrow}{\downarrow}}\mathbb{E}_{i}

and therefore also of

\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j^{\prime}<j}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{j^{\prime}}
.

Definition 3.3.2
.

Let
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
be a system of extensions
over a theory
\mathbb{T}
.
A
system of model extensions

(E_{i})_{i\in I}

for
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}

in a Grothendieck topos
\mathcal{E}

over some
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

is just a model extension
\sum_{i\in I}E_{i}

of
M
along
\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}
,
regarded as a family of model extensions
E_{i}

of
M+\sum_{j<i}E_{j}
along
\mathbb{E}_{i}
.

For a family of subtoposes
(\mathcal{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{E}

with
\mathcal{E}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{j}
for
j<i
,
a system of model extensions
in the
\mathcal{E}_{i}

is similarly a family
(E_{i})_{i\in I}
,
where each
E_{i}
is an extension
of
M|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}+\sum_{j<i}E_{j}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}

along
\mathbb{E}_{i}
.
If
M|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}+\sum_{j\leq i}E_{j}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}

is a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j\leq i}\mathbb{E}_{j}

for every
i\in I
,
then we call
(\mathbb{E}_{i},E_{i})_{i\in I}

a
system of presentations

of the
\mathcal{E}_{i}
over (
\mathbb{T}
and)
M
.

The following lemma
is our general formulation of how equivalence extensions
can be built up from smaller equivalence extensions.
We will also use it
(in Corollary
3.3.5

below)
to clarify the role of systems of presentations
which all present the same topos.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
be a system over
\mathbb{T}

where the partial order
I
is well-founded.
Then the assertions

(i)

For every
i\in I
,

\mathbb{E}_{i}
is an equivalence extension
of
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}
.

(ii)

For every
i\in I
,

\sum_{j\leq i}\mathbb{E}_{j}
is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}
.

are equivalent and they imply:

(iii)

\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}
is an equivalence extension of
\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

(i)\Rightarrow(iii)
.
We show this implication first,
because it will be used for the others.
To see that the functor

U_{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}}:{(\mathbb{T}+\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}\to{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

is an equivalence,
start by taking two models
M,M^{\prime}

of
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}
in
\mathcal{E}

and homomorphisms
f,g:M\to M^{\prime}

with
U_{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}}(f)=U_{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}}(g)
.
Then we can show
f=g

by well-founded induction over
i\in I

using the faithfulness of each

U_{\mathbb{E}_{i}}:{(\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j\leq i}\mathbb{E}_{j})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}\to{(\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}\text{.}

Given instead only

f_{0}:U_{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}}(M)\to U_{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}}(M^{\prime})
,
we use the fullness of the
U_{\mathbb{E}_{i}}

to construct
f:M\to M^{\prime}

with
U_{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}}(f)=f_{0}

by well-founded recursion.
Finally,
let
M_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

and notice that the
U_{\mathbb{E}_{i}}
are not only essentially surjective
but strictly surjective on objects (since they are isofibrations).
This means we can again use well-founded recursion
to construct a model extension
\sum_{i\in I}E_{i}
of
M_{0}

and therefore a (strict) preimage
M_{0}+\sum_{i\in I}E_{i}

of
M_{0}
under
U_{\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}}
.

(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
.
Fix
i\in I
.
Then this follows immediately
by applying
(i)\Rightarrow(iii)

to the restricted system
(\mathbb{E}_{j})_{j\leq i}
.

(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
.
By well-founded induction,
let
i\in I

with
\mathbb{E}_{j}
an equivalence extension for all
j<i
.
Then by
(i)\Rightarrow(iii)

applied to
(\mathbb{E}_{j})_{j<i}
,

\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}
is an equivalence extension.
But since
\sum_{j\leq i}\mathbb{E}_{j}=\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}+\mathbb{E}_{i}

is an equivalence extension by assumption,

\mathbb{E}_{i}
must be one too.
∎

Remark 3.3.4
.

The assumption that
I
is well-founded
is necessary in Lemma
3.3.3
.
Indeed, we can take
\mathbb{T}
to be the empty theory
and consider the system of theory extensions

(\mathbb{E}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}

where every
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is the quotient extension
consisting of the contradictory axiom
\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot
.
Then none of the
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{m\leq n}\mathbb{E}_{m}

is equivalent to
\mathbb{T}
,
and neither is
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{n}
,
but every
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is an equivalence extension of

\mathbb{T}+\sum_{m<n}\mathbb{E}_{m}
.
If we insist on treating syntactically equivalent theories as equal,
we can even say that every
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is the
empty
extension
of its respective base theory.
In this sense,
a system of theory extensions
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}

is not fully determined by the individual extensions
\mathbb{E}_{i}
,
for
I
not well-founded.
But we would rather argue that
the notion of knowing an extension
without knowing its base theory
is ill-defined.
(In the language of type theory,
the type of extensions is inherently a dependent type.)

Corollary 3.3.5
.

Let
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
be universal.
Then a system of extensions
(\mathbb{E}_{i},E_{i})_{i\in I}

over
(\mathbb{T},M)
in
\mathcal{E}

with
I
well-founded
is a system of presentations of
\mathcal{E}

if and only if
all
\mathbb{E}_{i}
are equivalence extensions.
And in this case,

M+\sum_{i\in I}E_{i}\in{(\mathbb{T}+\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

is also universal.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.3.4
,
the universality of the models
M+\sum_{j\leq i}E_{j}

for all
i\in I

is just condition
(ii)
of Lemma

3.3.3

and the universality of
M+\sum_{i\in I}E_{i}

is condition
(iii)
of Lemma

3.3.3
.
∎

3.4
Systems of conditional extensions

We now briefly describe how to apply
the construction of conditional extensions
to systems of theory extensions.
Given a system
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
over
\mathbb{T}

and a family of closed geometric formulas
\phi_{i}
of
\mathbb{T}
,
we can of course blindly apply the rules of
Definition
3.2.1

to every
\mathbb{E}_{i}
,
and we do get a new system of extensions

(\mathbb{E}_{i}/\phi_{i})_{i\in I}

over
\mathbb{T}
,
simply because the signature of
\mathbb{E}_{i}/\phi_{i}

is the same as that of
\mathbb{E}_{i}
.
But then we can not say that
the extension
\mathbb{E}_{i}/\phi_{i}

of
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}/\phi_{j}

is an honest conditional extension,
since there is no sensible way
to regard
\mathbb{E}_{i}
as an extension of

\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}/\phi_{j}+\phi_{i}
.
For example,
fixing some
\mathbb{T}
,
\mathbb{E}_{1}
and
\phi_{1}

and using
\phi_{2}=\top
,
the operation of mapping an extension
\mathbb{E}_{2}

of
\mathbb{T}+\phi_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{1}

to
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}_{1}/\phi_{1}+\mathbb{E}_{2}

is not well-defined with respect to syntactic equivalence.

To remedy this,
we impose the condition that

\phi_{i}\;\vdash_{[]}\;\phi_{j}\qquad\text{for $j\leq i$}

is provable in
\mathbb{T}
.
Then we can regard each
\mathbb{E}_{i}
as an extension of

\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}/\phi_{j}+\phi_{i}=\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j<i}\mathbb{E}_{j}+\phi_{i}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and obtain the system
(\mathbb{E}_{i}/\phi_{i})_{i\in I}

of conditional extensions.

In Definition
3.2.1
,
we treated
\mathbb{E}
as an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\phi
,
not of
\mathbb{T}
,
which is justified in view of
Lemma
3.2.4
.
This refinement is dropped here
in order to be able to use
our definition of a system of theory extensions
without modification.
In our application
(see Theorem
3.5.4
below),
the extensions
\mathbb{E}_{i}
are such that

\phi_{i}
is provable from
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{j\leq i}\mathbb{E}_{i}
.

We also want to be able to construct model extensions,
to be denoted as

M+\sum_{i\in I}E_{i}/\phi_{i}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

for systems of conditional extensions,
as we did in
Proposition
3.2.3

for a single conditional extension.
Let
(E_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of model extensions
for
(\mathbb{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
,
over some
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})
,
in the subtoposes

\mathcal{E}_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{E}_{o(\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket_{M})}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

This makes sense because
our requirement on the
\phi_{i}

ensures
\mathcal{E}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{j}
for
j\leq i
.
Let
\iota_{i}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to\mathcal{E}

be the open embeddings,
and also
\iota_{i}^{j}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to\mathcal{E}_{j}

for
j\leq i
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{i}^{j}}

Then,
like in Proposition
3.2.3
,
applying
(\iota_{i})_{!}
to the interpretations
of the sorts of
\mathbb{E}_{i}
in
E_{i}

produces objects in
\mathcal{E}/\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket_{M}
,
as required for
a model extension of
M
along
\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{E}_{i}/\phi_{i}
.
The signatures of the relation symbols of
\mathbb{E}_{i}

can contain sorts
S
from
\mathbb{E}_{i}
,
sorts
S^{\prime}
from various
\mathbb{E}_{j}
with
j\leq i

and sorts
S^{\prime\prime}
from
\mathbb{T}
,
so the interpretation of such a symbol is a subobject
of an object like

\llbracket S\rrbracket\times(\iota_{i}^{j})^{*}\llbracket S^{\prime}\rrbracket\times(\iota_{i})^{*}\llbracket S^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\;\in\;\mathcal{E}_{i}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Applying
(\iota_{i})_{!}

yields precisely a subobject of

(\iota_{i})_{!}\llbracket S\rrbracket\times(\iota_{j})_{!}\llbracket S^{\prime}\rrbracket\times\llbracket S^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\times\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket
,
since

(\iota_{i})_{!}(\iota_{i}^{j})^{*}\llbracket S^{\prime}\rrbracket=(\iota_{i})_{!}(\iota_{i})^{*}(\iota_{j})_{!}\llbracket S^{\prime}\rrbracket=(\iota_{j})_{!}\llbracket S^{\prime}\rrbracket\times\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Also,
the modified axioms of
\mathbb{E}_{i}
in
\mathbb{E}_{i}/\phi_{i}

hold for the structure in
\mathcal{E}
defined this way
because they can be tested after pulling back to
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
as before.
In summary,
we have constructed a model extension

\sum_{i\in I}E_{i}/\phi_{i}
of
M

(in the topos
\mathcal{E}
),
such that for every
i
,
the extension
E_{i}/\phi_{i}

is obtained from the model extension
E_{i}

(in the topos
\mathcal{E}_{i}
)
as in Proposition
3.2.3
,
in accordance with our previous use of the notation
E/\phi
.

3.5
Syntactic presentation of a glued topos

We need a lemma about
testing the property of a geometric morphism to be an equivalence
on an open cover of the target topos.
Or rather,
we need its reformulation in terms of models of theories
(Corollary
3.5.3
below),
saying that the property of a model to be universal
can be tested on an
“open cover of the theory”
.

Lemma 3.5.1
.

Let
f:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F}

be a geometric morphism
and let a cover
\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}

by open subtoposes
\mathcal{F}_{i}=\mathcal{F}_{o(U_{i})}
be given
such that
the induced geometric morphisms
f_{i}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
where
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{E}_{o(f^{*}U_{i})}
,
are all equivalences.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Then
f
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The fact that the
\mathcal{F}_{i}
cover
\mathcal{F}
means

1_{\mathcal{F}}=\bigvee_{i\in I}U_{i}

and, equivalently,
the sheafification functors
a_{U_{i}}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{i}

are jointly conservative.
Setting
V_{i}\vcentcolon=f^{*}U_{i}

we similarly have
1_{\mathcal{E}}=\bigvee_{i\in I}V_{i}

since
f^{*}
preserves colimits.
We will show that the unit
\eta_{f}
and counit
\varepsilon_{f}

of the adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}

are isomorphisms
by showing that
a_{U_{i}}\eta_{f}
and
a_{V_{i}}\varepsilon_{f}

are isomorphisms for all
i\in I
.
More precisely,
if
\eta_{f_{i}}
and
\varepsilon_{f_{i}}

are the unit and counit of
{f_{i}}^{*}\dashv{f_{i}}_{*}

we will show that

a_{U_{i}}\eta_{f}
and
\eta_{f_{i}}a_{U_{i}}

only differ by an isomorphism of their codomains

a_{U_{i}}f_{*}f^{*}\cong{f_{i}}_{*}{f_{i}}^{*}a_{U_{i}}
,
and similarly

a_{V_{i}}\varepsilon_{f}
and
\varepsilon_{f_{i}}a_{V_{i}}

only differ by an isomorphism of their domains

a_{V_{i}}f^{*}f_{*}\cong{f_{i}}^{*}{f_{i}}_{*}a_{V_{i}}
.

Recall that
a_{U}:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}
has two fully faithful adjoints

j_{U}\dashv a_{U}\dashv i_{U}
,
where
j_{U}(a_{U}(X))=X\times U
and
i_{U}(a_{U}(X))=X^{U}
.
Thus we have the following functors and adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{o(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{U}}

where

\varepsilon_{U}:a_{U}i_{U}\to\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}}
,

\tilde{\eta}_{U}:\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}}\to a_{U}j_{U}
,

\varepsilon_{V}:a_{V}i_{V}\to\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{E}_{o(V)}}
and

\tilde{\eta}_{V}:\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{E}_{o(V)}}\to a_{V}j_{V}

are isomorphisms.
The geometric morphism
\mathcal{E}_{o(V)}\to\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}

is given by
a_{V}f^{*}j_{U}\dashv a_{U}f_{*}i_{V}
.
Our assumption therefore means that
the following two natural transformations
(denoted as string diagrams)
are isomorphisms.

\tilde{\eta}_{U}
\eta_{f}
\eta_{V}
j_{U}
a_{U}
f^{*}
f_{*}
a_{V}
i_{V}

\tilde{\varepsilon}_{U}
\varepsilon_{f}
\varepsilon_{V}
i_{V}
a_{V}
f_{*}
f^{*}
a_{U}
j_{U}

The only additional ingredient we need
is a certain compatibility of
f^{*}
and
f_{*}

with the adjunctions
j_{U}\dashv a_{U}
and
j_{V}\dashv a_{V}
.
Since
f^{*}(X\times U)=f^{*}X\times V

and
(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{U})_{X}:X\times U\to X

and
(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{V})_{Y}:Y\times V\to Y

are the projections on the first factor,
we have a natural isomorphism

\hbox to61.72pt{\vbox to68.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 30.94923pt\lower-34.24767pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

For
f_{*}
the situation is slightly different
as
f_{*}(V)\neq U
,
but we have an arrow
(\eta_{f})_{U}:U\to f_{*}(V)

which becomes an isomorphism after applying
a_{U}

(since
f_{*}(V)\times U=U
).
Thus we have an isomorphism

Now we can piece together isomorphisms
reducing to
a_{U}\eta_{f}
and
a_{V}\varepsilon_{f}
respectively.
(Start by introducing a new squiggle
involving
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{V}
and
\tilde{\eta}_{V}

below
\tilde{\eta}_{V}^{-1}
in the first diagram,
and a new squiggle involving
\eta_{V}
and
\varepsilon_{V}

above
\varepsilon_{V}^{-1}
in the second diagram.)

∎

Remark 3.5.2
.

Lemma
3.5.1

does not generalize to covers by arbitrary subtoposes,
if cover just means that the join of these subtoposes is
\mathcal{F}
.
For example,
the intersection of any open subtopos
\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}

with its closed complement
\mathcal{F}_{c(U)}

is empty,
so the geometric morphism

\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}\amalg\mathcal{F}_{c(U)}\to\mathcal{F}

from their disjoint union
(which is given by the product of categories)
becomes an equivalence when pulled back to
either
\mathcal{F}_{o(U)}
or
\mathcal{F}_{c(U)}
.

Corollary 3.5.3
.

Let
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
be a model
and let
(\phi_{i})_{i\in I}
be closed formulas
such that
\mathbb{T}
proves
\bigvee_{i\in I}\phi_{i}
.
If
M|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
is a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\phi_{i}

for every
i\in I
,
where
\mathcal{E}_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{E}_{o(\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket_{M})}
,
then
M
was already a universal model of
\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

Fixing a classifying topos
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

with universal model
M_{\mathbb{T}}\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}])}
,
we have a geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

with
f^{*}M_{\mathbb{T}}\cong M
.
Since
\bigvee_{i\in I}\phi_{i}
is provable,

U_{i}\vcentcolon=\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket_{M_{\mathbb{T}}}

define an open cover of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

with
M_{\mathbb{T}}|_{\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]_{o(U_{i})}}

a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\phi_{i}

and
f^{*}U_{i}\cong\llbracket\phi_{i}\rrbracket_{M}
.
The induced geometric morphisms

f_{i}:\mathcal{E}_{i}\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]_{o(U_{i})}

are equivalences
since
f_{i}^{*}(M|_{\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]_{o(U_{i})}})\cong M|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}

is also a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\phi_{i}
by assumption.
So Lemma
3.5.1
tells us
that
f
is an equivalence,
that is,

f^{*}M_{\mathbb{T}}\cong M
is a universal
\mathbb{T}
-model.
∎

We can now give our construction of
a syntactic presentation of a topos
\mathcal{E}

covered by open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
i\in I
,
from syntactic presentations of the
\mathcal{E}_{i}

and of the intersections
\mathcal{E}_{S}\vcentcolon=\bigcap_{i\in S}\mathcal{E}_{i}

for
S\subseteq I
finite and nonempty.
The notation

\Delta(I)\vcentcolon=\{\,S\subseteq I\mid 1\leq\lvert S\rvert<\infty\,\}

(thinking of simplices with vertices drawn from
I
)
for the partial order
indexing the required system of presentations
will be useful.
Note that we can write
S^{\prime}\in\Delta(S)

instead of
S^{\prime}\leq S
or
S^{\prime}\subseteq S

in this partial order.

In order to give an elegant description of the resulting theory,
the open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}

will be assumed
to be given by the interpretations of formulas
\phi_{i}

of some base theory
\mathbb{T}_{0}

in a (not necessarily universal)
base model
M_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{0}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})
.
This requirement can always be met,
starting from an arbitrary base theory (e.g. the empty one),
by adding proposition symbols
p_{i}

and the axiom
\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i}

to it,
and then,
since the
p_{i}
necessarily also show up
in the presentation of each
\mathcal{E}_{j}
,
add axioms to these presentations
defining the
p_{i}
by already available formulas
for the open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{j}
.

Theorem 3.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a topos,

\mathbb{T}_{0}
a geometric theory
with closed formulas
\phi_{i}

such that
\bigvee_{i\in I}\phi_{i}
is provable,
and
M_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}_{0}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
a model.
For
S\in\Delta(I)
,
set
\phi_{S}\vcentcolon=\bigwedge_{i\in S}\phi_{i}

and
\mathcal{E}_{S}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{E}_{o(\llbracket\phi_{S}\rrbracket_{M_{0}})}
.
Let a system of presentations
(\mathbb{E}_{S},E_{S})_{S\in\Delta(I)}

of the
\mathcal{E}_{S}
over
M_{0}
be given.
Then
\mathcal{E}
classifies the theory

\mathbb{T}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{T}_{0}+\sum_{S\in\Delta(I)}\mathbb{E}_{S}/\phi_{S}

with universal model

M\vcentcolon=M_{0}+\sum_{S\in\Delta(I)}E_{S}/\phi_{S}.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.5.3
,
we only have to show that
M|_{\mathcal{E}_{\{i\}}}

is a universal model of
\mathbb{T}+\phi_{i}

for every
i\in I
.
So fix
i
.
We know that
M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{\{i\}}}+E_{\{i\}}
,
which lives in the same topos as
M|_{\mathcal{E}_{\{i\}}}
,
is a universal model of
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}}

(in particular,
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}}
proves
\phi_{i}
),
and fortunately,

\mathbb{T}+\phi_{i}
can be written as

\mathbb{T}+\phi_{i}=\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}}+\sum_{S\in\Delta_{i}(I)}(\mathbb{E}_{S\setminus\{i\}}+\mathbb{E}_{S})/\phi_{S}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where
\Delta_{i}(I)\vcentcolon=\{\,S\cup\{i\}\mid S\in\Delta(I\setminus\{i\})\,\}=\{\,S\in\Delta(I)\mid\{i\}\subsetneq S\,\}
.
Also,
the
(\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}})
-part of
M|_{\mathcal{E}_{\{i\}}}

is
M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{\{i\}}}+E_{\{i\}}
.
Thus,
by Lemma
2.3.4
,
we are reduced to showing that
the remaining extension

\sum_{S\in\Delta_{i}(I)}(\mathbb{E}_{S\setminus\{i\}}+\mathbb{E}_{S})/\phi_{S}

is an equivalence extension,
which, by Lemma
3.3.3

and Lemma
3.2.4
,
we may do by showing that
for every
S\in\Delta_{i}(I)
,
the extension

\mathbb{E}^{i}_{S}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}_{S\setminus\{i\}}+\mathbb{E}_{S}

is an equivalence extension of

\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}}+\phi_{S}+\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\Delta_{i}(S),S^{\prime}\neq S}\mathbb{E}^{i}_{S^{\prime}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

For this,
observe that
M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}+E_{\{i\}}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}

is a universal model in
\mathcal{E}_{S}

of
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}}+\phi_{S}
,
which we now treat as a base theory.
And if we set

E^{i}_{S^{\prime}}\vcentcolon=E_{S^{\prime}\setminus\{i\}}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}+E_{S^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

for every
S^{\prime}\in\Delta(S)
,
this in fact constitutes a system of presentations of the topos
\mathcal{E}_{S}
,
since by assumption,
every

M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S^{\prime}}}+\sum_{S^{\prime\prime}\in\Delta(S^{\prime})}E_{S^{\prime\prime}}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S^{\prime}}}

is a universal model,
which can be restricted to a universal model in
\mathcal{E}_{S}

while adding
\phi_{S}
to the theory.
Then Corollary
3.3.5

allows us to conclude that
in particular
the topmost theory extension
\mathbb{E}^{i}_{S}
of this system
is an equivalence extension,
as needed.
∎

Let us also show that
a system of presentations as required in
Theorem
3.5.4

always exists,
starting from presentations of the
\mathcal{E}_{i}

(which exist simply by
Theorem
2.2.2
).
In particular,
it becomes clear that presentations are only needed
for two- and threefold intersections of the open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
not for arbitrary finite intersections.

Proposition 3.5.5
.

Let
M_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}_{0}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
,
let
\phi_{i}
,
i\in I
be closed formulas of
\mathbb{T}_{0}

and let
\phi_{S}
,
\mathcal{E}_{S}
for
S\in\Delta(I)
be as above.
Then any family of presentations
(\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}},E_{\{i\}})

of the
\mathcal{E}_{\{i\}}

over
(\mathbb{T}_{0},M_{0})

can be extended layer-wise
to a system of presentations
(\mathbb{E}_{S},E_{S})_{S\in\Delta(I)}

of the
\mathcal{E}_{S}
,
where
\mathbb{E}_{S}
can be chosen localic for
\lvert S\rvert=2
,
a quotient for
\lvert S\rvert=3

and empty for
\lvert S\rvert\geq 4
.
If the
\mathcal{E}_{\{i\}}
are localic,
then
\mathbb{E}_{S}
can even be chosen a quotient for
\lvert S\rvert=2

and empty for
\lvert S\rvert\geq 3
.

Proof.

Let
S\in\Delta(I)
with
\lvert S\rvert\geq 2

and let
\mathbb{E}_{S^{\prime}}
,
E_{S^{\prime}}
be already defined
for
S^{\prime}\in\Delta(S),S^{\prime}\neq S
.
Fixing any
i\in S

and setting
\widetilde{S}\vcentcolon=S\setminus\{i\}
,
we regroup these extensions as

\mathbb{E}^{i}_{S^{\prime}}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}_{S^{\prime}}+\mathbb{E}_{S^{\prime}\cup\{i\}}

and include
\phi_{S}
from the beginning
to obtain a system of presentations

(\mathbb{E}^{i}_{S^{\prime}},E^{i}_{S^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}})
,

S^{\prime}\in\Delta(\widetilde{S})\setminus\{\widetilde{S}\}

over
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{\{i\}}+\phi_{S}

and
M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}+E_{\{i\}}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}
,
all presenting
\mathcal{E}_{S}
.
By Corollary

3.3.5
,
the sum of this system,
which is

\mathbb{T}_{0}+\phi_{S}+\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\Delta(S)\setminus\{\widetilde{S},S\}}\mathbb{E}_{S^{\prime}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

also presents
\mathcal{E}_{S}
.
We can therefore apply Lemma
2.3.8

to revert the left-over extension
\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{S}}

(with accompanying model extension
E_{\widetilde{S}}
),
obtaining
\mathbb{E}_{S}
and
E_{S}

such that

M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}+\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\Delta(S)}E_{S^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{E}_{S}}\;\in\;\Bigl(\mathbb{T}_{0}+\sum_{S^{\prime}\in\Delta(S)}\mathbb{E}_{S^{\prime}}\Bigr)\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E}_{S})

is universal.
(We can drop
\phi_{S}
,
since we definitely include all
\mathbb{E}_{\{j\}}
,
j\in S
now.)
And indeed

\mathbb{E}_{S}
is localic,
and even a quotient (respectively empty)
if
\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{S}}
is localic (respectively a quotient).
∎

3.6
The localic case

Proposition
3.5.5

suggests that a more concrete formulation of
Theorem
3.5.4

might be feasible
if the open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}

can be presented by localic extensions
of an appropriate common base theory.
This is the case in our applications
to algebro-geometric toposes.
We don’t assume the subterminal objects
U_{i}

to be expressible by formulas of the base theory here.
The theory extension adding a proposition symbol
p

will be denoted
\langle p\rangle
.

Corollary 3.6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a topos
covered by open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{E}_{o(U_{i})}
,
let
M_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{0}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})

and let
E_{i}\in\mathbb{E}_{i}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}})

be model extensions along localic extensions
\mathbb{E}_{i}
of
\mathbb{T}_{0}

such that

M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}+E_{i}\in(\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{i})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E}_{i})

is universal for every
i\in I
.
Then:

(i)

For every
i
,
there are closed formulas
\phi_{i,j}
of
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{i}

presenting the open subtoposes
\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
and for every
i\neq j
,
there is a diagonal quotient extension

\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,j\}}

for
\mathbb{E}_{i}+\phi_{i,j}
and
\mathbb{E}_{j}+\phi_{j,i}

over
\mathbb{T}_{0}

(see Definition
2.3.10
)
consisting of axioms fulfilled by
M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}}+E_{1}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}}+E_{2}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}}
.

(ii)

For any such
\phi_{i,j}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,j\}}
,
the topos
\mathcal{E}
classifies the theory

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}\;\vcentcolon=\;\mathbb{T}_{0}

\displaystyle+\langle p_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}+(\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i})+(\mathbb{E}_{i}/p_{i})_{i\in I}

\displaystyle+\Bigl((p_{j}\;\dashv\vdash_{[]}\;\phi_{i,j})/p_{i}\Bigr)_{i\neq j\in I}+\Bigl(\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,j\}}/(p_{i}\land p_{j})\Bigr)_{i\neq j\in I}

with universal model

M\vcentcolon=M_{0}+(\llbracket p_{i}\rrbracket\vcentcolon=U_{i})_{i\in I}+(E_{i}/p_{i})_{i\in I}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Note that finding appropriate quotient extensions
\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,j\}}
