just means stating enough properties of

M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}}+E_{1}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}}+E_{2}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}}

as axioms
to make the relation symbols of
\mathbb{E}_{2}

definable by formulas of
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{1}

and vice versa,
see Remark
2.3.6
.

Proof.

The existence of the
\phi_{i,j}
is clear.
For
\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,j\}}
,
observe that

\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{i}+\phi_{i,j}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{j}+\phi_{j,i}

both present the topos
\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{j}
,
with universal models agreeing in the
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-part.
Thus, we obtain
\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,j\}}

from Corollary
2.3.9
.

Now set

\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{T}_{0}+\langle p_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}+(\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i}),\quad\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\{i\}}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}_{i}+p_{i}+(p_{j}\;\dashv\vdash_{[]}\;\phi_{i,j})_{j\neq i},\quad\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\{i,j\}}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,j\}}.

Then
\widetilde{M}_{0}\vcentcolon=M_{0}+(\llbracket p_{i}\rrbracket\vcentcolon=U_{i})_{i\in I}

is a model of
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
in
\mathcal{E}
,
and
\widetilde{M}_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}+E_{i}

is a universal model of

\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}+\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\{i\}}
,
since this only differs from
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{i}

by an extension by definitions,
and similarly for
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\{i,j\}}
.
By Proposition
3.5.5
,
this system of presentations can be extended
by setting
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{S}=\varnothing

for all
S\in\Delta(I)
with
\lvert S\rvert\geq 3
.
Then we can apply
Theorem
3.5.4

and obtain the theory
\mathbb{T}
with universal model
M
as stated.
(We only split up
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\{i\}}/p_{i}

and dropped the trivial extension
p_{i}/p_{i}
.)
∎

3.7
Application to Zariski toposes

We now apply our results
to deduce a syntactic presentation
for the big Zariski topos of an arbitrary scheme,
which can be viewed as glued from big Zariski toposes
of affine schemes.
We first recall the definition,
and the classifying property in the affine case.

The
big Zariski site
of a scheme
S

is the category
\mathrm{Sch}/S
of schemes over
S
,
equipped with the Zariski topology
J_{\mathrm{Zar}}

in which a sieve on an object
T\to S

is covering if and only if
there is an open cover
T=\bigcup_{i}T_{i}

of the scheme
T

such that the sieve contains the open embeddings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

The site defined in this way
does not admit any (small) dense set of objects,
so one has to restrict the class of objects in an appropriate way
before the sheaf topos can be formed.
(The resulting topos is still called a
big
Zariski topos,
unless the site only contains open subschemes of
S
.)
We choose the subcategory
(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}}

of schemes locally of finite presentation over
S
,
yielding the
big Zariski topos

S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}\vcentcolon=\Sh((\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}},J_{\mathrm{Zar}})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The motivation for this particular class of objects is
that in the affine case
S=\Spec K

(where
K
is any commutative unitary ring),
the big Zariski topos
(\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is the classifying topos of the theory of local
K
-algebras.

Definition 3.7.1
.

(i)

The (algebraic) theory of rings will be denoted
\mathrm{Ring}
.
It has one sort
A
,
function symbols
0,1:A
,
{-}:A\to A
,

{+},{\cdot}:A\times A\to A

and the usual axioms of a commutative unitary ring.

(ii)

In the theory
\mathrm{Ring}
(or any extension of it),
we use the abbreviation

\inv(x)\quad\vcentcolon=\quad\exists\overline{x}{\,:\,}A.\;(x\overline{x}=1)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(iii)

We can require a ring to be local
by the quotient extension

(\mathrm{loc})\vcentcolon=\quad\Bigl\{\;\exists\vec{y}{\,:\,}A^{n}.\;(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}=1)\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:A^{n}}\;\bigvee_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\inv(x_{i})\quad\mid\quad n\in\mathbb{N}\;\Bigr\}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(An alternative, syntactically equivalent axiomatization
is given by the two sequents

0=1\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot

and

\top\;\vdash_{x:A}\;\inv(x)\lor\inv(1-x)
.)

(iv)

For a ring
K
,
the extension
{K\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}
of the theory
\mathrm{Ring}

consists of
constant symbols
c_{\lambda}:A
for all
\lambda\in K

and the axioms (for all
\lambda,\mu\in K
as needed)

c_{0}=0,\quad c_{\lambda}+c_{\mu}=c_{\lambda+\mu},\quad c_{1}=1,\quad c_{\lambda}c_{\mu}=c_{\lambda\mu}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(v)

The theory of
K
-algebras is

{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Ring}+{K\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

A quick sketch of a proof
that
(\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

classifies
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})
,
using notions from Section
4
,
goes as follows.
Since the theory
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}
is algebraic,
a presheaf site for its classifying topos
is given by the opposite of the category of
finitely presented
K
-algebras.
But this is equivalent to the full subcategory
on the affine objects of
(\mathrm{Sch}/\Spec K)_{\mathrm{lofp}}
,
which is a dense subcategory with respect to
J_{\mathrm{Zar}}
,
and therefore,
equipped with the topology induced by
J_{\mathrm{Zar}}
,
an alternative site for the same topos
by the Comparison Lemma.
Finally,
the subtopos defined by
this Zariski topology on the finitely presented
K
-algebras
is the subtopos presented by the quotient extension
(\mathrm{loc})
.

The universal model of
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})
in
(\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is the structure sheaf

\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}\;:\;{(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set},\quad T\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{T}(T)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Remark 3.7.2
.

The theory extension
{K\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}

(and therefore the theory
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}
)
can be formulated more economically,
using a presentation of the ring
K
by generators and relations.
Namely,
if
K=\mathbb{Z}[X_{i}]/(r_{j})
,
where
i
and
j
run over any two index sets,
it suffices to add constant symbols
c_{i}:A

and axioms
r_{j}(c_{i})=0

to the theory of rings,
where
r_{j}(c_{i})
is to be interpreted as a closed term,
using the available ring structure.
The theory
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}
from
Definition
3.7.1

can then be obtained by an extension by definitions.
So for example,
the topos
(\Spec\mathbb{Z}[X])_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

classifies the theory of local rings
with one distinguished element.

The big Zariski topos is functorial in the scheme
S
,
in that a morphism
S^{\prime}\to S
of schemes
induces a geometric morphism

S^{\prime}_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}\to S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
,
see
[
9
,
Tag 0210
]
.
But we only need the special case where

S^{\prime}
is an open subscheme of
S
here.
In this case,
observe that
S^{\prime}
can be regarded as
a subterminal object of the site
(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}}

and,
since the Zariski topology
J_{\mathrm{Zar}}
is subcanonical,
determines a subterminal sheaf

U_{S^{\prime}}\vcentcolon=\Hom_{S}(-,S^{\prime})\;:\;{(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Lemma 3.7.3
.

(i)

The mapping

S^{\prime}\mapsto(S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{S^{\prime}})}

from open subschemes of
S

to open subtoposes of
S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is monotone and
preserves finite intersections and arbitrary unions.

(ii)

For any open subscheme
S^{\prime}
of
S
,
the open subtopos
(S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{S^{\prime}})}

is equivalent to
{S^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
.
The inverse image
\iota^{*}

and the further left adjoint
\iota_{!}

of the open embedding

\iota:{S^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}\hookrightarrow S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

are given by

(\iota^{*}F)(T^{\prime})=F(T^{\prime})\qquad(T^{\prime}\in(\mathrm{Sch}/S^{\prime})_{\mathrm{lofp}})

and

(\iota_{!}F^{\prime})(T)=\begin{cases}F^{\prime}(T),&\text{if $T\to S$ factors through $S^{\prime}$}\\
\varnothing,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad(T\in(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(iii)

If
S=\Spec K
is affine
(and therefore
S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
classifies
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})
)
and
f\in K
,
then the open subtopos
(S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{D(f)})}

corresponding to the standard open
D(f)\subseteq S

is presented by the closed geometric formula

\inv(c_{f})\quad\vcentcolon=\quad\exists x{\,:\,}A.\;(xc_{f}=1)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Proof.

(i)

We can identify open subtoposes with subterminal sheaves

F\subseteq 1_{S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}
,
which can in turn be thought of
as certain classes of objects of
(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}}
,
where
(S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{S^{\prime}})}

corresponds to the class of all
scheme morphisms
T\to S

(locally of finite presentation)
that factor through
S^{\prime}
.
Monotonicity is then clear,
as well as
U_{S}=1_{S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}
.
For binary intersections,
we have to check that
T\to S

factors through
S^{\prime}_{1}\cap S^{\prime}_{2}

if and only if it factors through
S^{\prime}_{1}
and
S^{\prime}_{2}
.
But this is also clear.
For unions,
let
(S^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
be some family of open subschemes
and let
F\subseteq 1_{S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}

be given with
U_{S^{\prime}_{i}}\leq F
for all
i
,
that is,
\lvert F(S^{\prime}_{i})\rvert=1
.
Because
S^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\bigcup_{i\in I}S^{\prime}_{i}

is covered by the
S^{\prime}_{i}
with respect to
J_{\mathrm{Zar}}
,
this implies
\lvert F(S^{\prime})\rvert=1
,
so
U_{S^{\prime}}\leq F
, as needed.

(ii)

The underlying category of
the open subtopos
(S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{S^{\prime}})}

is equivalent to

\Sh((\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}},J_{\mathrm{Zar}})/\Hom_{S}(-,S^{\prime})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

the category of all those sheaves
F
on
(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}}

for which
F(T)\neq\varnothing
implies that

T\to S
factors through
S^{\prime}
.
The category of presheaves on
(\mathrm{Sch}/S)_{\mathrm{lofp}}
with this property
is clearly equivalent to
\PSh((\mathrm{Sch}/S^{\prime})_{\mathrm{lofp}})
,
and one can check that the sheaf conditions
with respect to the two Zariski topologies
are then equivalent.
Then we have

(\iota^{*}F)(T^{\prime})=(F\times U_{S^{\prime}})(T^{\prime})=F(T^{\prime})

for
T^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
,
and the further left adjoint
\iota_{!}

is the forgetful functor

S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}/U_{S^{\prime}}\to S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
,
as stated.

(iii)

We know that
S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

classifies
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})

with universal model

\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}
,
and
(D(f))_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}=(\Spec K_{f})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

classifies
{K_{f}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})
,
which is equivalent to
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})+(\inv(c_{f}))
,
with universal model

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}_{(D(f))_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}
.
So to show that the embedding
\iota

is presented by the quotient extension
(\mathrm{loc})
,
all we have to check is that

\iota^{*}\mathcal{O}\cong\mathcal{O}^{\prime}

as models of
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}
.
And this follows from the description of
\iota^{*}
in (ii).

∎

Remark 3.7.4
.

Not all open subtoposes of
S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

correspond to open subschemes of
S
in this way.
For example,

\Hom_{S}(-,S^{\prime})

is also a subterminal object
if
S^{\prime}
is a
closed
subscheme of
S
.

Before we formulate the theory classified by
S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

in generality,
we discuss the example of the projective line.

Proposition 3.7.5
.

The big Zariski topos
(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

of the projective line over a ring
K

classifies the theory
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}}
,
which is
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})

expanded by two relation symbols

\widetilde{c}_{1},\widetilde{c}_{2}\subseteq A

and certain axioms as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}}\;\vcentcolon=\;{}

\displaystyle{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})+\langle\widetilde{c}_{1},\widetilde{c}_{2}\subseteq A\rangle

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\land x^{\prime}\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\;\vdash_{x,x^{\prime}}\;x=x^{\prime}\Bigr)_{i=1,2}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;(\exists x.\;x\in\widetilde{c}_{1})\lor(\exists x.\;x\in\widetilde{c}_{2})\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x_{1}\in\widetilde{c}_{1}\land x_{2}\in\widetilde{c}_{2}\;\vdash_{x_{1},x_{2}}\;x_{1}x_{2}=1\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\land\inv(x)\;\vdash_{x}\;\exists y.\;y\in\widetilde{c}_{i^{\prime}}\Bigr)_{(i,i^{\prime})=(1,2),(2,1)}

The relation symbols
\widetilde{c}_{1},\widetilde{c}_{2}\subseteq A

should be understood as

“partial constants”

c_{1},c_{2}:A
,
with the following properties.
At least one of them is defined,
and if one is defined then the other is its inverse,
which may or may not be defined.

Proof.

An affine open cover of the scheme
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}

is given by two copies of the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}
,
we write

\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}=\Spec K[X_{1}]\cup\Spec K[X_{2}]\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and their intersection is

\Spec K[X_{1}]\cap\Spec K[X_{2}]=\Spec K[X_{1},X_{2}]/(X_{1}X_{2}-1)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

with the open inclusions corresponding to
the
K
-algebra maps suggested by the notation.
In other words,
as an open subscheme of each
\Spec K[X_{i}]
,
the overlap is the standard open
D(X_{i})
,
and the identification corresponds to
the isomorphism of rings is

\varphi:K[X_{1}]_{X_{1}}\to K[X_{2}]_{X_{2}},\quad X_{1}\mapsto{X_{2}}^{-1}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

We want to apply Corollary
3.6.1

to the open cover of toposes

(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}=\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}

induced by the above
via Lemma
3.7.3
.
Consider the structure sheaf of
(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
,
which is a sheaf of
K
-algebras,
as a model

M_{0}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{O}\in({K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Then the
({K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}})
-models
M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}

are the respective structure sheaves
(by Lemma
3.7.3
(ii)),
that is,
they become universal models of

{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\langle c_{i}:A\rangle+(\mathrm{loc})

when the constant symbol
c_{i}

is interpreted as the global section
of
M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{(\Spec K[X_{i}])_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}

corresponding to
X_{i}
.
In particular,

M_{0}
satisfies
(\mathrm{loc})
,
so we can use as our base theory

\mathbb{T}_{0}\vcentcolon={K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and we have presentations
(\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{i},M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}+E_{i})

of the
\mathcal{E}_{i}
with

\mathbb{E}_{i}\vcentcolon=\langle c_{i}:A\rangle\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Using Lemma
3.7.3
(ii),
the subtopos
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}

is presented in
\mathcal{E}_{i}

by the closed geometric formula

\phi_{i}\vcentcolon=\inv(c_{i})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

It remains to find a diagonal quotient extension
of
\mathbb{E}_{1}+\phi_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{2}+\phi_{2}
over
\mathbb{T}_{0}

satisfied by
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}}

(with the interpretations of
c_{i}
coming from
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}
).
Since
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}}
is
the universal local
K[X_{1},X_{2}]/(X_{1}X_{2}-1)
-algebra,
we can take

\mathbb{Q}_{\{1,2\}}\vcentcolon=\Bigl(\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;c_{1}c_{2}=1\Bigr)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Now,
to form conditional extensions like
\mathbb{E}_{i}/p_{i}
,
we have to replace the constant symbols
c_{i}

by relation symbols
\widetilde{c}_{i}\subseteq A

with appropriate axioms,
and rewrite the formulas in which they appear.
(We need to
“desugar”
the syntax.)
Thus we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{i}

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{c}_{i}\subseteq A\rangle+\Bigl(\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\exists x.\;x\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\Bigr)+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\land x^{\prime}\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\;\vdash_{x,x^{\prime}}\;x=x^{\prime}\Bigr),

\displaystyle\phi_{i}

\displaystyle=\exists x.\;(x\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\land\inv(x)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{\{1,2\}}

\displaystyle=\exists x_{1},x_{2}.\;(x_{1}\in\widetilde{c}_{1}\land x_{2}\in\widetilde{c}_{2}\land x_{1}x_{2}=1).

Then Corollary
3.6.1

gives us a theory
\mathbb{T}
classified by
(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
.
This
\mathbb{T}
contains proposition symbols
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
which can however be eliminated since
\mathbb{T}
proves

p_{i}\;\dashv\vdash_{[]}\;\exists x.\;x\in\widetilde{c}_{i}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(This is always the case
when the extensions
\mathbb{E}_{i}
contain at least one constant symbol.)
Then one can check that the resulting theory
is syntactically equivalent to
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{K}}

as defined in the statement.
∎

In the general case,
we will of course have more than two affine schemes in the covering,
but also,
their intersections
will not be given as single standard open subschemes.
Recall, however,
that the intersection
S_{1}\cap S_{2}

of two affine open subschemes
of a scheme
S

can always be covered by open subschemes
which are standard opens in both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}

[
11
, 5.3.1. Proposition]
.

Theorem 3.7.6
.

Let
S=\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}
be a scheme
covered by affine open subschemes
S_{i}=\Spec K_{i}
,
and for every
i\neq i^{\prime}\in I
,
let an open cover

S_{i}\cap S_{i^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}S_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}^{j}

be given,
such that

\Spec K_{i}\supseteq D(f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j})=S_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}^{j}=D(f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j})\subseteq\Spec K_{i^{\prime}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

with corresponding ring isomorphisms

\varphi_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}=(\varphi_{i^{\prime},i}^{j})^{-1}:(K_{i})_{f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}\to(K_{i^{\prime}})_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Then the big Zariski topos
S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

classifies the theory

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{S}\;\vcentcolon=\;{}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ring}+(\mathrm{loc})+\langle p_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}+(\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i})+(({K_{i}\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}})/p_{i})_{i\in I}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}\land\inv(x)\;\vdash_{x}\;p_{i^{\prime}}\Bigr)_{i\neq i^{\prime}\in I,j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(p_{i}\land p_{i^{\prime}}\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bigvee_{j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}\exists x.\;(x\in\widetilde{c}_{f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}\land\inv(x))\Bigr)_{i\neq i^{\prime}\in I,j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}}\land\inv(x)\land y\in\widetilde{c}_{\lambda}\land z\in\widetilde{c}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\;\vdash_{x,y,z}\;x^{n}y=z\Bigr)_{i\neq i^{\prime}\in I,j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}},\lambda\in K_{i}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where in the last family of axioms,

\lambda^{\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

are chosen for each
\lambda\in K_{i}

such that

\varphi_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}(\lambda)=(f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j})^{-n}\lambda^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.7.3
,
the topos
\mathcal{E}\vcentcolon=S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
is covered by the open subtoposes

\mathcal{E}_{i}\vcentcolon=(S_{i})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
.
The restrictions
\mathcal{O}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}}

of the
\mathrm{Ring}
-model
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}

can be extended by
K_{i}
-algebra structures,
resulting in universal models of
{K_{i}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+(\mathrm{loc})
.
So we can use the base theory

\mathbb{T}_{0}\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Ring}+(\mathrm{loc})

with the model

M_{0}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{O}\;\in\;(\mathrm{Ring}+(\mathrm{loc}))\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and have presentations of the
\mathcal{E}_{i}
with

\mathbb{E}_{i}\vcentcolon={K_{i}\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The open subtopos
\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{i^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{i}

is then presented by the closed geometric formula

\phi_{i,i^{\prime}}\;\vcentcolon=\;\bigvee_{j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}\inv(c_{f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

To find appropriate diagonal quotient extensions
\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}
,
consider the model

M_{0}|_{\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\mathcal{E}_{i^{\prime}}}=\mathcal{O}_{(S_{i}\cap S_{i^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which carries both a
K_{i}
-algebra structure
and a
K_{i^{\prime}}
-algebra structure.
For every
j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}
,
the further restriction to
(S_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}^{j})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is the universal local
(K_{i})_{f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}
-algebra
and at the same time
the universal local
(K_{i^{\prime}})_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}}
-algebra,
with the algebra structures coinciding
via the isomorphism
\varphi_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}
.
For every
j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}

and every
\lambda\in K_{i}
,
write

\varphi_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}(\lambda)=(f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j})^{-n}\lambda^{\prime}

for some
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}_{j,\lambda}\in K_{i^{\prime}}
,

n=n_{j,\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
\mathcal{O}_{(S_{i}\cap S_{i^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}

satisfies the axioms

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{i,i^{\prime}}\quad

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\quad\Bigl\{\quad\inv(c_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}})\;\vdash_{[]}\;(c_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}})^{n}c_{\lambda}=c_{\lambda^{\prime}}\quad\mid\quad j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}},\lambda\in K_{i}\quad\Bigr\}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}\quad

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\quad\mathbb{Q}_{i,i^{\prime}}+\mathbb{Q}_{i^{\prime},i}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Note that the sequents in
\mathbb{Q}_{i,i^{\prime}}
also make

\inv(c_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}})\;\vdash_{[]}\;\inv(c_{f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}})

provable,
because we can set
\lambda\vcentcolon=f_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}

and then
\lambda^{\prime}
will be invertible
as an element of
(K_{i^{\prime}})_{f_{i^{\prime},i}}
.

The quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}_{i,i^{\prime}}

clearly makes each
c_{\lambda}

definable in terms of the
K_{i^{\prime}}
-algebra structure
if we assume one of the formulas
\inv(c_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}})
.
But we need
c_{\lambda}
to be definable by
a single formula of
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{i^{\prime}}+\phi_{i^{\prime},i}
.
Thus, consider

\phi_{c_{\lambda}}(x)\quad\vcentcolon=\quad\bigvee_{j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}\Bigl(\inv(c_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}})\land(c_{f_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}})^{n}x=c_{\lambda^{\prime}}\Bigr)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Then we see that
\mathbb{Q}_{i,i^{\prime}}

is exactly what we need,
together with
\phi_{i^{\prime},i}
,
to prove

\phi_{c_{\lambda}}(x)\;\dashv\vdash_{x}\;x=c_{\lambda}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

completing our argument why
\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}

is a diagonal quotient extension
of
\mathbb{E}_{i}+\phi_{i,i^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{i^{\prime}}+\phi_{i^{\prime},i}

over
\mathbb{T}_{0}
.
(It is automatic that

\phi_{c_{\lambda}}(x)
is provably functional
as a formula of
\mathbb{T}_{0}+\mathbb{E}_{i^{\prime}}+\phi_{i^{\prime},i}
,
see Remark
2.3.6
.)

Now Corollary
3.6.1

can finally be applied to the data

\mathbb{T}_{0}
,
\mathbb{E}_{i}
,
\phi_{i,i^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}
,
considering all the constant symbols
c_{\lambda}:A

to be
“syntactic sugar”
for
relation symbols
\widetilde{c}_{\lambda}\subseteq A

together with appropriate axioms.
The resulting theory
\mathbb{T}

is syntactically equivalent to
\mathbb{T}_{S}
as given in the statement.
We have only simplified the axioms
by dropping
p_{i}
in the antecedent
whenever a formula of the form
x\in\widetilde{c}_{\lambda}

appears there too,
for some
\lambda\in K_{i}
,
since the equivalence

p_{i}\;\dashv\vdash_{[]}\;\exists x.\;x\in\widetilde{c}_{\lambda}

is already contained in

({K_{i}\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}})/p_{i}
.
∎

Remark 3.7.7
.

Theorem
3.7.6

can be regarded as a generalization of

[
3
, Proposition 16.3]
,
where the
\mathrm{Set}
-based points of
S_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

are identified as the
“local rings over
S
”
,
that is,
local rings
A

together with a scheme morphism
\Spec A\to S
.
One can check that
this category is indeed equivalent to

\mathbb{T}_{S}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})
.

3.8
Conditional extensions and Artin gluing

We now consider the question
what the classifying topos of
a conditionally extended theory
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi

looks like,
that is,
we aim to describe
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi]

in terms of the geometric morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbb{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi}}

where
\pi_{\phi}
is an open embedding,
but
\pi_{\mathbb{E}}
can be an arbitrary geometric morphism
(see Theorem
2.2.2
).
This situation can perhaps be visualized
by the following picture.
{\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]}
{\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi]}
{\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}]}

In the special case where
the base theory is simply the theory
\langle p\rangle

of a proposition symbol
p
with no axioms,
we can give an answer
directly by analyzing the syntactic site of the resulting theory.
Recall that the
Sierpiński cone
(or Freyd cover)
\scn(\mathcal{E})

of a topos
\mathcal{E}
is the topos with underlying category
the comma category
(\mathrm{Set}\downarrow\Gamma)
,
where
\Gamma:\mathcal{E}\to\mathrm{Set}
is the global sections functor,
and that it has a canonical subterminal object

U=(\varnothing,1_{\mathcal{E}},!)
,
where
!:\varnothing\to\Gamma(1_{\mathcal{E}})=\{*\}
,
such that the corresponding open subtopos is

\scn(\mathcal{E})_{o(U)}\simeq\mathcal{E}
,
while its closed complement is just a point,

\scn(\mathcal{E})_{c(U)}\simeq\mathrm{Set}
.
This corresponds to the fact that

\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p+p
is equivalent to
\mathbb{T}

and
\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p+\lnot p

is equivalent to the empty theory
(over the empty signature)
classified by
\mathrm{Set}
.

Proposition 3.8.1
.

For any geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
,
the theory
\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p

is classified by the Sierpiński cone
\scn(\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}])
.

Proof.

A construction of the Sierpiński cone
on the level of sites
proceeds as follows.
Given a site
(C,J)
,
we define
C^{\prime}
by freely adjoining a terminal object to
C
,
that is,
\Ob(C^{\prime})=\Ob(C)\sqcup\{*\}

and
\lvert\Hom(c,*)\rvert=1
,
\Hom(*,c)=\varnothing

for all
c\in\Ob(C)
.
The topology
J^{\prime}
is given by simply leaving the covering sieves of any
c\in C

(which are still sieves in
C^{\prime}
, as there is no arrow
*\to c
)
as they are
and declaring only the maximal sieve on
*
to be covering.
The axioms of a Grothendieck topology are clearly satisfied.
Then a sheaf
F^{\prime}
on
(C^{\prime},J^{\prime})

is exactly a sheaf
F
on
(C,J)

together with a set
F^{\prime}(*)

and a map
F^{\prime}(*)\to\Gamma(F)
,
as required.

We claim that this is precisely the relationship
between the syntactic sites
C_{\mathbb{T}}
and
C_{\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p}
.
First note that we have a full and faithful functor

C_{\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p+p}\to C_{\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p},\quad\{\vec{x}.\;\phi\}\mapsto\{\vec{x}.\;\phi\land p\}

with essential image
C_{\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p}/\{[].\;p\}
,
the subcategory of all
\{\vec{x}.\;\phi\}

with
\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}}\;p
provable in
\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p
.
(This is true for any closed formula of any geometric theory.)
But of course
C_{\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p+p}\simeq C_{\mathbb{T}}
.
And if an object
\{\vec{x}.\;\phi\}\in C_{\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p}

does not lie over
\{[].\;p\}
,
we can show that the context
\vec{x}
must be empty
(because
\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p
contains the axiom

\top\;\vdash_{x\,{:}\,S}\;p
for any of its sorts)
and, by induction over
\phi
,
that
\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\phi
is provable in
\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p
.
So the only missing object (up to isomorphism)
is the terminal object
\{[].\;\top\}
,
and this terminal object is strict,
since
\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;p
is not provable in
\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p
.
It is easy to verify that
the Grothendieck topology on
C_{\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{T}/p}

is also the one coming from
C_{\mathbb{T}}
as above.
∎

For the general case,
recall from
[
7
, Proposition 4.5.6]

that any topos
\mathcal{E}

equipped with a subterminal object
U

can be reconstructed from the toposes
\mathcal{E}_{o(U)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{c(U)}

(the open respectively closed subtopos of
\mathcal{E}

corresponding to
U
)
and the left exact functor

F\vcentcolon=j^{*}\circ i_{*}:\mathcal{E}_{o(U)}\to\mathcal{E}_{c(U)}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

called the
fringe functor
of
\mathcal{E}

(with respect to
U
),
where
i:\mathcal{E}_{o(U)}\to\mathcal{E}
and
j:\mathcal{E}_{c(U)}\to\mathcal{E}

are the inclusion geometric morphisms.
Namely,

\mathcal{E}
is equivalent to the
Artin gluing
of
F
,
defined, for any left exact functor
F:\mathcal{E}_{1}\to\mathcal{E}_{2}
between toposes,
as the comma category

\Gl(F)\vcentcolon=(\mathcal{E}_{2}\downarrow F)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

equipped with the subterminal object

(\varnothing_{\mathcal{E}_{2}},1_{\mathcal{E}_{1}},!)
,
where
!:\varnothing_{\mathcal{E}_{2}}\to F(1_{\mathcal{E}_{1}})=1_{\mathcal{E}_{2}}
.
In our situation,
the topos
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi]

is therefore an Artin gluing of the open subtopos

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi+\phi]=\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}]

and its closed complement

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi+\lnot\phi]=\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\lnot\phi+\mathbb{E}/\bot]\simeq\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\lnot\phi]\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Conjecture 3.8.2
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a geometric theory,

\phi
a closed formula of
\mathbb{T}

and
\mathbb{E}
an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\phi
.
Then the theory
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi

is classified by the topos

\Gl\Bigl(\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}]\xrightarrow{{\pi_{\phi+\mathbb{E}}}_{*}}\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]\xrightarrow{{\pi_{\lnot\phi}}^{*}}\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\lnot\phi]\Bigr)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The functor
{\pi_{\lnot\phi}}^{*}\circ{\pi_{\phi+\mathbb{E}}}_{*}

can also be understood as the composition
F\circ{\pi_{\mathbb{E}}}_{*}
,
where
F:\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\lnot\phi]

is the fringe functor of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]
,
that is,
\Gl(F)\simeq\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]
.
What the conjecture says, then,
is that the topos
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi]

is the result of replacing
the open subtopos
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi]
of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

by
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}]

in a canonical way,
using the given geometric morphism
\pi_{\mathbb{E}}
.

To prove it,
we would have to show that the given left exact functor
is isomorphic to the fringe functor of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi]
.
One attempt to do this is to show that
the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi+\phi]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi+\lnot\phi]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\lnot\phi]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{\phi}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{\mathbb{E}/\phi}}_{*}}

\scriptstyle\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{\mathbb{E}}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{\mathbb{E}/\phi}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{\lnot\phi}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{\phi}}_{*}}

\cong

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{\lnot\phi}}^{*}}

\Rightarrow

The left half, consisting of direct image functors, does commute.
In the right half,
where direct and inverse images occur,
we can write a
“Beck–Chevalley”
natural transformation
which we would hope to be an isomorphism.
For this, however,
it cannot suffice to use the information that
the corresponding square of geometric morphisms is a pullback
with the horizontal geometric morphisms closed embeddings
and the right vertical one an equivalence,
since this would also be true,
for example,
if we wrote instead of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}/\phi]

the disjoint union of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\phi+\mathbb{E}]

and
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\lnot\phi]

(which is the Artin gluing of the constant functor
with value
1
).

If we assume the conjecture to be true,
we can spell out another special case
(containing Proposition
3.8.1
)
and give a syntactic presentation for the

open mapping cylinder
of a geometric morphism,
which is the Artin gluing of its direct image functor.

Proposition 3.8.3
.

Let
\pi_{\mathbb{E}}:\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{E}]\to\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

be a geometric morphism
with chosen syntactic presentation.
Assuming Conjecture
3.8.2
,
the theory
\mathbb{T}+\langle p\rangle+\mathbb{E}/p

is classified by the open mapping cylinder of
\pi_{\mathbb{E}}
,

\Gl({\pi_{\mathbb{E}}}_{*})
.

Proof.

We regard
\mathbb{E}
as an extension of
\mathbb{T}+\langle p\rangle+p

and apply the conjecture to the base theory
\mathbb{T}+\langle p\rangle
.
What then remains to show is that the composite

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]\simeq\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\langle p\rangle+p]\xrightarrow{{\pi_{p}}_{*}}\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\langle p\rangle]\xrightarrow{{\pi_{\lnot p}}^{*}}\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\langle p\rangle+\lnot p]\simeq\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

is isomorphic to the identity functor.
This is true,
since
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}+\langle p\rangle]

is the product of
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

with the Sierpiński topos
\mathrm{Set}[\langle p\rangle]
,
which can indeed be obtained as
the Artin gluing along the identity functor.
∎

Remark 3.8.4
.

There is a dual notion to conditional extensions,
where a model involves a model extension in some closed subtopos
instead of an open one,
and one could make a dual conjecture
about replacing a closed subtopos,
using the inverse image part of a geometric morphism.
Namely,
if
\mathbb{E}
is an extension
(without function symbols)
of
\mathbb{T}+\lnot\phi

(where
\phi
is a closed geometric formula
and
\lnot\phi
is the axiom
\phi\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot
),
then we would denote
\mathbb{E}/\lnot\phi
the extension of
\mathbb{T}

consisting of the following.

•

For every sort
S
of
\mathbb{E}
,
a sort
S
and the axioms

\phi\;\vdash_{x,x^{\prime}\,{:}\,S}\;x=x^{\prime}

and
\phi\;\vdash_{[]}\;\exists x{\,:\,}S.\;\top
.

•

For every relation symbol
R\subseteq\vec{S}
of
\mathbb{E}
,
a relation symbol
R\subseteq\vec{S}

and the axiom
\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{S}}\;R(\vec{x})
.

•

For every axiom
\psi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\chi
,
the axiom
\psi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\chi\lor\psi
.

4
Theories of presheaf type

4.1
Introduction

In this section,
we develop some results,
to be used in the next section,
concerning geometric theories
whose classifying topos is
of the form

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]\simeq\PSh(C)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

called theories
of presheaf type

[
1
]
.
This class of theories is of great significance
for the problem of finding a concise syntactic presentation
of a given topos,
since it offers the following shortcut.
Whenever we know that a theory
\mathbb{T}
is of presheaf type,
there is a canonical (presheaf) site of definition for
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

defined in a purely categorical way
from the category
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

of
\mathrm{Set}
-based models of
\mathbb{T}
.
Namely,

\mathbb{T}
is of presheaf type if and only if

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]\simeq[\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c},\mathrm{Set}]\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}
denotes the category of
compact objects in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})
,
also known as the finitely presentable models of
\mathbb{T}
.
In particular,
a theory of presheaf type is fully determined
by its
\mathrm{Set}
-based models,
in contrast to the fact that a general geometric theory
can have no
\mathrm{Set}
-based models at all
without being inconsistent.

To make use of this feature,
we need ways to recognize theories of presheaf type
without having to construct presheaf sites
for their classifying toposes by hand.
The basic (and already very useful) result of this kind
is that a theory is of presheaf type
if its axioms satisfy certain syntactic restrictions,
such as, in the simplest case,
algebraic theories.
If a given theory
does not meet this condition,
one can of course try to replace the problematic axioms
by equivalent axioms of the required form,
or, much more drastically,
to transform the whole theory into a Morita equivalent theory
satisfying the syntactic restrictions,
using equivalence extensions
to introduce and eliminate
function symbols, relation symbols and sorts.
However, it is not clear how to find such a transformation
if it is not obvious from the theory.

Instead, one can also regard
\mathbb{T}
as a quotient
of a theory
\mathbb{T}_{0}
which contains only syntactically simple axioms.
Then, by the duality between quotient theories and subtoposes,
the additional axioms of
\mathbb{T}
correspond to a Grothendieck topology
on the canonical presheaf site for
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}_{0}]
.
If this topology is rigid,
meaning that its sheaves
are just the presheaves on some smaller presheaf site
and therefore
\mathbb{T}
is again of presheaf type,
we speak of a
rigid-topology quotient
.
For testing this rigidity condition,
it is important to have a convenient description
of the topology induced by the additional axioms,
which we provide in
Theorem
4.3.1
.

This strategy can also be used
to show that certain classes of axioms,
such as axioms stated in the empty context,
can be added to
any
theory of presheaf type
without destroying the presheaf type property.
This allows us, for example,
to start with a syntactically simple base theory,
apply some equivalence extension to it
(possibly involving syntactically complex axioms),
and
then
add an axiom
that is known to always preserve presheaf type.
This is in contrast to the syntactically simple axioms,
like algebraic axioms,
which are only harmless in a base theory
but not when added to an arbitrary theory of presheaf type,
as we will see.

We further explore the possibilities
of building up theories of presheaf type incrementally
by looking at extensions involving function
symbols.
While adding finitely many constant symbols
does in fact always preserve presheaf type,
we quickly obtain negative results after that.
Not only can countably many constant symbols
or a single unary function symbol
destroy presheaf type,
they can even do so
when added to a theory which
is trivial up to Morita equivalence,
that is, classified by
\mathrm{Set}
.

The following table summarizes our findings
about which syntactic forms of extensions
always preserve presheaf type
and which can destroy presheaf type.

Extension

Always preserves

Reference

presheaf type

Algebraic axiom

no

Example
4.5.4

Axiom in empty context

yes

Corollary
4.4.2

Countably many axioms in empty context

no

Remark
4.4.4

Any number of negated axioms

yes

Corollary
4.4.1

Constant symbol

yes

Proposition
4.4.5

Function symbol

no

Example
4.5.3

Countably many constant symbols

no

Examples
4.5.2

and
4.5.3

4.2
Background

A geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
is
of presheaf type

if it admits a universal model in some presheaf topos,
that is,

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]\simeq\PSh(C)

for some small category
C
.
To discuss this notion properly,
we first have to recall the definition of compact objects.

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
C
be a category with all filtered colimits.
An object
X
of
C

is
compact

if
\Hom(X,-):C\to\mathrm{Set}

preserves filtered colimits.
We denote the full subcategory of
C

on the compact objects
by
C_{c}
.

The compact objects in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

are usually called the
finitely presentable
models of
\mathbb{T}
,
but we will simply call them the compact models
and denote the category of these models by

\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

For this to make sense,
we must show that the category
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

has all filtered colimits.
We show this for an arbitrary (Grothendieck) topos
\mathcal{E}
instead,
to illustrate how well-behaved models of geometric theories are
with respect to filtered colimits.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a geometric theory and
\mathcal{E}
be a (Grothendieck) topos.
Then the category
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}

admits all filtered colimits,
and for every geometric formula-in-context
\phi
,
the interpretation functor

{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}\to\mathcal{E},\quad M\mapsto\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}

preserves filtered colimits.

Proof.

Let
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
be a filtered diagram in
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
.
We start with the case where
\mathbb{T}
is the empty theory
over some signature
\Sigma
,
meaning that
\mathbb{T}
-models are just
\Sigma
-structures.
For every sort
A
of
\Sigma
,
set
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M}\vcentcolon=\colim_{i\in I}\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M_{i}}
.
For every function symbol
f:A_{1}\times\dots\times A_{n}\to B
,
we have

\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}=\colim_{i\in I}(\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M_{i}}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M_{i}})

since filtered colimits commute with finite limits in
\mathcal{E}
,
and the
\llbracket f\rrbracket_{M_{i}}
induce an arrow

\llbracket f\rrbracket_{M}:\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}\to\llbracket B\rrbracket_{M}
.
Similarly,
the interpretations
\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M_{i}}\hookrightarrow\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M_{i}}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M_{i}}

of a relation symbol
induce an arrow

\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M}\vcentcolon=\colim_{i\in I}\llbracket R\rrbracket_{M_{i}}\hookrightarrow\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}
,
which is a monomorphism
because filtered colimits commute with pullbacks in
\mathcal{E}
.
In this way,
the canonical maps
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M_{i}}\to\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M}

obviously constitute
\Sigma
-structure homomorphisms.
And for a cocone of
\Sigma
-structure homomorphisms
M_{i}\to\widetilde{M}
,
the induced maps
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M}\to\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\widetilde{M}}

respect the interpretations of function and relation symbols too.

Now let
\phi
be a geometric formula (over
\Sigma
)
in the context
x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:A_{n}
.
Then we can prove by induction on the structure of
\phi

that
\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}=\colim_{i\in I}\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M_{i}}

as subobjects of

\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}=\colim_{i\in I}(\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M_{i}}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M_{i}})

(using for example that filtered colimits
commute with image factorizations).
This shows in particular
that any axiom
\phi\;\vdash\;\psi

satisfied by all
M_{i}

is also satisfied by
M
,
so
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathcal{E})}
is closed under filtered colimits
in the category of
\Sigma
-structures in
\mathcal{E}
.
∎

Let us also prove
the following lemma on compact objects,
which will be a convenient basic tool
for drawing conclusions about compact models
in some situations.

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Let
F\dashv G
be adjoint functors

F:C\to D
,
G:D\to C

between categories admitting filtered colimits.
If
G
preserves filtered colimits,
then
F
preserves compact objects.

Proof.

Let
X\in C
be compact
and let
Y_{i}
be a filtered diagram in
D
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\Hom(F(X),\colim_{i}Y_{i})

\displaystyle=\Hom(X,\colim_{i}G(Y_{i}))

\displaystyle=\colim_{i}\Hom(X,G(Y_{i}))

\displaystyle=\colim_{i}\Hom(F(X),Y_{i})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}\qed

Now we are ready to understand the usefulness
of theories of presheaf type.
It consists in the fact that
if a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
is of presheaf type,
then
\mathbb{T}
is classified by the topos

\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]=\PSh({\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}})=[\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c},\mathrm{Set}]\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

That is,
if there is any presheaf site for
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]
,
then the canonically given category

{\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}

is also a presheaf site of definition for
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]
.
We note that the category
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}

is essentially small,
so that it can actually be used as a site.
There is also a simple description
of the universal
\mathbb{T}
-model
M
in
[\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c},\mathrm{Set}]
,
namely,

M
is the
“tautological”
model,
interpreting a sort
A

by the functor

\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}\to\mathrm{Set},\quad M^{\prime}\mapsto\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M^{\prime}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and similarly for relation and function symbols

[
5
, Theorem 6.1.1]
.

For a theory of presheaf type
\mathbb{T}
,
the category
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})
is finitely accessible.
This can be a useful criterion
for showing that a theory is not of presheaf type.

The basic source of theories of presheaf type
is the following.
Recall that a geometric theory is a
Horn
theory,
if all of its axioms are of the form

\phi\;\vdash_{\Gamma}\;\psi

with
\phi
and
\psi
finite conjunctions of atomic formulas.
In particular,
algebraic theories are Horn theories.
Recall also that in a
cartesian
theory
the formulas
\phi
and
\psi
can
in addition to finite conjunctions
also contain existential quantifiers,
but only if these refer to unique existence,
provably relative to all preceding axioms
in some chosen well-ordering.
Then, all Horn theories
and more generally all cartesian theories
are of presheaf type

[
5
, Theorem 2.1.8]
.
For Horn theories,
there is even a good general description
of the compact models.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a Horn theory.
Then a model
M
of
\mathbb{T}
is compact
if and only if
it is presented by some Horn formula
(finite conjunction of atomic formulas)
in context
\vec{x}.\,\phi
,
meaning that there is a natural isomorphism

\Hom(M,-)\cong\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{-}\;:\;\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\to\mathrm{Set}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Furthermore,
every Horn formula in context
presents some model.

Proof.

See
[
4
, Lemma 4.3]
.
∎

In other words,
the compact models of a Horn theory
are the ones presented by
finitely many generators
(with specified sorts)
and finitely many relations
in the form of atomic formulas.

For understanding the notion of rigid-topology quotients,
we should recall the Comparison Lemma,
which allows to eliminate some of the objects
of a site under certain conditions.
If
(C,J)
is a site
and
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
is any full subcategory,
then we can restrict the Grothendieck topology
J
to
C^{\prime}
,
letting a sieve
S
on an object
c\in C^{\prime}

be covering for
J|_{C^{\prime}}

if and only if the sieve generated by the arrows of
S

in the bigger category
C

is covering for
J
.
In other words,

J|_{C^{\prime}}
is the biggest topology on
C^{\prime}

such that the inclusion
C^{\prime}\hookrightarrow C

preserves covers.
A
dense
full subcategory
C^{\prime}\subseteq C

of a site
(C,J)

is one such that every object of
C

can be covered by (a sieve generated by arrows from)
objects in
C^{\prime}
.

Theorem 4.2.5

(Comparison Lemma)
.

If
C^{\prime}
is a dense full subcategory
of a site
(C,J)
,
then restricting sheaves on
C

to
C^{\prime}
is an equivalence of categories

\Sh(C^{\prime},J|_{C^{\prime}})\simeq\Sh(C).

Proof.

See
[
7
, Theorem C2.2.3]
.
∎

Now suppose we want to add some axioms
to a theory of presheaf type
and would like to show that the resulting theory
is again of presheaf type.
By the duality between
quotient theories and subtoposes,
and the fact that the latter
correspond to Grothendieck topologies
on any given site of definition,
a quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}
of a theory of presheaf type
\mathbb{T}

induces a topology on
{\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
which we denote
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
If there is a dense full subcategory
of
{\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}

with respect to
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
,
such that
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
becomes the trivial topology
when restricted to this subcategory,
then by the Comparison Lemma,

\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
is again of presheaf type.
This condition can equivalently be expressed by saying that
the
irreducible
objects of
{\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
which admit no covering sieve except the maximal sieve,
are dense.
A topology with this property is called a
rigid
topology.

Definition 4.2.6
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a theory of presheaf type.
We say that a quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}
of
\mathbb{T}

is a
rigid-topology quotient

if the Grothendieck topology
J_{\mathbb{Q}}

on
{{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}

is rigid.

Note that for a quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}

of an arbitrary geometric theory
\mathbb{T}
,
we would have to fix a site of definition for
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]

before we can ask if the induced topology is rigid.

After proving that some
\mathbb{Q}

is a rigid-topology quotient,
we would probably like to have a description of
the compact models of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
.
From Lemma
4.2.2

it follows that
the full subcategory

(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\subseteq\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

is closed under filtered colimits.
With this,
one easily sees that a
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q})
-model
which is compact as a
\mathbb{T}
-model
is also compact as a
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q})
-model
(even without any presheaf type conditions).
The converse is part of the following result,
which simultaneously answers the question
whether
\mathbb{Q}
being a rigid-topology quotient
is necessary for
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
to be again of presheaf type.

Lemma 4.2.7
.

A quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}

of a theory of presheaf type
\mathbb{T}

is a rigid-topology quotient
if and only if

\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
is again of presheaf type
and additionally
every compact
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q})
-model
is also compact as a
\mathbb{T}
-model.

Proof.

See
[
5
, Theorem 8.2.6]
.
∎

So for example,
take as
\mathbb{T}
the theory consisting of a sort
A

and countably many constant symbols
c_{n}\,{:}\,A
,
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be the axiom

\top\;\vdash_{x,y\,{:}\,A}\;x=y\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Then both
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
are algebraic theories
and thus of presheaf type.
But in the unique
\mathrm{Set}
-based model of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
,
the constants
c_{n}
are all identified,
which can not happen in a compact model of
\mathbb{T}
,
by Lemma
4.2.4
.
So
\mathbb{Q}
is not a rigid-topology quotient.

4.3
Rigid-topology quotients

Let
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}
be a compact model
of a theory of presheaf type
and let

\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}}\;\psi

be an axiom that we would like to add to
\mathbb{T}
.
This axiom is satisfied in the model
M

if and only if we have

\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}\subseteq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{M}
.
But in any case,
given some
\vec{x}\in\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}
,
we can consider the collection of all arrows

f:M\to M^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}

that map
\vec{x}
into
\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{M^{\prime}}
,
which turns out to be a cosieve on
M
,
since any further model homomorphism
M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}

preserves truth of the geometric formula
\psi
.
We now show that these sieves
in
{\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}

generate the Grothendieck topology
corresponding to the axiom

\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}}\;\psi
.

Theorem 4.3.1
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a theory of presheaf type
and let
\mathbb{Q}=\{\,\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}}\;\psi\,\}

be a quotient extension of
\mathbb{T}

adding a single axiom.
Then the Grothendieck topology
J_{\mathbb{Q}}

on
{{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}

is generated by the sieves
(cosieves in
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}
)

S_{\vec{x}}=S_{M,\vec{x},\psi}\vcentcolon=\{\,f:M\to M^{\prime}\mid f(\vec{x})\in\llbracket\psi\rrbracket_{M^{\prime}}\,\}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}

and
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}\subseteq\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}
.

Proof.

The axiom
\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}\,{:}\,\vec{A}}\;\psi

is satisfied in a model
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathcal{E})

(in any topos)
if and only if
the inclusion

\llbracket\psi\land\phi\rrbracket_{M}\hookrightarrow\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}

of subobjects of

\llbracket A_{1}\rrbracket_{M}\times\dots\times\llbracket A_{n}\rrbracket_{M}

is an isomorphism.
For the universal
\mathbb{T}
-model in
[\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c},\mathrm{Set}]
,
this is the inclusion

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\psi\land\phi\rrbracket_{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{-}}

of functors
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}\to\mathrm{Set}
.
The classifying topos of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}

is therefore the greatest subtopos
(corresponding to the Grothendieck topology
with the fewest covering sieves)
with the property that
this inclusion is a
local isomorphism
,
that is, it becomes an isomorphism when sheafified.

Now, let
M\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}

and let
\vec{x}\in\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}
be given,
corresponding to an arrow

\Hom(M,-)\to\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{-}
.
Then the cosieve
S_{\vec{x}}
on
M
,
regarded as a subobject of
\Hom(M,-)

in
[\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c},\mathrm{Set}]
,
is the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\psi\land\phi\rrbracket_{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\vec{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(M,-)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}

Thus, if the top row is a local isomorphism,
then the bottom row
is a local isomorphism
for all
M
and
\vec{x}
.
But the converse is also true,
since any local section

\widetilde{x}\in a(\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{-})(M)

of the sheafification of
\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{-}

is locally given by some
\vec{x}\in\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M^{\prime}}
,
which then locally lies in
\llbracket\psi\land\phi\rrbracket_{-}
.
From this, the statement follows,
because the covering sieves of a Grothendieck topology
are precisely those subobjects of representable presheaves
which are local isomorphisms.
∎

Remark 4.3.2
.

Theorem
4.3.1

is closely related to
[
5
, Theorem 8.1.10]
,
which describes the topology
J_{\mathbb{Q}}

by only one generating sieve per axiom,
assuming that the axioms are given in a certain form,
basically consisting of formulas that present compact models.
While this yields much more concise descriptions of the topology,
it seems harder to apply to concrete theories,
as it might be nontrivial to determine
whether a formula presents a model
(this property is characterized in

[
5
, Theorem 6.1.13]

as being an irreducible object of the syntactic site).
Also,
for our main purpose of recognizing the topology
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
as rigid,
the big number of covering sieves
S_{\vec{x}}

in Theorem
4.3.1

will actually be quite convenient
and can therefore be seen as a feature in this context.

If the quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}
contains more than one axiom,
the topology
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
is of course generated by
the union of all the sets of generators for the individual axioms.
(So there is one generating cosieve
for every axiom, compact model and
appropriate family of elements of the model.)
It should be noted that
the collection of cosieves of the form
S_{\vec{x}}

satisfies the pullback-stability axiom of a Grothendieck topology
(in the terminology of
[
7
, C2.1]
,
it is a (sifted) coverage).
Indeed, if
g:M\to M^{\prime}
is a homomorphism between compact models
then the push-forward of a cosieve
S_{\vec{x}}
on
M

for a family of elements
\vec{x}\in\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}

is simply the cosieve
S_{g(\vec{x})}
on
M^{\prime}
,
and we do have
g(\vec{x})\in\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M^{\prime}}
.

As a first application of Theorem
4.3.1
,
we can give a short proof of the following result.

Corollary 4.3.3

(see
[
5
, Theorem 8.2.5]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a theory of presheaf type
and
\mathbb{Q}
a quotient extension of
\mathbb{T}
.
Then a compact model of
\mathbb{T}

is
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
-irreducible
as an object of
{{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}

if and only if
it satisfies the axioms of
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

A compact model
M
of
\mathbb{T}

satisfies an axiom
\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:\vec{A}}\;\psi

if and only if
for every family of elements
\vec{x}\in\llbracket\phi\rrbracket_{M}
,
the cosieve
S_{\vec{x}}

from Theorem
4.3.1

is the maximal cosieve on
M
.
This shows the
“only if”
direction.
For the
“if”
direction,
one can check that saturating a collection of sieves
with respect to the transitivity condition of Grothendieck topologies
preserves the pullback-stability of the collection and
can never produce any nontrivial covers of an object
if there were none before.
∎

Remark 4.3.4
.

We can understand the cosieves
S_{\vec{x}}

from Theorem
4.3.1

as
operations
correcting the failure of a single instance
of one of the axioms of
\mathbb{Q}
.
Showing that
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
is rigid
then amounts to
providing an algorithm that turns any compact
\mathbb{T}
-model
into a
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q})
-model using these operations,
as follows.
Starting with a compact
\mathbb{T}
-model
M

which is not a model of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
,
we have to pick an axiom
\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:\vec{A}}\;\psi

of
\mathbb{Q}

and a family
\vec{x}
of elements of (the appropriate sorts of)
M

with
\phi(\vec{x})
but not
\psi(\vec{x})
.
Then, if we are lucky,
the cosieve
S_{\vec{x}}
is generated by a single arrow

M\to M^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

meaning that we have reduced
the problem of covering
M
by irreducibles
to covering
M^{\prime}
by irreducibles
by the transitivity property of the Grothendieck topology
J_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
If we can repeat this procedure a finite number of times
and arrive at a model that satisfies all axioms in
\mathbb{Q}
,
then we are done;
we have covered
M
by a single irreducible object.

It can of course happen that
S_{\vec{x}}
is not a principal cosieve,
so that we need multiple arrows

M\to M^{\prime}_{i}

to generate it.
Then
S_{\vec{x}}
should perhaps be seen as
a nondeterministic operation,
that will turn
M
into one of the
M^{\prime}_{i}
,
but we don’t know in advance which one it will be.
In this case,
we have to deal with all
M^{\prime}_{i}
,
and our algorithm must terminate in the sense that
any possible path of execution is finite.
(On the other hand,
we can also be even
more
lucky,
in that
S_{\vec{x}}
is the empty cosieve on
M
.
Then we are immediately done with the current branch.)

Here is a first illustration of this strategy.

Example 4.3.5
.

Start with the theory
\mathbb{T}

with two sorts
and a function symbol
A\to B
,
and add to it the axiom

\top\;\vdash_{y\,{:}\,B}\;\exists x{\,:\,}A.\;f(x)=y\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

requiring
f
to be surjective.
We already know that the resulting theory is of presheaf type,
since it is Morita equivalent to the theory
with just one sort
A

and an equivalence relation
{\sim}\subseteq A\times A

by Example
2.4.2
,
and this is a Horn theory.
But let us show that the surjectivity axiom
is a rigid-topology quotient of
\mathbb{T}
.

By Lemma
4.2.4
,
a model
M=(A\to B)\in\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

is compact if and only if
both
A
and
B
are finite sets.
Given such a compact model
(A\to B)

and an element
y\in B
,
the cosieve
S_{y}
on
(A\to B)

from Theorem
4.3.1

is generated by the single arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\to B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\sqcup\{x\}\to B)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

where
x
is sent to
y
in the new model
(which is clearly still compact),
since every model homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

with
\exists x\in A^{\prime}.\;f^{\prime}(x)=g_{B}(y)

factors through
(A\sqcup\{x\}\to B)
.
The model
(A\to B)
can thus be covered
(in the topology induced by the surjectivity axiom)
by the model
(A\sqcup\{x\}\to B)
.
We can go on by creating preimages of other elements of
B
,
until we have covered
(A\to B)
by a model
satisfying the new axiom
(since
B
is finite).

Lemma 4.3.6
.

If
\mathbb{T}
is of presheaf type,

\mathbb{Q}_{1}
is a rigid-topology quotient of
\mathbb{T}

and
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is a rigid-topology quotient of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1}
,
then
\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is a rigid-topology quotient of
\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

This follows easily from
Lemma
4.2.7
:

\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is of presheaf type
and every compact
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2})
-model
is compact as a
(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1})
-model
and hence as a
\mathbb{T}
-model.

Note that arguing more directly
by the transitivity of
J_{\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2}}

is not so simple,
as we would still have to relate the topology induced by
\mathbb{Q}_{2}

on
{(\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}

with that on
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}
.
∎

While we can assume,
after choosing a representing set of axioms
for the quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
,
that its base theory is
\mathbb{T}
instead of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1}
,
the assumptions of Lemma
4.3.6

are not at all the same as
requiring
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{2}

to be rigid-topology quotients of
\mathbb{T}
independently.
In fact,
\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2}
can fail
to be a rigid-topology quotient of
\mathbb{T}

in this case,
as we will see in Example

4.5.5
.
By the same method as there,
one can easily construct quotient extensions
\mathbb{Q}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{Q}_{n}

such that the sum of any proper subset
induces a rigid topology,
but adding all
n
even destroys presheaf type.

4.4
Syntactic constructions preserving presheaf type

We now investigate
in which ways a theory of presheaf type can be extended
such that the resulting theory is again of presheaf type.
At first sight,
one might hope for a result like

“an algebraic (or Horn, or even cartesian) extension
preserves presheaf type”
,
but unfortunately,
this is far too optimistic.
Indeed,
if we have any geometric axiom

\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:\vec{A}}\;\psi

we would like to add to a theory,
we can instead first perform an extension by definitions
(which surely preserves presheaf type),
defining
R_{\phi},R_{\psi}\subseteq\vec{A}

by
\phi
and
\psi
respectively,
and then add the Horn axiom

R_{\phi}\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:\vec{A}}\;R_{\psi}
.
Example
4.5.4
shows that
also an algebraic axiom can destroy presheaf type.

Corollary 4.4.1

(see
[
5
, Theorem 8.2.8]
)
.

Adding arbitrarily many axioms of the form

\phi\;\vdash_{\vec{x}:\vec{A}}\;\bot

to a theory of presheaf type
is always a rigid-topology quotient.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a theory of presheaf type,

\mathbb{Q}=\{\,\phi^{i}\;\vdash_{\vec{x}^{i}:\vec{A}^{i}}\;\bot\mid i\in I\,\}

and
M\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}
.
If there is some
i\in I

and
\vec{x}\in\llbracket\phi^{i}\rrbracket_{M}
,
then

S_{\vec{x}}=\{\,f:M\to M^{\prime}\mid f(\vec{x})\in\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{M^{\prime}}=\varnothing\,\}

is the empty cosieve on
M

and we are done.
But otherwise

M
was already a model of
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}
,
i.e. irreducible itself.
∎

Corollary 4.4.2
.

Adding finitely many axioms

\phi\;\vdash_{[]}\;\psi

with empty context
to a theory of presheaf type
is always a rigid-topology quotient.

Proof.

For one axiom
\phi\;\vdash_{[]}\;\psi
with empty context,
Theorem
4.3.1

says that any compact model
M

that satisfies the closed formula
\phi

is covered by all arrows
(necessarily preserving truth of
\phi
)
to compact models that also satisfy
\psi
,
as required.
And any model that does not satisfy
\phi

satisfies the new axiom anyway.
For more than one axiom of this form,
we can use Lemma
4.3.6
.
∎

Corollary 4.4.3
.

Any propositional theory
with finitely many axioms
is of presheaf type.

Proof.

This immediately follows from
Corollary
4.4.2
,
as a propositional theory
has no other contexts than the empty one.
∎

Remark 4.4.4
.

Corollary
4.4.2

becomes false if we omit the word
“finitely”
.
Indeed, it would otherwise imply that
all propositional theories are of presheaf type
(see Example
4.5.3

for one which is not).
In particular,
Lemma
4.3.6

becomes wrong if instead of two consecutive rigid-topology quotients
we consider the sum
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Q}_{i}

of an infinite sequence of quotient extensions
where every
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
is a rigid-topology quotient
of
\mathbb{T}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathbb{Q}_{i}
.

Proposition 4.4.5
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a theory of presheaf type
and
A
a sort of
\mathbb{T}
.
Then the theory
\mathbb{T}+(c:A)

obtained from
\mathbb{T}
by introducing a new constant symbol
c:A

is also of presheaf type.
Moreover,
a model of
\mathbb{T}+(c:A)
in
\mathrm{Set}

is compact if and only if
the underlying
\mathbb{T}
-model is compact.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]
be a classifying topos for
\mathbb{T}

with universal model
M_{\mathbb{T}}
.
Then
\mathbb{T}+(c:A)
is classified by
the slice topos
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]/\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M_{\mathbb{T}}}
,
see
[
8
, Exercise X.3]
.
But if
\mathrm{Set}[\mathbb{T}]=\PSh(C)
is a presheaf topos,
then we can use the formula

[
8
, Exercise III.8]

\PSh(C)/X\simeq\PSh(\int_{C}X)

for
X=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M_{\mathbb{T}}}

to see that
\mathbb{T}+(c:A)
is of presheaf type.

For the compact models,
we observe that
(\mathbb{T}+(c:A))\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})
is equivalent,
as a category over
\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})
,
to the category

\int^{\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}\llbracket A\rrbracket_{-}

of elements of the interpretation functor

\llbracket A\rrbracket_{-}:\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\to\mathrm{Set}
.
This functor preserves filtered colimits
by Lemma
4.2.2
,
so Lemma
4.4.6
below
tells us exactly what we need.
∎

Lemma 4.4.6
.

Let
C
be a category with filtered colimits
and let
F:C\to\mathrm{Set}
be a functor preserving filtered colimits.
Then the category of elements of
F
,
\int^{C}F
,
has filtered colimits too,
and the projection functor
U:\int^{C}F\to C

preserves and reflects compact objects.

Proof.

Let
(c_{i},x_{i})_{i\in I}
,
where
x_{i}\in F(c_{i})
,
be a filtered diagram in
\int^{C}F
.
It is clear that the
x_{i}
all represent the same element
in
\colim_{i}F(c_{i})\cong F(\colim_{i}c_{i})
,
let
x^{*}\in F(\colim_{i}c_{i})
be this element.
Then we have a cocone on
(c_{i},x_{i})_{i\in I}

with apex
(\colim_{i}c_{i},x^{*})
,
and it is easy to check that this is the colimit in
\int^{C}F
.
This also shows that
the functor
U:\int^{C}F\to C

preserves and reflects filtered colimits.

Let
(c,x)
be a compact object of
\int^{C}F
,
we want to show that
c
is compact in
C
.
So let
(c_{i})_{i\in I}
be a filtered diagram in
C

and fix a morphism

f:c\to\colim_{i\in I}c_{i}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

then we have to show that there is exactly one

[g]\in\colim_{i}\Hom(c,c_{i})
that induces
f
.
If we want to use the compactness of
(c,x)
,
we need to construct a filtered diagram in
\int^{C}F
.
Consider the element
f_{*}x\in F(\colim_{i}c_{i})\cong\colim_{i}F(c_{i})

and pick a representative
x_{i_{0}}\in F(c_{i_{o}})
for it.
Since
I
is filtered,
the comma category
(i_{0}\downarrow I)
is also filtered
and the functor
(i_{0}\downarrow I)\to I
is final.
This means that we have

\colim_{i}c_{i}=\colim_{i_{0}\to i}c_{i}

and
\colim_{i}\Hom(c,c_{i})=\colim_{i_{0}\to i}\Hom(c,c_{i})
,
so we can replace the diagram
(c_{i})_{i\in I}

with
(c_{i})_{(i_{0}\to i)\in(i_{0}\downarrow I)}

and assume without loss of generality
that
i_{0}
is initial in
I
.
Then setting
x_{i}\vcentcolon=({!}:i_{0}\to i)_{*}x_{i_{0}}\in F(c_{i})

yields a filtered diagram

(c_{i},x_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\int^{C}F
.
We also have

\widetilde{f}:(c,x)\to\colim_{i\in I}{(c_{i},x_{i})}

with
U(\widetilde{f})=f

by our original choice of
x_{i_{0}}
.
So by assumption,
there is a
\widetilde{g}:(c,x)\to(c_{i},x_{i})

for some
i

that induces
\widetilde{f}
.
Then
g\vcentcolon=U(\widetilde{g})
induces
f
.
On the other hand,
if
g_{1}:c\to c_{i_{1}}
and
g_{2}:c\to c_{i_{2}}

both induce
f
,
then we have
[{g_{1}}_{*}x]=[{g_{2}}_{*}x]=[x_{i_{0}}]\in\colim_{i}F(c_{i})
,
in particular
[{g_{1}}_{*}x]=[x_{i_{1}}]
and
[{g_{2}}_{*}x]=[x_{i_{2}}]
,
so we can prolong
g_{1}

to some
g^{\prime}_{1}:c\to c_{i^{\prime}_{1}}

such that
{g^{\prime}_{1}}_{*}x=x_{i^{\prime}_{1}}
,
and similarly for
g_{2}
,
meaning that we have

\widetilde{g}_{1}:(c,x)\to(c_{i^{\prime}_{1}})
and

\widetilde{g}_{2}:(c,x)\to(c_{i^{\prime}_{2}})

that both induce
\widetilde{f}
.
Then we can conclude

[\widetilde{g}_{1}]=[\widetilde{g}_{1}]\in\colim_{i}\Hom((c,x),(c_{i},x_{i}))
,
and from this it follows that

[g_{1}]=[g^{\prime}_{1}]=[g^{\prime}_{2}]=[g_{2}]\in\colim_{i}\Hom(c,c_{i})
.

Now let
c\in C
be compact
and let
x\in F(c)
,
we want to show that
(c,x)
is compact in
\int^{C}F
.
So let
(c_{i},x_{i})_{i\in I}
be a filtered diagram in
\int^{C}F

and let

\widetilde{f}:(c,x)\to\colim_{i\in I}(c_{i},x_{i})=(\colim_{i\in I}c_{i},x^{*})

be given.
That is, we have
f:c\to\colim_{i}c_{i}

with
f_{*}x=x^{*}
.
This
f
is induced by some
g:c\to c_{i}
,
implying that
[g_{*}x]=x^{*}\in\colim_{i}F(c_{i})
,
so it is also induced by some

g^{\prime}:c\to c_{i^{\prime}}
with
g^{\prime}_{*}x=x_{i^{\prime}}
.
This means that we have

\widetilde{g}:(c,x)\to(c_{i^{\prime}},x_{i^{\prime}})

inducing
\widetilde{f}
.
And for
\widetilde{g}_{1}
,
\widetilde{g}_{2}

both inducing
\widetilde{f}
,
we immediately see that

[U(\widetilde{g}_{1})]=[U(\widetilde{g}_{2})]\in\colim_{i}\Hom(c,c_{i})

and therefore

[\widetilde{g}_{1}]=[\widetilde{g}_{2}]\in\colim_{i}\Hom((c,x),(c_{i},x_{i}))
.
∎

Example 4.4.7
.

From Example
2.4.4

we know that the theory
\underline{A}
,
where we have just imported a set
A
,
is Morita equivalent to the empty theory
(over the empty signature).
In particular, it is of presheaf type
and we can apply Proposition
4.4.5

to conclude that the theory
c:\underline{A}
,
where we have added one more constant symbol
is also of presheaf type.
It is classified by
\mathrm{Set}/A
.

It is also Morita equivalent to a disjoint disjunction
of an
A
-indexed family of proposition symbols,

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bigvee_{a\in A}p_{a}\qquad\text{and}\qquad p_{a}\land p_{a^{\prime}}\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot\quad(\text{for $a\neq a^{\prime}\in A$})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

by identifying
p_{a}
with
c=c_{a}
.
This way,
we can easily see that the theory is of presheaf type
using Corollary
4.4.2

for the first axiom
and Corollary
4.4.1

for the
A
-indexed family of negated axioms.

Example 4.4.8
.

Consider the theory of a surjective function

f:A\twoheadrightarrow B
,
which is of presheaf type by
Example
2.4.2

or Example
4.3.5
.
By Proposition
4.4.5
,
it is still of presheaf type
after adding a constant symbol
c:B
.

When we translate this extension
to the theory of an equivalence relation

{\sim}\subseteq A\times A
,
we obtain a relation symbol
R\subseteq A
with the axioms

R(x)\land x\sim x^{\prime}\;\vdash_{x,x^{\prime}\,{:}\,A}\;R(x^{\prime})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}\qquad R(x)\land R(x^{\prime})\;\vdash_{x,x^{\prime}\,{:}\,A}\;x\sim x^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}\qquad\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\exists x{\,:\,}A.\;R(x)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The existential quantifier in the last axiom
is not cartesian,
but the context of the axiom is empty
(and all other axioms of the theory are Horn),
so we again find that the theory is of presheaf type
using Corollary
4.4.2
.
However,
if the sort
A
and the equivalence relation
\sim

are instead given as part of a theory of presheaf type
which is not cartesian,
then the Horn axioms on
R
can not be argued away so easily,
but we can still introduce the quotient
B=A/{\sim}

and add a constant symbol
c:B

without destroying presheaf type.

4.5
Some counterexamples

We now give a number of examples
of non-presheaf-type theories,
to show that the above positive results
are optimal in certain aspects
and to illustrate the various techniques
useful for recognizing such theories.
Our first example shows that
even a very simple, finite theory
can fail to be of presheaf type.

Example 4.5.1
.

Consider the theory

\mathbb{T}=\left\{\hbox to51.56pt{\vbox to18.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.05554pt\lower-10.40498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.66414pt}{-2.66666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

consisting of one sort
A
,
a unary function symbol
f:A\to A

and one axiom

\top\;\vdash_{y:A}\;\exists x{\,:\,}A.\;f(x)=y

stating that
f
is surjective.
We show that
{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
is not finitely accessible,
implying that
\mathbb{T}
is not of presheaf type.

Specifically,
we show that the model
(\mathbb{Z},+1)
,
where
A=\mathbb{Z}
and
f(n)=n+1
,
is not a filtered colimit of compact models.
Consider the models

M_{n}=\Big(\,\{\,(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mid y\leq\min(n,-x)\,\}\,,\,(x,y)\mapsto(x+1,\min(y,-x-1))\,\Big)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which can be pictured like this.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\dots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\dots}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\smash{\ddots}}}

The inclusions
M_{n}\hookrightarrow M_{n+1}

(adding another row on top)
are model homomorphisms,
and we have a homomorphism

(\mathbb{Z},+1)\to M_{\omega}\vcentcolon=\colim_{n}M_{n},\quad k\mapsto(k,-k)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

that does not factor through any
M_{n}
.
This shows that
(\mathbb{Z},+1)
is not compact.
But any map
M\to(\mathbb{Z},+1)
from a nonempty model
M

is necessarily surjective,
so the composite
M\to(\mathbb{Z},+1)\to M_{\omega}

does not factor through any
M_{n}
either,
showing that
(\mathbb{Z},+1)
is not a colimit of compact models.

Another method
to show that a theory has
“too few compact models”

to be of presheaf type
is illustrated
by the next example.
In it,
we see that adding countably many constant symbols of the same sort
can destroy presheaf type
(in contrast to Proposition
4.4.5
).
An even more drastic version of this statement
will be one of the conclusions of
Example
4.5.3
.

Example 4.5.2
.

Start with the theory of a surjective function

f:A\twoheadrightarrow B

as in Example
4.4.8
,
but then add countably many constant symbols

c_{0},c_{1},\ldots:B

instead of just a single one.
We show that this theory
\mathbb{T}
is not of presheaf type
by investigating its compact models.

A\twoheadrightarrow B\ni c_{0},c_{1},\dots

Let
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
be the theory of inhabited sets,
with one sort
A
and the axiom

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;(\exists x{\,:\,}A.\;\top)
.
Then we can regard
\mathbb{T}
as an extension of
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}

and the forgetful functor

U:\mathbb{T}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\to\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set}),\quad(A\twoheadrightarrow B)\mapsto A

has a right adjoint

A^{\prime}\mapsto(A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\{*\})
,
which preserves filtered colimits
by their description in
Lemma
4.2.2
.
Thus,
U
preserves compact objects by
Lemma
4.2.3
.
But the compact objects of
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

are the finite nonempty sets,
so, in summary,
for any compact model
(A\twoheadrightarrow B)
of
\mathbb{T}
,

A
is a finite set.
This means that
B
is finite too,
from which we can in particular conclude that
the geometric formula

\phi\vcentcolon=\quad\bigvee_{i\neq j}(c_{i}=c_{j})

is satisfied in all compact models.
If
\mathbb{T}
was of presheaf type,
this would mean that
\phi
is satisfied
in the universal model
and therefore provable in
\mathbb{T}
.
This is not the case,
as witnessed by the model
(\mathbb{N}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{N})

with
c_{n}=n
.

The following example will allow us
to draw several conclusions.
The technique used here is
to compare a theory to
another theory with the same
\mathrm{Set}
-based models
which is known to be of presheaf type.

Example 4.5.3
.

Given two sets
A
and
B
,
there are (at least) two different theories

“of a map from
A
to
B
”
.
One is

\langle f:\underline{B^{A}}\rangle\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where we import
(see Example
2.4.4
)
the set
B^{A}
of functions from
A
to
B

and add a constant symbol
f
of this sort;
it is of presheaf type
as in Example
4.4.7
.
The other is

\langle f:\underline{A}\to\underline{B}\rangle\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where we import
A
and
B
separately
and add a unary function symbol as indicated.
For both theories,
the category of models in
\mathrm{Set}

is (up to equivalence)
the discrete category with
B^{A}
objects.
So if
\langle f:\underline{A}\to\underline{B}\rangle

was of presheaf type too,
then this would imply that the two theories are Morita-equivalent.

Specialize to the case
A=\mathbb{N}
and
B=2=\{0,1\}
.
Then
\langle f:\underline{2^{\mathbb{N}}}\rangle
is classified by
\mathrm{Set}/2^{\mathbb{N}}
,
or equivalently, by
\Sh(X)
,
where
X
is the discrete space with
2^{\mathbb{N}}
points.
But by
[
8
, Exercise VIII.10]
,

\langle f:\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\underline{2}\rangle

is classified by
\Sh(2^{\mathbb{N}})
,
where
2^{\mathbb{N}}
is the Cantor space.
Since these two sober topological spaces are not homeomorphic,
