\langle f:\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\underline{2}\rangle

is not of presheaf type.

Another way to see that
\langle f:\underline{2^{\mathbb{N}}}\rangle

and
\langle f:\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\underline{2}\rangle

are not Morita equivalent
is as follows.
First, as in Example
4.4.7
,
a constant symbol in an imported set,
such as
f:\underline{2^{\mathbb{N}}}
,
can be replaced by a disjoint disjunction of
2^{\mathbb{N}}

proposition symbols.
On the other hand,
the function symbol
f:\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\underline{2}

can be replaced by
countably many constant symbols
c_{n}:\underline{2}

and therefore by countably many decidable propositions
(disjoint binary disjunctions).
In this way,
one sees that the models of both theories
in any topos
\mathcal{E}
form discrete categories,
namely,

\langle f:\underline{2^{\mathbb{N}}}\rangle
classifies
decompositions of
\mathcal{E}
into
2^{\mathbb{N}}
clopen subtoposes,
while
\langle f:\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\underline{2}\rangle

classifies countable families of compositions
into two clopen subtoposes.
Now,
we can consider the topological spaces

X_{n}\vcentcolon=\{0,\dots,n\}

(with the discrete topology)
and
X_{\omega}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
the one-point compactification of
\mathbb{N}
,
and the continuous maps

X_{\omega}\to X_{n},\quad x\mapsto\min(x,n)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Then we see that every model of
\langle f:\underline{2^{\mathbb{N}}}\rangle

in
X_{\omega}

is (isomorphic to)
the pullback of a model in some
X_{n}
,
because any open set of
X_{\omega}
containing
\infty
is cofinite,
but this is not the case for

\langle f:\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\underline{2}\rangle
,
because we can choose a family of partitions like

X_{\omega}=\{0,\dots,n\}\sqcup\{n+1,\dots,\infty\}
.

In summary,
we can record the following.

(i)

Adding a single unary function symbol
(such as
f:\underline{\mathbb{N}}\to\underline{2}
)
to a theory of presheaf type
(even a theory that is classified by
\mathrm{Set}
)
can destroy presheaf type.

(ii)

Adding countably many constant symbols
(such as
c_{n}:\underline{2}
)
to a theory of presheaf type
(even a theory that is classified by
\mathrm{Set}
)
can destroy presheaf type.

(iii)

A propositional theory
(such as the theory of
a countable family of decidable propositions,
with
p_{n}\lor q_{n}
and
p_{n}\land q_{n}\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bot
)
can fail to be of presheaf type.
(There are of course many other examples of this.)

(iv)

Adding countably many closed geometric formulas
(such as
p_{n}\lor q_{n}
in the previous item,
where all other axioms were negated ones,
which can’t destroy presheaf type)
can destroy presheaf type.

Example 4.5.4
.

Let us show that adding an algebraic axiom
can destroy presheaf type.
We do this by showing that
adding an algebraic axiom is,
up to Morita equivalence,
at least as expressive as
adding an arbitrary family

\phi_{i}

of closed geometric formulas
as axioms.
We can then for example use
\phi_{n}=(p_{n}\lor q_{n})

to obtain the theory of countably many decidable propositions
from a theory with only negated axioms,
as in example
4.5.3
.

So let
\mathbb{T}
be a theory
with closed geometric formulas
\phi_{i}
,
i\in I
.
Import the set
I\sqcup\{*\}
.
We can define (by a geometric formula)
an equivalence relation
\sim

on
\underline{I\sqcup\{*\}}

such that
c_{i}\sim c_{*}\;\dashv\vdash_{[]}\;\phi_{i}
.
Introduce the quotient

\underline{I\sqcup\{*\}}\twoheadrightarrow A

by
\sim
.
These equivalence extensions allow us to formulate the algebraic axiom

\top\;\vdash_{x,y:A}\;x=y\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which is syntactically equivalent (as a quotient extension) to

\top\;\vdash_{x:\underline{I\sqcup\{*\}}}\;x\sim c_{*}
,
and therefore to the set of axioms

\{\,\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\phi_{i}\mid i\in I\,\}
.

Example 4.5.5
.

Here we give an example of
two rigid-topology quotients
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{2}

of the same theory of presheaf type
\mathbb{T}

such that
\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is not a rigid-topology quotient again
and
\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2}
is not even of presheaf type.
Start with the theory
\mathbb{T}

of a sequence of maps,
infinite to the left:

\dots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}A_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}A_{0}.

The compact
\mathrm{Set}
-models of
\mathbb{T}

are those with finitely many elements in total.
Consider the quotient extension
\mathbb{Q}_{1}

requiring every second map
to be surjective:

\top\;\vdash_{y:A_{n}}\;\exists x{\,:\,}A_{n+1}.\;f_{n}(x)=y\qquad\qquad\text{for $n$ even}.

The topology on
{{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}
induced by these axioms
is rigid:
Given
M=(\dots\to A_{1}\to A_{0})\in{\mathbb{T}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}

and an element
y\in A_{n}
not in the image of
f_{n}
,
the cosieve
S_{y}

is generated by the single model homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n+1}\sqcup\{x\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

where we have freely added a preimage
x
of
y
.
After finitely many steps,
we have covered
M

by a compact
\mathbb{T}
-model that satisfies
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
.

However,
denoting by
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
the quotient extension
requiring
f_{n}
to be surjective for
n
odd,
which is a rigid-topology quotient for the same reason,
the theory

\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{T}+\mathbb{Q}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{2}

is
not
of presheaf type.
To see this,
we show that
{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}

is not finitely accessible.
In fact,
we can show that
the only compact object of
{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}

is the empty model
(\dots\to\varnothing\to\varnothing)
,
by considering the following sequence of inclusions.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1,2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1,2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\mapsto 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\mapsto 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\mapsto 0}

The colimit of this diagram
is a model
M^{\omega}
with
A^{\omega}_{n}=\{0,\dots,n\}
.
If
M=(\dots\to A_{1}\to A_{0})
is any nonempty model,
then
A_{n}
is nonempty for all
n
,
so the model homomorphism
M\to M^{\omega}

sending any
x\in A_{n}

to
n\in A^{\omega}_{n}

does not factor through any of the models in the diagram,
showing that
M
is not compact.

Finally,
it seems appropriate to show that
the theory of local rings,
which played an important role in
Subsection
3.7

and will do so again in
Section
5
,
is not of presheaf type.
Equivalently,
this shows that the big Zariski topos
(\Spec\mathbb{Z})_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is not equivalent to a presheaf topos.

Proposition 4.5.6
.

The theory of local rings,

\mathrm{Ring}+(\mathrm{loc})

(see Definition
3.7.1
),
is not of presheaf type.

Proof.

The argument given here resembles
the one given in
[
5
, Section 9.4]

showing that the usual geometric theory of fields
is not of presheaf type.
Assume that the theory of local rings
is of presheaf type,
that is,
it is classified by
[\mathrm{locRing}_{c},\mathrm{Set}]

with the tautological local ring as universal model.
We aim to reveal a subterminal object of
[\mathrm{locRing}_{c},\mathrm{Set}]

which is not definable by a closed geometric formula.
Note that every local ring
A

is a
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-algebra for some prime number
p
,
since at most one prime number can be non-invertible
as an element of
A
.
This means that the join of
the subterminal objects

U_{p}\vcentcolon=\Hom(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)},-):\mathrm{locRing}_{c}\to\mathrm{Set}

is
\bigvee_{p}U_{p}=1
.
Now, if there were geometric formulas
\phi_{p}

such that
\llbracket\phi_{p}\rrbracket=U_{p}

(i.e.
\phi_{p}
expresses that
all prime numbers except
p
are invertible)
for all
p
,
then the disjunction
\bigvee_{p}\phi_{p}
,
being valid in the universal model,
would be provable in the theory of local rings.
But since this is a coherent theory,
a finite disjunction
\bigvee_{p\in P}\phi_{p}

with
P
a finite set of primes
would also have to be provable
by
[
5
, Theorem 10.8.6 (iii)]
.
This is not the case,
as
\bigvee_{p\in P}\phi_{p}

is falsified by the local ring
\mathbb{Z}_{(p^{\prime})}

for any
p^{\prime}\notin P
.
∎

5
Syntactic presentations for crystalline toposes

5.1
Introduction

In this section,
we determine a syntactic presentation
of the big crystalline toposes
used for studying crystalline cohomology in algebraic geometry.
This fulfills a promise made by Wraith
in 1979
in
[
12
, p. 743]
,
where he writes:

“It is my belief that
a great many of the toposes occurring in algebraic geometry
can be conveniently described in terms of the theories they classify.
This is certainly so in the case of étale and crystalline toposes,
for example.”

This optimistic statement is however not accompanied by any hint
of what the classified theory might be,
and no answer has been given since.

The reason why the question is not as simple
as for the big Zariski topos,
for which the answer has been known
since the infancy of the field of classifying toposes,
is that much more data is involved in its construction.
To wit,
the crystalline topos depends on two schemes,
a scheme morphism between them,
and a certain structure known as a divided powers structure,
or PD structure on one of them.

In the affine case,
a PD structure on a ring
A

of positive characteristic
p

assigns to an element
a\in A

another element of
A
,
acting as a replacement for the expression

\frac{1}{n}a^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which can never be taken literally
when
p
divides
n
.
The intuition why such a structure might be useful
for cohomological techniques
is that
in positive characteristic
p
,
taking the derivative of a polynomial,
or a power series,

t^{n}\mapsto nt^{n-1}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

is never a surjective map,
as there is no integral of
t^{p-1}
,
and this leads to a failure of the Poincaré Lemma.
But if divided powers of
t
are available,
then the integral does exist.

For us, this means that the syntactic presentation
of the crystalline topos
will involve two rings and a geometric theory formulation
of a PD structure.
To handle this somewhat complex theory,
we make heavy use of the techniques around theories of presheaf type
developed in Section
4
.

5.2
Background on divided power rings

Let us collect a number of
properties and constructions of divided power rings
that will be relevant.
A
PD structure

or
divided powers structure

on an ideal
I
of a ring
A

(always commutative and unitary)
is a family of functions

\gamma_{n}:I\to I,\quad n\geq 1

such that
x^{n}=n!\gamma_{n}(x)

for every
x\in I
,
compatible with natural operations on the ideal
I

and with each other,
as specified in
Definition
5.3.1
below.
For example,
the zero ideal
(0)
of any ring
carries the trivial PD structure
\gamma_{n}(0)=0
,
while any ideal of a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
A

admits a unique PD structure given by

\gamma_{n}(a)=\frac{a^{n}}{n!}
.
Slightly more interestingly,
the maximal ideal
(p)
of
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}

admits a unique PD structure,
as the prime factor
p
occurs
at most
n-1
times in
n!
,
so
\frac{p^{n}}{n!}\in(p)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}

for all
n\geq 1
.

An ideal equipped with a PD structure is called a
PD ideal

and a ring equipped with a PD ideal
(that is,
a model in
\mathrm{Set}
of the theory
\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD}
,
see Definition
5.3.1
below)
is a
PD ring
.
This also defines homomorphisms of PD rings.
We sometimes write a PD ring
(A,I,\gamma)

as
(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A)
.

Not every ideal
J\subseteq I

contained in the PD ideal of a PD ring
(A,I,\gamma)

is closed under the
\gamma_{n}
.
But we can always close it up in one step,
that is,
the ideal generated by
\gamma_{n}(a)

for
n\geq 1
and
a\in J

is a sub-PD-ideal of
I
,
and it actually suffices to take
\gamma_{n}(a)

for
a
in any generating set of the ideal
J
.
More generally,
for any ideal
J
of
A
,
we let
\overline{J}
be the ideal generated by
J

and
\{\,\gamma_{n}(a)\mid n\geq 1,a\in J\cap I\,\}

and call it the
PD saturation
of the ideal
J

(in the PD ring
(A,I,\gamma)
).
There is an induced PD structure
on the ideal
I/(J\cap I)

of the ring
A/J

if and only if

J
is PD saturated (
J=\overline{J}
)

[
9
,
Tag 07H2
]
,
and this constitutes a bijective correspondence
between PD saturated ideals of
A

and isomorphism classes of
maps of PD rings
(A,I,\gamma)\to(A^{\prime},I^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})

such that both
A\to A^{\prime}
and
I\to I^{\prime}
are surjective.

For a PD ring
(A,I,\gamma)

and an element
a\in A
,
there is a unique PD structure
\gamma_{a}

on the ideal
I_{a}
of
A_{a}=A[a^{-1}]

such that
A\to A_{a}
is a homomorphism of PD rings,
namely
\gamma_{a,n}(\frac{a^{\prime}}{a^{k}})=\frac{\gamma_{n}(a^{\prime})}{a^{nk}}
.
The new PD ring
has the expected universal property:
a homomorphism

(\gamma_{a}\curvearrowright I_{a}\triangleleft A_{a})\to(\gamma^{\prime}\curvearrowright I^{\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime})

is the same as one

(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A)\to(\gamma^{\prime}\curvearrowright I^{\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime})

that makes
a
invertible in
A^{\prime}
.

The
PD envelope
construction

[
9
,
Tag 07H8
]

is a left adjoint functor for the forgetful functor

(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\to(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and more generally,
for a fixed PD ring

(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
,
a left adjoint for the forgetful functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})/(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K,I_{K})/(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Forming the PD envelope
(A^{\prime},I^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
of
(A,I)

(possibly over some
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
)
not only
“enlarges”
the ideal
I
as necessary
but also the ring
A
.
However,
there is an isomorphism

A/I\cong A^{\prime}/I^{\prime}
,
induced by the unit of the adjunction

\eta_{(A,I)}:(A,I)\to(A^{\prime},I^{\prime})
.

The polynomial ring
A[X]

over a PD ring
A=(A,I,\gamma)

inherits a PD structure on the ideal
I[X]

(generated by the elements of
I
),
such that PD maps
A[X]\to A^{\prime}

over
(A,I,\gamma)

correspond to elements of
A^{\prime}
.
The same is true for an arbitrary set of polynomial variables
instead of a single one.
But there is a separate notion of
PD polynomial algebra

[
9
,
Tag 07H4
]
,
denoted
A\langle X\rangle
,
which is freely generated as an
A
-module
by
1
and the divided powers
\gamma_{n}(X)
of
X

(instead of the ordinary powers
X^{n}
).
The PD ideal of
A\langle X\rangle

is generated by the elements of
I
and the
\gamma_{n}(X)
,
and PD maps
A\langle X\rangle\to A^{\prime}
over
(A,I,\gamma)

correspond to elements of the PD ideal
I^{\prime}
of
A^{\prime}
.
Equivalently,

A\langle X\rangle
is the PD envelope of
the ideal
I[X]+(X)
in
A[X]

over
(A,I,\gamma)
.
Again, an arbitrary set of variables works just as well.

5.3
Relevant geometric theories

Here,
we define the various theories and theory extensions
from which the syntactic presentation of the crystalline topos
will be built.
We do this right away,
since it will also be of some use
in the definition of the crystalline topos itself.
In particular,
by giving our formal definition of a PD structure
in the form of an extension of geometric theories,
it can immediately be used to define PD structures on schemes
as well.

Definition 5.3.1
.

(i)

We denote
\mathrm{Ideal}
the extension of the theory
\mathrm{Ring}

(see Definition
3.7.1
)
consisting of a relation symbol
I\subseteq A

with these axioms:

0\in I,\qquad x\in I\land y\in I\;\vdash_{x,y:A}\;x+y\in I,\qquad x\in I\;\vdash_{\lambda,x:A}\;\lambda x\in I\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(ii)

In the theory
\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}
,
we can require the ideal to be a nil ideal
by adding the axiom:

(\mathrm{nil})\vcentcolon=\qquad x\in I\;\vdash_{x:A}\;\bigvee_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(x^{n}=0)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(iii)

We denote
\mathrm{PD}\text{-}\mathrm{Ideal}
the following extension
of the theory
\mathrm{Ring}
.
First introduce a sort
S_{I}
,
a function symbol
\iota:S_{I}\to A

with

\iota(x)=\iota(y)\;\vdash_{x,y:S_{I}}\;x=y

and function symbols

0:S_{I}
,
{+}:S_{I}\times S_{I}\to S_{I}

and
{\cdot}:A\times S_{I}\to S_{I}

together with equational axioms stating that

\iota
is an
A
-module homomorphism.
Then add function symbols
\gamma_{n}:S_{I}\to S_{I}

for all
n\geq 1

and these equational axioms
(see
[
9
,
Tag 07GL
]
):

•

\gamma_{1}(x)=x

•

\gamma_{n}(x+y)=\gamma_{n}(x)+\gamma_{n}(y)+\sum\limits_{i+j=n,\;i,j\geq 1}\iota(\gamma_{i}(x))\gamma_{j}(y)

•

\gamma_{n}(\lambda x)=\lambda^{n}\gamma_{n}(x)

•

\iota(\gamma_{m}(x))\gamma_{n}(x)=\binom{m+n}{m}\gamma_{m+n}(x)

•

\gamma_{m}(\gamma_{n}(x))=\frac{(mn)!}{m!(n!)^{m}}\gamma_{mn}(x)

(iv)

To be able to talk about a PD structure
on an existing ideal,
we define the extension
\mathrm{PD}
of
\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}

to be
\mathrm{PD}\text{-}\mathrm{Ideal}
plus the axiom

\exists x{\,:\,}S_{I}.\;\iota(x)=y\;\dashv\vdash_{y:A}\;y\in I\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Note that
\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD}
and
\mathrm{PD}\text{-}\mathrm{Ideal}

are Morita equivalent extensions of the theory
\mathrm{Ring}
,
since the axioms in
\mathrm{Ideal}
are redundant here,
and the relation symbol
I
is definable by the formula

\exists x{\,:\,}S_{I}.\;\iota(x)=y
.
We prefer to write
\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD}

because we think of the divided powers
as an additional structure on an ideal.
Also note that
\mathrm{PD}
is equivalent to
a localic extension of
\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}
,
as the divided power structure
could alternatively be implemented as relation symbols

\widetilde{\gamma}_{n}\subseteq A\times A

expressing partial functions from
A
to
A
.
The main purpose of the sort
S_{I}
above
is to be able to write the axioms in a more readable equational style.

Definition 5.3.2
.

(i)

Similarly to the extension
{K\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}
of
\mathrm{Ring}

from Definition
3.7.1
,
for a
K
-algebra
R

we denote
{R\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}_{K}
the extension of
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}

adding an
R
-algebra structure
compatible with the given
K
-algebra structure.
If necessary,
we write
{R\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}_{K}(A)
to indicate the sort
A

to which the
R
-algebra structure is added.

(ii)

For a ring
K
and a
K
-algebra
R
,
we denote
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
the theory
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+{R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}

extended by a function symbol
f:A\to B

(where
A
is the
K
-algebra and
B
is the
R
-algebra)
and equational axioms
expressing that
f
is a
K
-algebra homomorphism.
A model can be pictured as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

When we treat
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}

as if it contained
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}
,
we intend to use the kernel of
f
as the ideal,
that is,
x\in I
is defined as
f(x)=0
.

(iii)

We set
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}\vcentcolon=K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}+(\mathrm{surj})
,
where

(\mathrm{surj})\vcentcolon=\qquad\top\;\vdash_{y:B}\;\exists x{\,:\,}A.\;f(x)=y\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(iv)

For a ring
K
and an ideal
I_{K}\subseteq K
,
we denote
\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}
the extension of
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}

consisting of the extension
\mathrm{Ideal}
and the additional axioms

c_{\lambda}\in I
for all
\lambda\in I_{K}
.

(v)

For a PD ring
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
,
we denote
\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

the extension of
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}

consisting of the extension
\mathrm{PD}

and the additional axioms
(for every
\lambda\in I_{K}
)

\iota(x)=c_{\lambda}\;\vdash_{x:S_{I}}\;\iota(\gamma_{n}(x))=c_{\gamma_{K,n}(\lambda)}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

We can write
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

if
I_{K}
vanishes in
R
,
since
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
then proves

f(c_{\lambda})=0
for
\lambda\in I_{K}
.

Similarly to the economical version of the theory
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}

mentioned in Remark
3.7.2
,
we can use a presentation

R=K[X_{i}]/(r_{j})
of
R
as a
K
-algebra
to implement the
R
-algebra structure of
B

in the theory
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
.
That is,

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
is equivalent to
the extension of
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}

where we only add constant symbols
c_{i}:B

and axioms
r_{j}(c_{i})=0
,
using the available
K
-algebra structure
to interpret
r_{j}(c_{i})
as a term of sort
B
.
This will be used in Propositions

5.5.1

and

5.7.3
.

Remark 5.3.3
.

The theories

{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}
,
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}
and

{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

have in common that their categories of
\mathrm{Set}
-models
are equivalent to certain slice categories.

\displaystyle{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

\displaystyle\simeq K/(\mathrm{Ring}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set}))

\displaystyle({K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

\displaystyle\simeq(K,I_{K})/((\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set}))

\displaystyle({K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

\displaystyle\simeq(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})/((\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set}))

We even have

(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\simeq(K\to R)/(\mathrm{Ring}^{\to}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set}))

if we use a theory
\mathrm{Ring}^{\to}
of ring homomorphisms.
We could in fact define these theories
by a general construction
that turns a geometric theory
\mathbb{T}

and a
\mathrm{Set}
-model
M
of
\mathbb{T}

into a theory of
“
M
-algebras”
.
This is the same construction that is used by Blechschmidt
in
[
4
, Definition 3.1]
,
who however applies it
internally in the classifying topos of
\mathbb{T}
,
to the universal model of
\mathbb{T}
instead of a
\mathrm{Set}
-based model.
But for our present goals,
we need to be able to combine the above theories and extensions
in flexible ways,
so that such a formulation does not help us much.

5.4
Definition of the big crystalline topos

Before we can define the crystalline topos,
we need to introduce a number of notions
involving PD structures on schemes.
Recall that a closed embedding
U\to T

defined by a quasi-coherent ideal sheaf

\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{T}

is a
thickening

(in the terminology of
[
9
,
Tag 04EX
]
),
that is,
U\to T
is a homeomorphism,
if and only if every local section of
\mathcal{I}

is locally nilpotent,
which in turn means simply that
the internal ideal
\mathcal{I}
of the internal ring
\mathcal{O}_{T}

in
\Sh(T)

satisfies the axiom
(\mathrm{nil})
.

Definition 5.4.1

(see
[
9
,
Tag 07I1
]
)
.

(i)

A
PD scheme
is a scheme
S

together with a quasi-coherent ideal sheaf

\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{S}

and a PD structure on
\mathcal{I}
,
that is,
a model extension along
\mathrm{PD}

of the
(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal})
-model

(\mathcal{I}\triangleleft\mathcal{O}_{S})

in
\Sh(S)
.
A
morphism
of PD schemes

(S,\mathcal{I},\gamma)\to(S^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})

is a morphism of schemes
f:S\to S^{\prime}

such that
f^{\sharp}:f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}_{S}

is a
(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD})
-model homomorphism.
In particular,
it induces a morphism
between the closed subschemes of
S
and
S^{\prime}

defined by
\mathcal{I}
respectively
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(\mathcal{I})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

(ii)

A
PD thickening

is a PD scheme
(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)

such that the closed embedding
V(\mathcal{I})\to T

is a thickening,
that is,
the
(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD})
-model

(\mathcal{O}_{T},\mathcal{I},\gamma)

in
\Sh(T)

fulfills the axiom
(\mathrm{nil})
.
PD thickenings are sometimes denoted
(U,T,\gamma)
,
where
U=V(\mathcal{I})
.

(iii)

If
(S,\mathcal{I}_{S},\gamma_{S})
is a PD scheme
and
X
is a scheme over
the closed subscheme
S_{0}\vcentcolon=V(\mathcal{I}_{S})
of
S
,
then a PD thickening
over

S

and

X

is a PD thickening
(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)

over
(S,\mathcal{I}_{S},\gamma_{S})

together with a morphism of schemes
V(\mathcal{I})\to X
over
S
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(\mathcal{I})}

A
morphism
of PD thickenings over
S
and
X

is a morphism of PD schemes over
S

such that the induced morphism
V(\mathcal{I})\to V(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})

respects the structure morphisms to
X
.

The PD thickenings over
S
and
X

will be the objects of the crystalline site of
X/S
.
Most important to us is that
if
S
and
X
are affine
and we also require
T
to be affine,
then we can nicely describe the resulting category
by a geometric theory.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

The full subcategory of the category of PD thickenings
where the underlying scheme
T
is affine
is equivalent to

{(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD}+(\mathrm{nil}))\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}^{\mathrm{op}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

For a fixed PD ring
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})

and a
K/I_{K}
-algebra
R
,
the full subcategory of the category of PD thickenings
over
S=\Spec(K)
and
X=\Spec(R)

where the underlying scheme
T
is affine
is equivalent to

{(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil}))\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}^{\mathrm{op}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Proof.

The category

{(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}

is of course dual to
the category of affine schemes
equipped with a quasi-coherent ideal sheaf,
via
(I\triangleleft A)\mapsto(\Spec A,\tilde{I})
.
One can check that the closed embedding

\Spec(A/I)\to\Spec A

is a thickening if and only if every element of
I
is nilpotent.
Similarly,
a PD structure on
I

uniquely determines a PD structure on
\tilde{I}
,
and morphisms respecting one
correspond to morphisms respecting the other

[
2
, p. 3.18]
.

For the second part,
the theory extension
\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

(requiring a PD structure on the kernel of
f:A\to B

extending
\gamma_{K}
)
makes sense because the PD ideal
I_{K}
vanishes in
R
,
so the theory
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}
proves
f(c_{\lambda})=0

for all
\lambda\in I_{K}
.
The rest follows from the first part.
∎

If
(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)
is a PD thickening
and
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
is an open subscheme,
then
(T^{\prime},\mathcal{I}|_{T^{\prime}},\gamma|_{T^{\prime}})

is again a PD thickening
(as restricting to
T^{\prime}
is pulling back along
the geometric morphism

\Sh(T^{\prime})\to\Sh(T)
)
and there is a canonical inclusion morphism

(T^{\prime},\mathcal{I}|_{T^{\prime}},\gamma|_{T^{\prime}})\to(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)
.
This allows us to define a Zariski topology
on the category of PD thickenings over
S
and
X
.

Definition 5.4.3
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{I}_{S},\gamma_{S})
be a PD scheme
and let
X
be a scheme over
S_{0}\vcentcolon=V(\mathcal{I}_{S})
.
The
big crystalline site

\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)

is the category of PD thickenings over
S
and
X
,
endowed with the Zariski topology,
that is,
a sieve on
(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)
is covering
if and only if
it contains all the arrows

(T_{i},\mathcal{I}|_{T_{i}},\gamma|_{T_{i}})\to(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)

for some open cover
T=\bigcup_{i}T_{i}
of
T
.

If we want to consider the topos
\Sh(\mathrm{Cris}(X/S))

of sheaves on the crystalline site,
there remains an issue of size.
There is no way to talk about the collection of presheaves
on a big category such as
\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)

(or generally,
about the collection of functions
between two proper classes)
in ZFC set theory.
If the category in question is essentially small,
then one can circumvent this
by choosing a skeleton.
More generally,
if
J
is a Grothendieck topology
on a category
\mathcal{C}

such that there is a (small) dense set
S\subseteq\mathcal{C}

of objects of
\mathcal{C}
,
then one can write

\Sh(\mathcal{C},J)\vcentcolon=\Sh(S,J|_{S})

(where
J|_{S}
is the greatest topology on
S

such that
S\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}
preserves covers).
This is justified because
if
S^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is another dense set of objects,
then by the Comparison Lemma,
there are canonical equivalences

\Sh(S,J|_{S})\simeq\Sh(S\cup S^{\prime},J|_{S\cup S^{\prime}})\simeq\Sh(S^{\prime},J|_{S^{\prime}})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

However,
the site
\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)
does not admit a dense set of objects.
To define a crystalline topos,
a choice of some class
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)

which does admit a dense subset
is therefore necessary.
We leave this choice
as an explicit parameter to the definition for now,
but we will specialize to a particular class
\mathcal{C}

in Definition
5.7.1
.

To avoid an ambiguity in the topology
on the resulting site,
we require
\mathcal{C}
to be closed under
taking open subschemes
T^{\prime}\subseteq T

(with the induced PD structure on
T^{\prime}
).
While there is always the induced topology
J_{\mathrm{Zar}}|_{\mathcal{C}}
,
for which a sieve on
T\in\mathcal{C}
is covering
if and only if
it generates a covering sieve in
\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)
,
if
\mathcal{C}
does not contain all open subschemes of
T
,
then there is no guarantee that such a sieve
actually contains a
“distinguished”
Zariski cover
by open subschemes.
With this assumption,
the two conditions become equivalent.

Definition 5.4.4
.

Let
S
,
X
be as before and
let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)

be a class of objects
admitting a dense subset
and closed under taking open subschemes.
Then we denote
\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}(X/S)

the full subcategory on these objects
endowed with the Zariski topology as above.
And the topos

(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}\vcentcolon=\Sh(\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}(X/S))

is the
big crystalline topos

of
X
over
(S,\mathcal{I}_{S},\gamma_{S})

(defined using the objects in
\mathcal{C}
).

There is a structure sheaf on
\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}(X/S)

(see
[
9
,
Tag 07IN
]
),

\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}}\;:\;\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}(X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set},\quad(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{T}(T)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

As in the case of the Zariski topos,
this sheaf carries a ring structure.
But here, there is more.
We have a second ring object

\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}}\;:\;\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}(X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set},\quad(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{U}(U)=\mathcal{O}_{V(\mathcal{I})}(V(\mathcal{I}))\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

sometimes denoted
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
Furthermore, there is a surjective ring homomorphism

\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
and its kernel

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}}\;:\;\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}(X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set},\quad(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)\mapsto\mathcal{I}(T)

carries a canonical PD structure.
In summary,
we have a model

(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{J})\;\in\;(\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}((X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

An extension of this model
(along a localic theory extension)
will turn out to be
the universal model of a theory
classified by
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}
,
at least for an appropriate choice of
\mathcal{C}
.

The Zariski topology on
\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)

(or
\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}(X/S)
)
in particular ensures that every object can be covered
by objects
(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)
with
T
affine,
in other words, the affine objects are dense.
Thus we can use the Comparison Lemma
to obtain an equivalent site
consisting of affine objects,
which we have already described as a category in
Lemma
5.4.2
.
The following lemma complements this
with a description of the topology
on the new site.

Lemma 5.4.5
.

Let
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
be a PD ring
and let
R
be a
K/I_{K}
-algebra.
The topology induced via the Comparison Lemma
on
(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil}))\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})^{\mathrm{op}}

regarded as a (dense) full subcategory
of
\mathrm{Cris}(\Spec R/\Spec K)

is the following:
A cosieve on an object

(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A\twoheadrightarrow B)

is covering
if and only if
it contains the canonical arrows to

(\gamma_{a_{i}}\curvearrowright I_{a_{i}}\triangleleft A_{a_{i}}\twoheadrightarrow B_{a_{i}})

for some finite family of elements
a_{i}\in A

with
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=(1)
.

Proof.

We first note that the model

(\gamma_{a_{i}}\curvearrowright I_{a_{i}}\triangleleft A_{a_{i}}\twoheadrightarrow B_{a_{i}})

is well-defined
— the ring map
A_{a_{i}}\to B_{a_{i}}
is surjective
and has kernel
I_{a_{i}}

since localization is exact,
and
I_{a_{i}}
is still a nil ideal —
and corresponds to the open subscheme
D(a_{i})

of the PD scheme
\Spec A_{a_{i}}
.
A cosieve on

(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A\twoheadrightarrow B)

is covering for the induced topology
if it generates a covering sieve in
\mathrm{Cris}(\Spec R/\Spec K)
.
But such a sieve contains
(the morphisms of
PD thickenings over
\Spec K
and
\Spec R

induced by)
an open cover of
\Spec A

if and only if it contains a cover by standard opens
D(a_{i})
,
which in turn
cover
\Spec A
if and only if
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=(1)
.
∎

5.5
Preliminary presheaf type results

We saw in Lemma
5.4.2

that the crystalline site of affine schemes

S=\Spec K
,
X=\Spec R

is closely tied to the theory

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Our strategy for proving a classification result
on the crystalline topos
is to show,
under appropriate assumptions,
that this theory is of presheaf type,
then choose the class of objects of the crystalline site
in such a way that the affine objects
turn out to be exactly the compact models of the theory,
and finally add the quotient extension
(\mathrm{loc})

to produce the Zariski topology.

Right now,
we want to approach the presheaf type part
by looking at somewhat simpler theories.
While
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
is of presheaf type
simply because it is an algebraic theory,
the situation already becomes more interesting
for
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}
,
as the axiom
(\mathrm{surj})
is not cartesian.
We also include the extension
\mathrm{PD}

but assume the PD structure on
K
to be trivial
and forget about
(\mathrm{nil})
for now.

The following two propositions
can also serve as an illustration
of the different ways in which
one might try to decompose the same theory into parts
to show that it is of presheaf type,
and how this can lead to results of different strengths.

Proposition 5.5.1
.

Let
R
be a finitely presented
K
-algebra.
Then the theory

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}

is of presheaf type.

Proof.

The cartesian theory
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD}

is Morita-equivalent to
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}K\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}

by introducing a sort
B
for the quotient ring
A/I
,
similarly to Example
2.4.2
.
So the latter theory is of presheaf type
and we are only missing the
R
-algebra structure
on the
K
-algebra
B
.
Since
R\cong K[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]/(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})

is a finitely presented
K
-algebra,
the extension
{R\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}_{K}(B)
is equivalent to
finitely many constant symbols
c_{i}\,{:}\,B
,

i=1,\dots,n
,
and finitely many axioms in the empty context

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;r_{j}(\vec{c})=0
,

j=1,\dots,m
.
Thus we are done by
Proposition
4.4.5

and Corollary
4.4.2
.
∎

We can reduce the assumption on
R

to finite type instead of finite presentation
if we handle the axiom
(\mathrm{surj})

in a different way.
For this, we need a lemma.

Lemma 5.5.2
.

Let
M=(\gamma\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}\triangleleft A\xrightarrow{f}B)\in\mathcal{C}={(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}+\mathrm{PD})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}

and
b\in B
.
Then there is a universal triple
(M^{\prime},a^{\prime},g)
,
where
M^{\prime}=(\gamma^{\prime}\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}B^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
,

a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}

and
g=(g_{A},g_{B}):M\to M^{\prime}
,
such that
f^{\prime}(a^{\prime})=g_{B}(b)
;
that is,
the following diagram in
[\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Set}]

is a pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(M^{\prime},-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(M,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket A\rrbracket_{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket B\rrbracket_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket f\rrbracket_{-}}

Furthermore,
g_{B}:B\to B^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Consider the
A
-algebra homomorphism

\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{A}\vcentcolon=A[X]\to B

with
\widetilde{f}(X)=b

and set
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\vcentcolon=\ker\widetilde{f}
.
Let
\gamma^{\prime}\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime}

be the PD envelope (over
\gamma
)
of the
(\mathfrak{a}\triangleleft A)
-algebra

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\triangleleft\widetilde{A}
.
In particular, we have

\widetilde{A}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\cong A^{\prime}/\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}

(as
A
-algebras)
via the unit

\eta_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\triangleleft\widetilde{A}}:(\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\triangleleft\widetilde{A})\to(\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime})

of the adjunction,
so that
\widetilde{f}
induces
an
A
-algebra homomorphism
f^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to B

with
\ker f^{\prime}=\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}
.
We have thus defined a model
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}

and an arrow
g:M\to M^{\prime}
,
where
g_{A}:A\to A^{\prime}
is just the homomorphism of PD-rings
from the PD envelope construction
and
g_{B}=\mathrm{id}_{B}
.
Setting
a^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\eta_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}\triangleleft\widetilde{A}}(X)

satisfies
f^{\prime}(a^{\prime})=b
.

To check the universal property,
let
h:M\to M^{\prime\prime}=(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime\prime}}B^{\prime\prime})

be given.
An element
a^{\prime\prime}\in A^{\prime\prime}=\llbracket A\rrbracket_{M^{\prime\prime}}

with
f^{\prime\prime}(a^{\prime\prime})=h_{B}(b)

is the same thing as
an
A
-algebra homomorphism
\widetilde{A}\to A^{\prime\prime}

fitting in the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}

But then it is automatic that

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is sent to
\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime}
,
so there is a unique homomorphism of PD-rings

(\gamma^{\prime}\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}^{\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime})\to(\gamma^{\prime\prime}\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}^{\prime\prime}\triangleleft A^{\prime\prime})

over
A

sending
a^{\prime}
to
a^{\prime\prime}
,
and it makes the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}

commute,
as required for a model homomorphism
M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 5.5.3
.

Let
R
be a finitely generated
K
-algebra.
Then the theory

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}

is of presheaf type.

Proof.

The theory
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}+\mathrm{PD}

(with a PD structure on the kernel of the
K
-algebra homomorphism

A\to B
)
is of presheaf type because it is cartesian.
We show that the missing axiom
(\mathrm{surj})

is a rigid-topology quotient.

Let a compact model

M=(\gamma\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}\triangleleft A\xrightarrow{f}B)

of
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}+\mathrm{PD}

and an element
b\in B
be given.
Consider the model homomorphism
g:M\to M^{\prime}

from Lemma
5.5.2
.
We first note that
M^{\prime}
is also compact,
since
\Hom(M^{\prime},-)
is a finite limit of functors
preserving filtered colimits.
(For
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{-}
and
\llbracket B\rrbracket_{-}

preserving filtered colimits,
see Lemma
4.2.2
.)
Secondly,
a map
h:M\to M^{\prime\prime}
factors through
M^{\prime}

(not necessarily uniquely)
if and only if

h_{B}
maps
b
to something in the image of
f^{\prime\prime}
.
This means that the cosieve on
M
generated by
g

(in the category
{(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}+\mathrm{PD})\operatorname{-mod}(\mathrm{Set})}_{c}
)
is exactly the cosieve
S_{b}

as in Theorem
4.3.1

and thus
M
is
J_{(\mathrm{surj})}
-covered by
M^{\prime}
.
And lastly,
the subset
f^{\prime}(A^{\prime})\subseteq B^{\prime}=B

contains of course both
f(A)
and the element
b
.

Now,
since
M
is compact,

B
is a finitely presented
R
-algebra.
This follows from
Lemma
4.2.3

by observing that the right adjoint of the forgetful functor
in the following diagram preserves filtered colimits.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}+\mathrm{PD})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma\curvearrowright\mathfrak{a}\triangleleft A\to B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((0)\triangleleft B^{\prime}\xrightarrow{=}B^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}

\vdash

Thus,
B
is also a finitely generated
K
-algebra by assumption.
But if
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in B
are
K
-algebra generators of
B
,
then we can successively apply Lemma
5.5.2

to them
to obtain a covering arrow
M\to M_{n}

(using the transitivity of the topology
J_{(\mathrm{surj})}
)
such that
b_{i}\in f_{n}(A_{n})
for all
i
,
implying that
f_{n}
is surjective.
∎

Remark 5.5.4
.

Propositions
5.5.1

and
5.5.3

become wrong if we drop the finiteness assumption entirely.
For example, take
K=\mathbb{Q}
,
R=\mathbb{C}
.
The extension
\mathrm{PD}
is an equivalence extension
of
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}\mathbb{C}\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}

because the
\gamma_{n}
are definable by

\gamma_{n}(x)=y\;\dashv\vdash_{x,y\,{:}\,S_{I}}\;\frac{1}{n!}\iota(x)^{n}=\iota(y)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

using the available
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra structure.
And
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}\mathbb{C}\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}
has
too few compact
\mathrm{Set}
-based models
to be of presheaf type,
similarly to the situation in
Example
4.5.2
.

Namely,
we can apply
Lemma
4.2.3

to the pair of adjoint functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}\mathbb{C}\text{-}\mathrm{Quot})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\twoheadrightarrow B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}

\vdash

to see that
if
A\twoheadrightarrow B
is a compact
model of
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}\mathbb{C}\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}
,
then
A
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra.
Then the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
B

is also a finitely generated
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra,
which implies
B=0

for cardinality reasons.
But there are certainly non-compact models with
B\neq 0

(for example, let
A
be freely generated by the elements of
B
),
so the sequent

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;1_{B}=0_{B}

can not be provable in
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}\mathbb{C}\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}
,
meaning that the compact
\mathrm{Set}
-based models
are not jointly conservative
and therefore
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}\mathbb{C}\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}
is not of presheaf type
(see
[
5
, Theorem 6.1.1]
).

We can now prove a
“lazy version”

of our classification result,
where we simply assume the crystalline site
to contain exactly the desired objects.
More precisely,
we even have to assume that a suitable class of objects

\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathrm{Cris}(X/S)

exists,
which is not trivial
given the additional assumption that

\mathcal{C}
is closed under taking open subschemes.
The axiom
(\mathrm{nil})
is made redundant here
by an additional assumption on
K
.

Corollary 5.5.5
.

Let
K
be a ring of nonzero characteristic,
regard
K
as a PD ring with the trivial PD ideal
(0)
,
and let
R
be a finitely generated
K
-algebra.
Assume there is a class

\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathrm{Cris}(\Spec R/\Spec K)

admitting a dense subset
and closed under open subschemes,
such that an affine object belongs to
\mathcal{C}

if and only if the corresponding model of

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}+(\mathrm{nil})

is compact.
Then the topos
(\Spec R/\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}

classifies the theory

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}+(\mathrm{nil})+(\mathrm{loc})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Proof.

If
n=0
in
K
for some
n\geq 1
,
then for any PD ring
(A,I,\gamma)
over
K

and any
a\in I
,
we have
a^{n}=n!\gamma_{n}(a)=0
.
This calculation can be carried out
within the theory
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}
,
so that the axiom
(\mathrm{nil})
is redundant.
Thus by Proposition

5.5.3
,

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}+(\mathrm{nil})
is of presheaf type.
By our assumption on
\mathcal{C}
,
the underlying category of
the site of affine objects
for
(\Spec R/\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}

is

{(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}+(\mathrm{nil}))\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}}^{\mathrm{op}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

the site for the presheaf topos classifying this theory.
The only difference is the Zariski topology
as described in Lemma
5.4.5
.
By Theorem
4.3.1
,
this is precisely the topology corresponding to
the quotient extension
(\mathrm{loc})
,
as each arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A\twoheadrightarrow B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{a_{i}}\curvearrowright I_{a_{i}}\triangleleft A_{a_{i}}\twoheadrightarrow B_{a_{i}})}

is the universal arrow
that sends
a_{i}\in A
to something invertible.
∎

Remark 5.5.6
.

When adding
(\mathrm{loc})
to a theory like
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
,
we mean to impose the axioms on the
K
-algebra
A
,
not on the
R
-algebra
B
.
However,
in the presence of
(\mathrm{surj})
and
(\mathrm{nil})
,
the two options are in fact syntactically equivalent.
This is because
an elementary calculation using these axioms
shows

\inv(f(x))\;\dashv\vdash_{x\,{:}\,A}\;\inv(x)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

5.6
Finiteness conditions for PD schemes

The set of objects that we include in the crystalline site
will be determined by the compactness condition
on models of the appropriate geometric theory.

Lemma 5.6.1
.

Let
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
be a PD ring.
The compact models of
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

are those PD rings over
K
which are of the form

K\langle X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\rangle[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}]/\overline{(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Proof.

While the theory
\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{Ideal}+\mathrm{PD}

as defined in Definition
5.3.1

is cartesian,
the equivalent theory
\mathrm{Ring}+\mathrm{PD}\text{-}\mathrm{Ideal}

is even a Horn theory.
We can similarly construct a Horn theory
equivalent to
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

by adding constants
\widetilde{c}_{\lambda}:S_{I}

in addition to
c_{\lambda}:A

for every
\lambda\in I_{K}
,
with
\iota(\widetilde{c}_{\lambda})=c_{\lambda}
.

By Lemma
4.2.4
,
the compact models of a Horn theory
are those presented by some Horn formula in context.
In our case,
the model presented by the formula
\top

in the context
\vec{x}:{S_{I}}^{m},\vec{y}:A^{n}

is
K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]
.
And since our theory has no relation symbols
and the inclusion
\iota:S_{I}\to A
is required to be injective,
any atomic formula in this context
is provably equivalent to
an equality of two terms of sort
A
,
and therefore also to one of the form
r=0
,
where
r
is a term representing an element of

K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]
.
Thus we are done,
as the universal arrow out of
K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]

that kills
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}

is
K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]\to K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(\vec{r})}
.
∎

We now want to globalize this property of PD rings
over
(K,I,\gamma)

to be able to apply it to morphisms of PD schemes.
This parallels the treatment of
morphisms of finite type and morphisms of finite presentation
as for example in
[
9
,
Tag 01T0
]
and
[
9
,
Tag 01TO
]
.

Definition 5.6.2
.

We say a homomorphism of PD rings
is
of finite PD type

if it is of the form

(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})\to K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\rho

for finite sets of variables

\vec{X}=X_{1},\dots,X_{n}

and
\vec{Y}=Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
,
where
\rho=\overline{\rho}

is any PD saturated ideal of

K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]
.
We say it is
of finite PD presentation

if it is of the form

(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})\to K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(\vec{r})}

for finitely many elements

r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\in K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]
.

Lemma 5.6.3
.

A homomorphism of PD rings
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})\to(A,I,\gamma)

is of finite PD type
if and only if
there are elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in I

and
y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\in A

such that
A
is generated as a
K
-algebra
by
\gamma_{k}(x_{i})
and
y_{i}
,
and the ideal
I
is generated by
the image of
I_{K}
and
\gamma_{k}(x_{i})
.

Proof.

The choice of elements
x_{i}\in I
and
y_{j}\in A

corresponds to a homomorphism
f:K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]\to A
.
The kernel of
f
is always a PD saturated ideal.
This
f
is surjective if and only if

A
is generated as a
K
-algebra by
\gamma_{k}(x_{i})
and
y_{j}
.
And in this case,
the induced map between
the PD ideals of
K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]
and
A

is surjective
if and only if
I
is generated by
the image of
I_{K}
and the
\gamma_{k}(x_{i})
.
∎

Lemma 5.6.4
.

Homomorphisms of finite PD presentation
have the following properties.

(i)

If
R\to A\to A^{\prime}
are homomorphisms of PD rings
with
R\to A
and
R\to A^{\prime}

of finite PD presentation,
then
A\to A^{\prime}
is also of finite PD presentation.

(ii)

If
\rho
is a PD saturated ideal of a PD ring
R

and
R\to R/\rho
is of finite PD presentation,
then there are finitely many elements
r_{i}\in R

such that
\rho=\overline{(\vec{r})}
.

Proof.

(i)

For any PD ring
R
and any element
r\in R
,
we have

R[X]/\overline{(X-r)}=R[X]/(X-r)\cong R
,
but we also have

R\langle X\rangle/\overline{(X-r)}\cong R

as long as
r
lies in the PD ideal of
R
.
Now,
let
A=R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(\vec{r})}
,

A^{\prime}=R\langle\vec{X}^{\prime}\rangle[\vec{Y}^{\prime}]/\overline{(\vec{r}^{\prime})}
,

f:A\to A^{\prime}
,
and choose
x_{i}
in the PD ideal of

R\langle\vec{X}^{\prime}\rangle[\vec{Y}^{\prime}]

and
y_{i}\in R\langle\vec{X}^{\prime}\rangle[\vec{Y}^{\prime}]

such that
f(X_{i})=x_{i}
and
f(Y_{i})=y_{i}
in
A^{\prime}
.
Then we can calculate:

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle=A^{\prime}\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(X_{i}-x_{i},Y_{i}-y_{i})}

\displaystyle=A^{\prime}\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(X_{i}-x_{i},Y_{i}-y_{i},\vec{r})}

\displaystyle=A\langle\vec{X}^{\prime}\rangle[\vec{Y}^{\prime}]/\overline{(X_{i}-x_{i},Y_{i}-y_{i},\vec{r}^{\prime})}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

(ii)

Let
R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(\vec{r})}\cong R/\rho
.
By choosing elements in
I_{R}
respectively
R

corresponding to the
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
,
this isomorphism is induced by a PD map

f:R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]\to R
.
But then we have
\rho=\overline{(f(r_{i}))}
.
∎

Lemma 5.6.5
.

Homomorphisms of PD rings of finite type
and
homomorphisms of PD rings of finite presentation
are both stable under composition.

Proof.

Let
(K,I,\gamma)
be a PD ring.
We have

\Big(K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(\vec{r})}\Big)\langle\vec{X}^{\prime}\rangle[\vec{Y}^{\prime}]/\overline{(\vec{r}^{\prime})}=K\langle\vec{X},\vec{X}^{\prime}\rangle[\vec{Y},\vec{Y}^{\prime}]/\overline{(\vec{r},\vec{r}^{\prime\prime})}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where
r^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is a lift of
r^{\prime}_{i}

to
K\langle\vec{X},\vec{X}^{\prime}\rangle[\vec{Y},\vec{Y}^{\prime}]
.
Here,

\vec{r}
and
\vec{r}^{\prime}

can either be finite lists
or arbitrary set-indexed families.
∎

Lemma 5.6.6
.

Homomorphisms of PD rings of finite type
and
homomorphisms of PD rings of finite presentation
are both stable under base change
(pushout in the category of PD rings).

Proof.

The pushout of
K\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(\vec{r})}

along
f:(K,I,\gamma)\to(K^{\prime},I^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})

is
K^{\prime}\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\overline{(f(\vec{r}))}
.
∎

Lemma 5.6.7
.

Being of finite PD type
and being of finite PD presentation
are
local
properties
of homomorphisms of PD rings,
in the sense of
[
9
,
Tag 01SR
]
,
that is:
Let
R=(R,I_{R},\gamma_{R})
and
A=(A,I,\gamma)

be PD rings.

(a)

If
R\to A

is of finite PD type (presentation)
and
r\in R
,
then
R_{r}\to A_{r}
,
with the induced PD structures,
is of finite PD type (presentation).

(b)

If
r\in R
,
a\in A

and
R_{r}\to A
is of finite PD type (presentation),
then
R\to A_{a}
is of finite PD type (presentation).

(c)

If
R\to A
is a homomorphism of PD rings,

a_{i}\in A
are elements such that
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=(1)
,
and
R\to A_{a_{i}}
is of finite PD type (presentation)
for every
i
,
then
R\to A
is of finite PD type (presentation).

Proof.

(a)

This is a special case of
Lemma
5.6.6
.

(b)

This follows from
Lemma
5.6.5

and the fact that
R_{r}\cong R[X]/\overline{(rX-1)}
,
and the same for
A
.

(c)

Let the
R\to A_{a_{i}}
be of finite PD type.
For every
i
,
choose elements
x_{i,j}a_{i}^{-m_{i,j}}\in I_{a_{i}}

and
y_{i,j}a_{i}^{-n_{i,j}}\in A_{a_{i}}

(with
x_{i,j}\in I
and
y_{i,j}\in A
)
according to Lemma
5.6.3
.
Then
A_{a_{i}}
is generated as an
R
-algebra
by
\gamma_{k}(x_{i,j}a_{i}^{-m_{i,j}})=\gamma_{k}(x_{i,j})a_{i}^{-km_{i,j}}

and
y_{i,j}a_{i}^{-n_{i,j}}
.
Let
\sum_{i}c_{i}a_{i}=1
in
A

and set
\widetilde{A}\vcentcolon=R[a_{i},c_{i},\gamma_{k}(x_{i,j}),y_{i,j}]\subseteq A
.
Then we see that for every element
a\in A

and every
i
,
we have
a_{i}^{N}a\in\widetilde{A}

for
N
big enough,
which means that we also have

a=(\sum_{i}c_{i}a_{i})^{N}a\in\widetilde{A}

for
N
big enough,
so
\widetilde{A}=A
.
We also have
(by Lemma
5.6.3
)
that
I_{a_{i}}
is generated as an ideal
by the elements of
I_{R}
and the

\gamma_{k}(x_{i,j})a_{i}^{-km_{i,j}}
.
But then if
\widetilde{I}\subseteq A
is the ideal
generated by
I_{R}
and all
\gamma(x_{i,j})
,
we have
\widetilde{I}_{a_{i}}=I_{a_{i}}

for all
i
,
which implies
\widetilde{I}=I
.
Thus,
R\to A
is of finite PD type
by the other direction of Lemma
5.6.3
.

Now let the
R\to A_{a_{i}}
be of finite PD presentation.
We already know

A=R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\rho

for a PD saturated ideal
\rho
.
For every
i
,
choose a representative

\widetilde{a}_{i}\in R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]

of
a_{i}\in A
.
We note that
\rho_{\widetilde{a}_{i}}
is also saturated,
and therefore

A_{a_{i}}=R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]_{\widetilde{a}_{i}}/\rho_{\widetilde{a}_{i}}
.
Since both
A_{a_{i}}

and
R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]_{\widetilde{a}_{i}}

are of finite PD presentation over
R
,
Lemma
5.6.4
tells us that

\rho_{\widetilde{a}_{i}}=\overline{(r_{i,j}a_{i}^{-n_{i,j}})}=\overline{(r_{i,j})}
,
with finitely many
r_{i,j}\in\rho
.
Let
c_{i}\in R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]

be elements with
1-\sum_{i}c_{i}a_{i}\in\rho
.
Then,
setting
\widetilde{\rho}\vcentcolon=\overline{(1-\sum_{i}c_{i}a_{i},r_{i,j})}\subseteq R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]
,
we have
\widetilde{\rho}\subseteq\rho
,

(\widetilde{a}_{i})=(1)

in
R\langle\vec{X}\rangle[\vec{Y}]/\widetilde{\rho}

and
\widetilde{\rho}_{\widetilde{a}_{i}}=\rho_{\widetilde{a}_{i}}

for all
i
.
This implies
\widetilde{\rho}=\rho
.
∎

Definition 5.6.8
.

A morphism of PD schemes

(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)\to(S,\mathcal{I}_{S},\gamma_{S})

is
locally of finite PD type (presentation)

if there are affine open coverings

T=\bigcup_{i}\Spec A_{i}

and
S=\bigcup_{i}\Spec K_{j}

such that every
\Spec A_{i}

maps to some
\Spec K_{j_{i}}

where
K_{j_{i}}\to A_{i}

(with the induced structures of PD rings)
is of finite PD type (presentation).

The following good properties
of morphisms locally of finite PD type
and morphisms locally of finite PD presentation
now follow from Lemmas

5.6.5
,

5.6.6

and
5.6.7

as in
[
9
,
Tag 01SQ
]
:
If
T\to S
is locally of finite PD type (presentation),
then for any affine open
\Spec A\subseteq T

mapping into an affine open
\Spec K\subseteq S
,
the homomorphism of PD rings
K\to A

is of finite PD type (presentation).
In particular,
the morphisms locally of finite PD type (presentation)
between affine PD schemes
correspond to the morphisms
of finite PD type (presentation)
between PD rings.
And morphisms locally of finite PD type (presentation)
are stable under composition and base change.

Definition 5.6.9
.

We say that the PD ideal
I

of a PD ring
(A,I,\gamma)

is
PD-generated
by a subset
G\subseteq I

if
\overline{(G)}=I
,
that is,
if it is generated (as an ideal)
by the elements
\gamma_{n}(g)

for
g\in G
and
n\geq 1
.
We say
I
is
finitely PD-generated

if it is PD-generated by a finite set
G
.

We also globalize the notion of a PD ideal
being finitely PD-generated.
Note that by Lemma
5.6.4
,
if
(A,I,\gamma)
is a PD ring
and we equip
A/I
with the trivial PD structure,
then
I
is finitely PD-generated
if and only if

A\to A/I
is of finite PD presentation.

Definition 5.6.10
.

The PD ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}

of a PD scheme
(S,\mathcal{I},\gamma)

is
locally finitely PD-generated

if for every affine open
U\subseteq S
,
the PD ideal
\mathcal{I}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{S}(U)

is finitely PD-generated,
that is,
if the closed embedding
V(\mathcal{I})\to S

is locally of finite PD presentation,
where
V(\mathcal{I})
is regarded as a PD scheme
with trivial PD structure.

5.7
Syntactic presentation of the big crystalline topos

We can now finish the definition
of the precise variant of the crystalline topos
for which we will give a syntactic presentation.
The objects we want to allow in the site
are those PD thickenings
(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)

over
S
and
X

where
T\to S
is locally of finite PD presentation.
An essentially small dense subcategory is for example
the one where
T
is additionally required
to be an open subscheme of an affine scheme
which is mapped into an affine open of
S
.
We also impose a finiteness assumption on
X

without which the connection to compact models
motivating this definition
would be lost.

Definition 5.7.1
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{I}_{S},\gamma_{S})
be a PD scheme
and let
X
be a scheme
locally of finite presentation over
S_{0}=V(\mathcal{I}_{S})
.
Then we set

(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}\vcentcolon=(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathcal{C}}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where
\mathcal{C}
is
the class of PD thickenings
(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)

over
S
and
X

for which
T\to S
is locally of finite PD presentation.

Now there is not much missing for the proof of
the classification result.
The following lemma will be used to show that
the axiom
(\mathrm{nil})
is a rigid-topology quotient.

Lemma 5.7.2
.

Let
(A,\mathfrak{a},\gamma)
be a PD ring.
If
\mathfrak{a}
is PD-generated by nilpotent elements,
then
\mathfrak{a}
is a nil ideal.

Proof.

We need to show that
\mathfrak{a}
is generated
(as an ordinary ideal)
by nilpotent elements.
So if
a\in\mathfrak{a}
is nilpotent,
say
a^{e}=0
,
we need to show that
\gamma_{n}(a)
is still nilpotent
for every
n\geq 1
.
It is most convenient to do the necessary calculation
in
\mathbb{Q}[X]
first
(with the unique PD structure
\gamma
on any ideal):

(\gamma_{n}(X))^{k}=\frac{1}{(n!)^{k}}X^{kn}=\frac{(kn-e)!}{(n!)^{k}}X^{e}\gamma_{kn-e}(X)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

One can check that
c\vcentcolon=\frac{(kn-e)!}{(n!)^{k}}

is an integer for
k
big enough
(
k\geq\lceil\frac{e}{n}\rceil(n+1)
suffices).
But then we have the equation

(\gamma_{n}(X))^{k}=cX^{e}\gamma_{kn-e}(X)

also in the sub-PD-ring

\mathbb{Z}\langle X\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{Q}[X]
,
and then,
via the unique PD morphism
\mathbb{Z}\langle X\rangle\to A

sending
X
to
a
,
we obtain
(\gamma_{n}(a))^{k}=0
for sufficiently large
k
.
∎

Proposition 5.7.3
.

Let
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
be a PD ring
with
I_{K}
finitely PD-generated
and let
R
be a finitely presented
K/I_{K}
-algebra.
Then the theory

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil})

is of presheaf type
and its compact models are those

(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A\twoheadrightarrow B)

where
(A,I,\gamma)
is of finite PD presentation
over
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
.

Proof.

We start with the cartesian theory

{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}
.
By Lemma
5.6.1
,
the compact models are the PD rings
of finite PD presentation over
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
.
It is an equivalence extension
to add a sort
B
with
K
-algebra structure
for the quotient
A/I
,
arriving at
K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}(K/I_{K})\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}
.
Since
R
is a finitely presented
K/I_{K}
-algebra,
finitely many constant symbols
and finitely many axioms in the empty context
suffice to add an
R
-algebra structure to
B
,
so by Proposition
4.4.5

and Corollary
4.4.2
,

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

is of presheaf type
and a model

(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A\twoheadrightarrow B)

is still compact if and only if

(A,I,\gamma)
is of finite PD presentation over
K
.
All that remains is to show that
(\mathrm{nil})

is a rigid-topology quotient of this theory.

Let a compact model
M=(\gamma\curvearrowright I\triangleleft A\twoheadrightarrow B)\in(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}})\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}

be given.
Since
I_{K}
is finitely PD-generated
and
A
is of finite PD presentation over
K
,
the PD ideal
I
is finitely PD-generated.
Let
a\in I
be one of the PD generators
in a chosen finite family.
The
J_{(\mathrm{nil})}
-covering cosieve
S_{a}

of Theorem
4.3.1

is the cosieve of all arrows
M\to M^{\prime}

that send
a
to an nilpotent element;
it is generated by the countable family
of the arrows

M\to M^{n}=(\gamma\curvearrowright I/\overline{(a^{n})}\triangleleft A/\overline{(a^{n})}\twoheadrightarrow B)
.
In each of the (still compact) models
M^{n}
,
the PD ideal
I/\overline{(a^{n})}

is PD generated by the images of the PD generators of
I
,
so we can apply this construction to the other PD generators in turn
to cover
M
by models where
the PD ideal is generated by nilpotent elements
and where thus,
by Lemma
5.7.2
,
the axiom
(\mathrm{nil})
is fulfilled.
∎

Theorem 5.7.4
.

Let
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
be a PD ring
with
I_{K}
finitely PD-generated
and let
R
be a finitely presented
K/I_{K}
-algebra.
Then the big crystalline topos

(\Spec R/\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

classifies the geometric theory

K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil})+(\mathrm{loc})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The universal model is
the short exact sequence
associated to the structure sheaf

\mathcal{J}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

with the canonical
K
-algebra structure on
\mathcal{O}

and
R
-algebra structure on
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Set

\mathbb{T}_{0}\vcentcolon=K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

The underlying category of
the site of affine objects for
(\Spec R/\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is dual to the subcategory of
\mathbb{T}_{0}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})

where
(A,I,\gamma)
is of finite PD presentation over
K
.
By Proposition
5.7.3
,
this is exactly
\mathbb{T}_{0}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c}
,
and the associated presheaf topos

[\mathbb{T}_{0}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c},\mathrm{Set}]

classifies
\mathbb{T}_{0}
.
The Zariski topology on this site,
that is,
the restriction of the one in
Lemma
5.4.5

to our compact models,
coincides with the topology induced by
(\mathrm{nil})

via Theorem
4.3.1

(just as in Corollary
5.5.5
).

For the universal model,
we note that the structure sheaf model

\mathcal{J}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
,
when restricted to the site of affine objects,
becomes simply the tautological model
in
[\mathbb{T}_{0}\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})_{c},\mathrm{Set}]
,
which is the universal model of
\mathbb{T}_{0}
.
In particular,
the presheaves of which the universal
\mathbb{T}_{0}
-model consists
are sheaves for the topology induced by
(\mathrm{loc})
,
implying that the same model,
regarded as a model in the subtopos,
is also the universal model of
\mathbb{T}_{0}+(\mathrm{loc})
.
∎

Corollary 5.7.5
.

Let
K
,
R
be as in
Theorem
5.7.4
.
Then the category of points of the big crystalline topos

(\Spec R/\Spec K)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is equivalent to

\Bigl(K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil})+(\mathrm{loc})\Bigr)\text{-}\allowbreak\mathrm{mod}(\mathrm{Set})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

the category of affine PD thickenings
(A,I,\gamma)

over
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})

(implying that
I
is a nil ideal)
together with an
R
-algebra structure on
A/I
,
such that
A
is a local ring.

Proof.

The points of a topos are by definition
the geometric morphisms from
\mathrm{Set}
to it.
So this follows immediately from
Theorem
5.7.4

and the definition of classifying topos.
∎

Remark 5.7.6
.

If the ring
R
has non-zero characteristic,
then there is a different way of dealing with the axiom
(\mathrm{nil})
.
Namely,
one can see that if the PD ideal
I
of
A

contains a non-zero integer,
then
(\mathrm{nil})
is actually equivalent to

\top\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bigvee_{0\neq n\in\mathbb{Z}}(c_{n}=0)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which is an axiom in the empty context.
So in Theorem
5.7.4
,
instead of assuming that
I_{K}
is finitely PD-generated,
we can alternatively assume
R
to have non-zero characteristic.

5.8
The non-affine case

Now we apply the results of Section
3

to the big crystalline topos
to obtain a syntactic presentation of
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

in the case where
S
and
X
are not affine.
This will be an adaptation of
the treatment of the Zariski topos
in Section
3

to the crystalline topos.
and we will mostly be concerned with the aspects which differ
in these two situations
and how to handle them.

Since we only know a theory classified by
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

in the case where both
S
and
X
are affine,
we will want to cover both
S
and
X

by affine opens
S_{i}
respectively
X_{i}

such that for each
i
,

X_{i}
lies over
S_{i}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}

This is always possible,
as we can first choose an affine cover of
S

by some
S_{i}

and then cover the
X\times_{S}S_{i}
by affine opens.
Also,
an open subscheme
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is canonically a PD scheme
as
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{I}_{S}|_{S^{\prime}},\gamma_{S}|_{S^{\prime}})
,
and we note that if
X^{\prime}\to S
factors through
S^{\prime}
,
then it automatically also factors through

S^{\prime}_{0}=V(\mathcal{I}_{S}|_{S^{\prime}})
.

When we turn to defining open subtoposes
corresponding to such pairs of open subschemes
(S^{\prime},X^{\prime})
,
there are two things to note.
Firstly,
it is no longer the case that
(S^{\prime},X^{\prime})

is an object of the site
{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X/S)
,
since
X^{\prime}
is not at all required to be isomorphic to
S^{\prime}_{0}
,
and the PD scheme
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{I}_{S}|_{S^{\prime}},\gamma_{S}|_{S^{\prime}})
itself
does not have to be a PD thickening.
But we can still consider the class of all objects

(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)\in{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X/S)

such that
T\to S
factors through
S^{\prime}

and
V(\mathcal{I})\to X
factors through
X^{\prime}
,
that is,
the subterminal presheaf

U_{(S^{\prime},X^{\prime})}\vcentcolon=\Hom_{X/S}(-,(S^{\prime},X^{\prime}))\;:\;{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Set}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and it is easy to see that this is in fact a sheaf
for the Zariski topology on
{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X/S)
.

Secondly, however,
this subterminal object
U_{(S^{\prime},X^{\prime})}

depends in fact only on
X^{\prime}
and not on
S^{\prime}
,
namely,
we have
U_{(S^{\prime},X^{\prime})}=U_{(S,X^{\prime})}
.
This is because the closed embedding

V(\mathcal{I})\hookrightarrow T

is a homeomorphism,
and whether or not
T\to S
factors through
the open subscheme
S^{\prime}

can be tested on the level of points.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(\mathcal{I})}

Thus, we only have induced open subtoposes

U_{X^{\prime}}\vcentcolon=U_{(S,X^{\prime})}

for all open subschemes
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
.

This leads to the slightly subtle situation
that a cover of
X
by affine open
X_{i}

with the property that every
X_{i}\to S

factors through some affine open
S_{i}\subseteq S

induces an open cover of
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

by subtoposes for which we know classified theories,
but if
S_{i}=\Spec K_{i}
and
S^{\prime}_{i}=\Spec K^{\prime}_{i}

are two different choices for the same
X_{i}=\Spec R_{i}
,
then we have no direct algebraic relation between
K_{i}
and
K^{\prime}_{i}
,
so we have no way to construct a diagonal extension
between the classified theories.
Therefore it seems more reasonable
to work with pairs
(S_{i},X_{i})
of open subschemes from the beginning
and only drop the condition that the
S_{i}
cover
S
.

Here is the analogue of
Lemma
3.7.3

that we need.

Lemma 5.8.1
.

(i)

The mapping

X^{\prime}\mapsto((X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{X^{\prime}})}

from open subschemes of
X
to open subtoposes of
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Zar}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

is monotone
and preserves finite intersections
and arbitrary unions.

(ii)

For open subschemes
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and
X^{\prime}\subseteq X

with
X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
,
the open subtopos
((X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{X^{\prime}})}

is equivalent to
(X^{\prime}/S^{\prime})_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
.

(iii)

If
S=\Spec K
and
X=\Spec R
are affine,
with
I_{K}
finitely PD-generated
and
R
finitely presented over
K/I_{K}
,
so that
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
classifies

\mathbb{T}_{K,R}\;\vcentcolon=\;K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}R\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}+(\mathrm{nil})+(\mathrm{loc})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

and
h\in R
,
then the open subtopos
((X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{D(h)})}

is presented by the closed geometric formula

\inv(c_{h})\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

which is also equivalent to

\inv(c_{g}\,{:}\,A)\land\inv(c_{h}\,{:}\,B)

for any
g\in K
such that
g\mid h
in
R
.

Proof.

(i)

Monotonicity and finite intersections are clear.
For unions,
let
X^{\prime}_{i}
be a family of open subschemes of
X

and let
F\subseteq 1_{(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}}

be a subterminal sheaf
with
U_{X^{\prime}_{i}}\leq F
for all
i
.
We want to show
U_{X^{\prime}}\leq F
for
X^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\bigcup_{i}X^{\prime}_{i}
.
So let
T=(T,\mathcal{I},\gamma)\in{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X/S)

be given with
V(\mathcal{I})\to X^{\prime}
.
By pulling back the
X^{\prime}_{i}
,
this induced an open cover of
V(\mathcal{I})
,
or equivalently an open cover
T=\bigcup_{i}T_{i}
,
such that
V(\mathcal{I}|_{T_{i}})\to X_{i}
.
This means
\lvert U_{X_{i}}(T_{i})\rvert=1
,
which implies
\lvert F(T_{i})\rvert=1
,
and since
F
is a sheaf for the Zariski topology on
{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X/S)
,
we obtain
\lvert F(T)\rvert=1
.

(ii)

As explained above,

U_{X^{\prime}}
selects exactly those objects of
{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X/S)

which belong to
{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}(X^{\prime}/S^{\prime})
.
The rest is exactly as in the case of the Zariski topos.

(iii)

We know that
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
classifies
\mathbb{T}_{K,R}

and

((X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}})_{o(U_{D(h)})}\simeq(D(h)/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}

classifies
\mathbb{T}_{K,R_{h}}
,
which is equivalent to
\mathbb{T}_{K,R}+\inv(c_{h})
.
It follows that
\inv(c_{h})
presents this open subtopos,
since one structure sheaf
pulls back to the other,
including the extra structure that makes up the universal models.
For
g\in K
with
g\mid h
in
R
,
the theory
\mathbb{T}_{K,R}
shows

\inv(c_{h})\;\vdash_{[]}\;\inv(f(c_{g}))

and also,
by Remark
5.5.6
,

\inv(f(c_{g}))\;\dashv\vdash_{[]}\;\inv(c_{g})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

∎

As the final prerequisite,
we show that intersections of pairs of open subschemes
can be covered by appropriate pairs of standard opens.

Lemma 5.8.2
.

Let
X\to S
be a morphism of schemes,
let
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq S

and let
X_{1},X_{2}\subseteq X

be affine open subschemes,

S_{i}=\Spec K_{i}
,

X_{i}=\Spec R_{i}
,
such that
X_{i}\to S_{i}
.
Then there are families of elements

g_{i}^{j}\in K_{i}
,
h_{i}^{j}\in R_{i}
,
j\in J
,
i\in\{1,2\}
,
such that
for every
j\in J

we have
g_{i}^{j}\mid h_{i}^{j}
in
R_{i}

and

\Spec K_{1}\supseteq D(g_{1}^{j})=D(g_{2}^{j})\subseteq\Spec K_{2}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

\Spec R_{1}\supseteq D(h_{1}^{j})=D(h_{2}^{j})\subseteq\Spec R_{2}

as open subschemes of
S
respectively
X
,
and

X_{1}\cap X_{2}=\bigcup_{j\in J}D(g_{1}^{j})=\bigcup_{j\in J}D(g_{2}^{j})\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Proof.

As mentioned before,
we can cover
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
by open subschemes
simultaneously standard open in
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.
The same is true for
X_{1}\cap X_{2}
,
and since the simultaneously standard opens
even form a basis of opens of
X_{1}\cap X_{2}
,
we can also arrange that
D(h_{1}^{j})
maps to
D(g_{1}^{j})
,
and, equivalently,
D(h_{2}^{j})
maps to
D(g_{2}^{j})
.
This means that
for each
i
and
j
,
we have the dashed arrow in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R_{i})_{h_{i}^{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K_{i})_{g_{i}^{j}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

so
g_{i}^{j}
is invertible in
(R_{i})_{h_{i}^{j}}
,
so
g_{i}^{j}
divides some power of
h_{i}^{j}
,
and replacing
h_{i}^{j}
with this power,
we obtain
g_{i}^{j}\mid h_{i}^{j}
in
R_{i}
.
∎

Recall the notation
{R\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}_{K}(B)

for the extension adding
to a sort
B

an
R
-algebra structure
compatible with a previously given
K
-algebra structure,
from Definition
5.3.2
.
For a PD ring
(K,I_{K},\gamma_{K})
,
we also denote

\gamma/\gamma_{K}

the axioms
distinguishing the extension
\mathrm{PD}_{\gamma_{K}}

of
{K\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}}+\mathrm{Ideal}_{I_{K}}

from
\mathrm{PD}
.

Theorem 5.8.3
.

Let
(S,\mathcal{I}_{S},\gamma_{S})
be a PD scheme
with
\mathcal{I}_{S}
locally finitely PD-generated,
and let
X
be a scheme locally of finite presentation
over
S_{0}=V(\mathcal{I}_{S})
.
Let

\Spec K_{i}=S_{i}\subseteq S,\qquad\Spec R_{i}=X_{i}\subseteq X

be open subschemes
such that
X_{i}\to S_{i}

and
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
.
For every
i\neq i^{\prime}\in I
,
let

g_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}\in K_{i},\quad g_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}\in K_{i^{\prime}},\quad h_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}\in R_{i},\quad h_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}\in R_{i^{\prime}},

j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}
,
be families of elements as in
Lemma
5.8.2
,
with corresponding ring isomorphisms

\varphi_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}=(\varphi_{i^{\prime},i}^{j})^{-1}:(K_{i})_{g_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}\to(K_{i^{\prime}})_{g_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}},\qquad\widetilde{\varphi}_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}=(\widetilde{\varphi}_{i^{\prime},i}^{j})^{-1}:(R_{i})_{h_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}\to(R_{i^{\prime}})_{h_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}

Then
(X/S)_{\mathrm{Cris}_{\mathrm{fp}}}
classifies the geometric theory

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{X/S}\;\vcentcolon=\;{}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\text{-}\mathrm{Quot}+\mathrm{PD}+(\mathrm{nil})+(\mathrm{loc})+\langle p_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}+(\bigvee_{i\in I}p_{i})

\displaystyle+\Bigl(\Bigl({K_{i}\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}(A)+{R_{i}\text{-}\mathrm{AlgStr}}_{K_{i}}(B)+\gamma/\gamma_{K_{i}}\Bigr)/p_{i}\Bigr)_{i\in I}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{h_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}\land\inv(x)\;\vdash_{x\,{:}\,B}\;p_{i^{\prime}}\Bigr)_{i\neq i^{\prime}\in I,j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(p_{i}\land p_{i^{\prime}}\;\vdash_{[]}\;\bigvee_{j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}\exists x{\,:\,}B.\;(x\in\widetilde{c}_{h_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}}\land\inv(x))\Bigr)_{i\neq i^{\prime}\in I,j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}}}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{g_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}}\land\inv(x)\land y\in\widetilde{c}_{\lambda}\land z\in\widetilde{c}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\;\vdash_{x,y,z\,{:}\,A}\;x^{n}y=z\Bigr)_{i\neq i^{\prime}\in I,j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}},\lambda\in K_{i}}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(x\in\widetilde{c}_{h_{i^{\prime},i}^{j}}\land\inv(x)\land y\in\widetilde{c}_{\mu}\land z\in\widetilde{c}_{\mu^{\prime}}\;\vdash_{x,y,z\,{:}\,B}\;x^{n}y=z\Bigr)_{i\neq i^{\prime}\in I,j\in J_{\{i,i^{\prime}\}},\mu\in R_{i}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}

where in the last two families of axioms,

\lambda^{\prime}\in K_{i^{\prime}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

are chosen for each
\lambda\in K_{i}

such that

\varphi_{i,i^{\prime}}^{j}(\lambda)=(g_{i^{\prime},i}^{j})^{-n}\lambda^{\prime}
,
and

\mu^{\prime}\in R_{i^{\prime}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}

are chosen for each
\mu\in R_{i}

such that
